## 📚 Paper List

- **[Training-Free Target-Aware Refinement of 3D Gaussian Language Map](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21619)**  `arXiv:2610.21619`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Sibaek Lee, Kyeongsu Kang, Seongbo Ha, Yewon Jeon, Joonyeol Choi, Daegeol Ko, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distilling vision-language features into 3D Gaussian Splatting yields a language map that can be queried with arbitrary text. In deployment, however, a small set of user-specified targets must be reliably located, which current distilled maps often miss, while open-vocabulary queryability for other words must remain intact. We propose a training-free, target-aware refinement of an already-distilled language map. Guided by a text-promptable segmenter, the visual embedding inside each detected target region is blended with the target text embedding, a convex combination whose coefficient sets the amount of injected prior, while non-target regions are left untouched. Injecting too much overwrites appearance cues and general queryability, so we seek, for each target, the smallest coefficient that still lets it win, and we obtain every coefficient in closed form, leaving no hyperparameters to tune. We further identify a failure mode of injection in language-goal localization. Text embeddings share a common component, and injecting them makes the standard cosine read-out query-agnostic, which a centering step in the read-out removes. Across three benchmarks, the refinement consistently improves open-vocabulary segmentation, roughly doubling the base mIoU on Replica, and localizes language goals more reliably than the base map. It also transfers as a plug-in to off-the-shelf field-based and object-level language maps and supports a real-world demonstration in which a legged robot navigates to natural-language goals. Project page and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[LoopWAM: Closed-Loop Generalist Policy Improvement from Deployment Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18873)**  `arXiv:2610.18873`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Wenxuan Guo, Yingping Liang, Zhaoguo Wang, Ziyuan Li, Angyuan Ma, Zhenyu Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling expert demonstrations alone does not enable generalist robot policies to improve from deployment. Real-world rollouts contain successes, suboptimal behavior, and failures: imitating every recorded action can reinforce poor behavior, while filtering out failures discards evidence of physical interactions and their consequences. We introduce LoopWAM, a closed-loop world-action model that learns directly from this full quality spectrum, using rollouts from both itself and other deployed policies. Source-dependent noise sampling unifies behavior learning and action-conditioned world modeling, while quality steering and quality-weighted supervision retain full video supervision across qualities and regulate action imitation. Video and action tokens interact throughout parallel denoising, allowing learned consequences to inform action generation directly. On RoboChallenge Table30-V2, three deployment-and-training rounds raise success from 23.67% to 43.33%, a 19.7-percentage-point gain, with a final score of 56.80. LoopWAM also achieves 94.5% average success on RoboTwin 2.0 and 49.5% overall success on RoboCasa365 without additional embodied pretraining, obtaining the best overall results among the compared methods across all three benchmarks. On challenging shirt folding task, two rounds of deployment-driven improvement after single-task post-training raise success from 13.20% to 55.56%. These results demonstrate closed-loop improvement from mixed-quality experience under both multi-task learning and single-task post-training. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ProTracer: Proprioception-Guided Failure Diagnosis in Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21369)**  `arXiv:2609.21369`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Chang Dong, Mehdi Hosseinzadeh, King Hang Wong, Lingqiao Liu, Francois Fraysse, Feras Dayoub, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents a comprehensive framework for robot manipulation failure analysis that includes binary failure detection, failure categorization, explanation generation, and the additional capability of failure onset localization, which aims to identify the earliest moment at which a robot execution deviates from a valid task-completion trajectory and is ultimately followed by task failure. To address these tasks, we propose ProTracer, a training-free framework that leverages existing Vision-Language Models (VLMs) together with proprioceptive signals for failure analysis. Our method uses proprioceptive dynamics to identify temporally informative action boundaries and converts richer robot-state signals into structured natural-language descriptions that can be jointly analyzed together with visual observations by the VLM. This design combines the temporal precision of proprioceptive signals with the multimodal reasoning capabilities of modern VLMs without requiring additional model training. We further introduce FailTime, a benchmark with synchronized visual and proprioceptive observations for evaluating conventional failure diagnosis tasks as well as failure onset localization. Experiments demonstrate that ProTracer achieves strong performance across both conventional failure diagnosis tasks and the newly introduced failure onset localization task, highlighting the importance of proprioceptive reasoning for fine-grained temporal failure analysis.
  </details>

- **[RoboAtlas: Contextual Active SLAM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26046)**  `arXiv:2606.26046`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Alexander Schperberg, Shivam K. Panda, Abraham P. Vinod, Bhagawan Rokaha, M. K. Jawed, Stefano Di Cairano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present RoboAtlas, a contextual Active SLAM framework that adaptively balances geometric exploration and semantic reasoning using a scalable 3D semantic mapping system, OpenRoboVox. RoboAtlas integrates frontier exploration, global semantic-map reasoning, and egocentric VLM-based reasoning through a contextual multi-armed bandit that transitions from exploration to semantically guided navigation as scene understanding improves. We evaluate the system in simulation and on a Unitree Go2 robot in large-scale real-world environments exceeding 1800 m2 with approx. 30k mapped semantic instances, achieving a 100% task success rate. On the GOAT-Bench "Val Unseen" benchmark, RoboAtlas achieves state-of-the-art performance with highest reported success rate (SR) of 90.6%, using GPT-4o, improving over the strongest prior baseline by 17.8 percentage points in SR. Using the much smaller Qwen2.5-VL-7B model, it still achieves 88.8% SR, outperforming all baselines using GPT-4o in SR, and revealing the importance of the information gained by our semantic mapping framework over simply replacing the underlying foundation model. The results demonstrate that grounding foundation models with large-scale 3D semantic maps enables robust and efficient contextual Active SLAM.
  </details>

- **[LIBERO-PeRM: Benchmarking Personalized Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22372)**  `arXiv:2610.22372`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhixu Li, Keqian Tang, Litian Gong, Jingyu Yao, Wenqian Zhang, Tianze Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalization is essential for general-purpose robots to transition into household environments and achieve true practicality. While general robotic manipulation has been extensively studied, personalized robotic manipulation (PeRM) lacks a controlled benchmark for separating task completion from preference satisfaction. We introduce LIBERO-PeRM, a benchmark for preference learning in embodied agents. LIBERO-PeRM studies five questions: 1) how policies follow preferences in zero-shot settings; 2) how they learn single preferences from demonstrations; 3) how efficiently they acquire preferences as demonstrations increase; 4) how learned preferences generalize across layouts and tasks; and 5) how policies compose preferences and behave under conflicts. We propose a controlled formulation and taxonomy of observable preferences in simulation, covering motion, skill, and user-profile choices. This formulation is restricted to language-expressible preferences that can be instantiated in LIBERO without changing the task goal, evaluated automatically, and reused across tasks. Building on LIBERO, our procedural pipeline generates paired demonstrations with and without preferences. We create five task suites (312 tasks) with high-quality demonstrations, rich metadata, and preference-satisfaction metrics. Experiments show that current VLA policies struggle with zero-shot personalization, while SFT makes preferences learnable. Mixed training achieves better no-preference retention than sequential adaptation and improves performance with contextual prompts compared with preference-only training, but can weaken motion-preference learning; preference generalization, decomposition, and conflict handling remain difficult. The code, datasets, and demos can be found on the project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Embodiment Causal Transfer from Human Videos to Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21798)**  `arXiv:2610.21798`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Xingyu Zhang, Yingchao Li, Peizheng Guo, Jie Hu, Changwen Zheng, Wenwen Qiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling robot manipulation models requires large amounts of interaction data, yet collecting diverse real-robot experience remains costly. Human manipulation videos, in contrast, provide abundant and diverse task experience that could potentially benefit robot learning. However, differences in morphology, action spaces, and control mechanisms make human motions unsuitable as direct supervision for robot actions, raising a scientific question: what knowledge in human videos should be transferred across embodiments to robot manipulation? To address this question, we analyze subtask transfer through the lens of causal beams, focusing on which contextual conditions and interaction dependencies should be retained to support local task effects. Based on this perspective, we propose Causal-Beam-Guided Subtask Transfer (CBST). CBST learns manipulation-related representations from human videos and grounds them in task-level semantic variables using robot demonstrations. Within a predefined manipulation model, causal-beam constraints guide contextual information selection by requiring retained assignments to sustain local mechanism outputs while preserving nontrivial responsiveness to retained inputs. The selected semantic information conditions a world-action model, allowing the robot to generate actions from its own observations without requiring human-robot motion correspondence. Experiments on robot manipulation benchmarks show improvements in task success over the corresponding world-action model baselines.
  </details>

- **[SOMA: Stack-of-Objects Manipulation using a Quadruped Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20968)**  `arXiv:2610.20968`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Stergios E. Bachoumas, Panagiotis Artemiadis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Executing heavy-duty physical manipulation in unpredictable environments remains a major challenge for quadrupedal robots lacking dedicated manipulators. This paper presents SOMA, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework for whole-body non-prehensile manipulation of multi-object stacks using a quadruped robot. Unlike traditional methods restricted to two-dimensional planar pushing, SOMA integrates explicit robot base-height control, unlocking full three-dimensional pushing strategies. This capability enables the quadruped to manipulate complex payload stacks as a unified system while preventing relative sliding or tipping of upper layers. The architecture factorizes control into a high-level manipulation policy using a Simplified Structured State Space Sequence (S5) encoder to reason over long-horizon temporal history and predict bounded target tracking commands via Beta distributions, and a low-level locomotion policy that dynamically adapts leg stance configurations. In extensive simulation benchmarks, SOMA achieves a $90.3\%$ success rate for single-object reposing and up to $90.1\%$ for dynamic two-box stacks under stringent error margins ($0.05\text{ m}$ position error, $1^\circ$ orientation error). Finally, we validate zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on a Unitree Go1 quadruped robot across diverse payload masses and geometries, demonstrating active base-height adjustments to maintain traction and balance during heavy pushing tasks.
  </details>

- **[DexMimic: A Generalist Dexterous Hand-Object Tracking Policy via Value-Guided Reset Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19089)**  `arXiv:2610.19089`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhexi Luo, Zhixuan Xu, Rui Yang, Zeyu Xiao, Yiwen Hou, Lin Shao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Achieving human-like dexterous manipulation remains a central challenge in robotics. Learning from human hand-object interaction (HOI) datasets offers a promising pathway to diverse dexterous skills, yet existing physics-based approaches train specialist policies for individual trajectories, thereby limiting skill reuse at scale. We introduce DexMimic, a generalist dexterous hand-object tracking policy jointly trained across diverse HOI trajectories. Efficient joint training requires allocating each rollout to both an undertrained phase and a starting state that supports continued tracking. DexMimic realizes this insight through Value-Guided Reset Allocation, which adapts the initialization distribution online to prioritize difficult phases and select reset states with higher expected tracking returns. We further introduce a benchmark spanning training, fine-tuning, and generalization. Experiments show improved tracking success and training efficiency, faster fine-tuning, and the potential for zero-shot generalization to unseen trajectories and objects; larger training sets further improve efficiency and generalization. Finally, we validate DexMimic on a real-world dexterous manipulation platform.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Multimodal LLMs as Generalist Vision-Language-Action Agents for Drone Control: Commanding, Approaching, Tracking and Searching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01404)**  `arXiv:2609.01404`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jaewoo Park, Minyoung Lee, Sukmin Seo, Moonbin Yim, Hyunwook Yoon, Dohoon Ryu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used to make decisions for drones, but existing evaluations are mostly limited to simulation, to a single drone, and to targets that are visible from the start and do not move. We present DroneCATS, a benchmark in which the MLLM alone controls the drone through four actions, and evaluate ten MLLMs from frontier models to small open models. The benchmark varies target visibility, target motion, scene and simulator, and extends to real drones. Beyond a single drone, it adds a multi-drone suite, where one model drives multiple drones, and a commander + sub-agents suite, where a commander model directs one sub-agent for each of the drones. Across these settings, we find that frontier models achieve the highest success rates, but their latency causes them to lose moving targets, while small open models often do not declare arrival even when they reach the target. Guided by this failure analysis, we fine-tune a 2B model on drone action data synthesized from public RGB-D images; its success rate rises from 0% to 31% in simulation and outperforms the frontier models in real flight. With multiple drones, we find that every zero-shot model succeeds more often as the commander of one sub-agent per drone than when it drives all of them from one context, and that commanding success follows general VLM ability. On real drones, we observe that the results broadly follow those in simulation, while latency and hardware limits matter more. We will fully open-source the benchmark.
  </details>

- **[VOiLA: Vectorized Online Planning with Learned Diffusion Models for POMDP Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19729)**  `arXiv:2606.19729`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Marcus Hoerger, Rishikesh Joshi, Rahul Shome, Ian Manchester, Hanna Kurniawati_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Planning under uncertainty is an essential capability for autonomous robots. The Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP) provides a powerful framework for such a capability. Although POMDP-based planning has advanced significantly, its application to real-world problems is often limited by the difficulty of obtaining faithful POMDP models. We present Vectorized Online planning wIth Learned diffusion model for POMDP Agents (VOiLA), a framework that learns task-agnostic POMDP world models for online planning under uncertainty. Specifically, VOiLA learns transition and observation samplers using conditional diffusion models and observation-likelihood models for particle-based belief updates. To enable efficient online planning, the diffusion samplers are distilled into compact feedforward generators and integrated with Vectorized Online POMDP Planner (VOPP), an online POMDP planner designed to leverage GPU parallelization. Experimental results indicate the distillation strategy reduces sampling cost by up to nearly three orders of magnitude, making learned generative POMDP models practical for online planning. Evaluation of VOiLA on three benchmark problems indicates that VOiLA achieves equal or better performance than current reinforcement learning (RL) methods for POMDPs, such as Recurrent Soft Actor Critic and DreamerV3, while using less than 10% of the training data, and generalizes much better to unseen environment configurations. In a physical robot experiment, VOiLA accomplishes the task in 10 of 10 runs while using models learned purely from simulated data for online planning.
  </details>

- **[RMBench: Memory-Dependent Robotic Manipulation Benchmark with Insights into Policy Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01229)**  `arXiv:2603.01229`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Tianxing Chen, Yuran Wang, Mingleyang Li, Yan Qin, Hao Shi, Zixuan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic manipulation policies have made rapid progress in recent years, yet most existing approaches give limited consideration to memory capabilities. Consequently, they struggle to solve tasks that require reasoning over historical observations and maintaining task-relevant information over time, which are common requirements in real-world manipulation scenarios. Although several memory-aware policies have been proposed, systematic evaluation of memory-dependent manipulation remains underexplored, and the relationship between architectural design choices and memory performance is still not well understood. To address this gap, we introduce RMBench, a simulation benchmark comprising 9 manipulation tasks that span multiple levels of memory complexity, enabling systematic evaluation of policy memory capabilities. We further propose Mem-0, a modular manipulation policy with explicit memory components designed to support controlled ablation studies. Through extensive simulation and real-world experiments, we identify memory-related limitations in existing policies and provide empirical insights into how architectural design choices influence memory performance. The website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Spark-0: Scaling Spatially Aligned Tactile Pretraining for Robot Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22342)**  `arXiv:2610.22342`  `cs.RO`  
  _Tianxing Chen, Wenwei Lin, Chuqiao Lyu, Peicheng Xiang, Junyuan Tang, Tian Nian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalist robot policies benefit from large-scale visual pretraining but often struggle with contact-rich dexterous manipulation, where force, compliance, and incipient slip are hard to observe from images. We present Spark-0, a spatially aligned vision-tactile-language-action (VTLA) model that uses touch in two complementary ways. During pretraining, image-space contact maps derived from tactile gloves, together with features from a frozen 3D geometry teacher, supervise the visual tokens; these objectives are removed at inference. In parallel, a tactile pathway encodes regional force histories and 3D taxel states to condition the action expert. A single checkpoint therefore supports both vision-only and tactile-conditioned deployment. To scale this supervision, we build HumanTouch, a 1,000-hour dataset of full-hand tactile human manipulation, and pretrain Spark-0 on it together with egocentric video, robot data, and simulation. We also release SparkArena, which pairs vision-only simulated tasks with tactile-conditioned real-robot tasks on one dexterous embodiment. Spark-0 achieves the highest mean success rate on both SparkArena tracks and ranks first on all eight aggregate metrics of the Ego Humanoid Manipulation Benchmark. Compared with vision-only pretraining on the same data and compute with touch discarded, retaining the touch of 1,000 hours of HumanTouch raises vision-only success on Ego Humanoid from 52.5% to 71.7%, and success on SparkArena-Real, where touch is also used at deployment, from 44.0% to 52.0%, indicating that touch serves not only as a policy input but also as scalable supervision for dexterous visuomotor pretraining. The Website is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sim-to-Real Reinforcement Learning for Path-Safe Target Reaching in Robotic Harvesting of Hydroponic Lettuce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22337)**  `arXiv:2610.22337`  `cs.RO`  
  _Al Bashir, Partho Ghose, Yibin Wang, Robert G. Hardin, Joshua Peeples, Genhua Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic harvesting requires precise manipulation to ensure accurate positioning of the end-effector, especially in a constraint workspace. This study introduces the Path-Safe Approach Proximal Policy Optimization (PSA-PPO), a target-conditioned local reaching framework designed for robotic harvesting of nutrient film technique (NFT)-hydroponic lettuce in greenhouses. The proposed PSA-PPO preserves the standard clipped PPO objective while introducing a harvesting-oriented policy formulation that integrates target-conditioned observations, corridor-aware spatial constraints, trajectory-history-based safety encoding, and task-specific success criteria for constrained robotic manipulation. PSAPPO was trained entirely in Isaac Lab and successfully transferred to a physical UR10e robotic arm without any real-world policy fine-tuning. In a controlled physical benchmark against 150 scheduled targets, PSA-PPO achieved the highest reaching rate of 94.7%, outperforming Cartesian+TOTG (86.0%), RRTConnect (88.0%), and RRT* (80.0%), while reducing median RMS jerk by 39.8 to 73.0% relative to the classical planners during the final approach segment. A formulation ablation evaluated over 200 simulation targets demonstrates that path-safety and side-corridor shaping are the principal drivers of harvesting-compatible performance: removing either term substantially degrades strict success even when terminal endpoint accuracy is preserved or improved, confirming that optimization toward the endpoint alone is an insufficient objective for constrained crop-proximal manipulation. Finally, PSA-PPO was integrated with an RGB-D vision module and an end-effector and evaluated as a complete greenhouse harvesting system across 144 lettuce harvesting attempts, achieving 94.4% vision-guided target reaching and 86.1% complete harvesting success.
  </details>

- **[Mem4Act: Learning Effective Latent Memory for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22323)**  `arXiv:2610.22323`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yun Zhang, Zhiyu Huang, Tianhui Cai, Chen Tang, Wei Wang, Jiaqi Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon robotic manipulation fundamentally requires memory: agents must retain task-relevant information from past observations and interactions and use it to guide future actions. Existing approaches typically preserve raw historical observations, compressed visual tokens, or language summaries, but these representations either expose the action policy to increasingly long histories or risk discarding task-relevant temporal, semantic, and perceptual information. We introduce Mem4Act, a framework for learning effective latent memory as a fixed-size representation explicitly optimized for long-horizon robotic control. Mem4Act addresses three coupled challenges: how to efficiently cover long histories, what information memory should retain, and how to ensure the policy actually relies on memory. To this end, it organizes history via multi-granularity temporal aggregation, preserves complementary semantic and perceptual information through question-answering supervision and key-frame DINO feature reconstruction, and employs memory-first two-stage training to encourage greater use of memory by the action policy. The resulting memory captures both high-level task state and fine-grained perceptual evidence while providing a bounded interface to the action policy. Across RoboMME, RMBench, and four real-world manipulation tasks, Mem4Act achieves state-of-the-art performance on memory-dependent control, with success rates of 49.88%, 61.0%, and 81.5%, respectively. Extensive ablations further demonstrate the importance of temporal aggregation, complementary memory supervision, and memory-first training.
  </details>

- **[VP-EVT: Active Visibility Preservation for Embodied Visual Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21999)**  `arXiv:2610.21999`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yanci Wen, Tianle Zeng, Jianwei Peng, Weixi Situ, Hanjing Ye, Hong Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied Visual Tracking (EVT) is an essential capability for intelligent robots in human-robot collaboration, requiring an agent to follow a designated target person in dynamic environments relying solely on egocentric visual perception. However, persistent pedestrian occlusions in dynamic scenes pose critical challenges to existing EVT methods. Lacking proactive visibility preservation, reactive tracking methods frequently experience prolonged target loss under severe occlusions. Therefore, we propose VP-EVT, a framework that treats target visibility as an explicit objective of motion control rather than a by-product of following. Acting on this objective requires understanding both what obstructs the target and how robot motion can change that obstruction. VP-EVT therefore uses language-guided perception to extract separate target and pedestrian observations. When occlusion makes current observations incomplete, VP-EVT combines visual history with ego-motion to maintain estimates of their relative positions and motion. Using these estimates and the current following waypoints, VP-EVT predicts whether continued following will sustain the obstruction. When the predicted risk is low, the original following motion is retained; otherwise, a recurrent controller combines updated estimates with maneuver context from preceding decisions to coordinate viewpoint adjustment and the return to stable following. Moreover, we introduce OccEVT-Bench for closed-loop evaluation of persistent occlusion and collect expert demonstrations for training. On its Risk split, VP-EVT improves success rate by 121.4% relative to a competitive baseline and reduces visibility deficit and longest target-loss duration by 78.8% and 74.4%, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory-based Diffusion from One-shot Human Video for Generalizable Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21792)**  `arXiv:2610.21792`  `cs.RO`  
  _Guoping Pan, Xinmiao Yu, Wannan Cao, Jincheng Li, Baolin Jiang, Yizhe Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Imitation learning enables robots to perform complex tasks by leveraging human-collected demonstrations. However, most existing approaches require labor-intensive data collection with additional teleoperation systems, which hinders scalability. Human videos represent a natural source of data that implicitly contain knowledge of manipulation behaviors. However, transferring them to robots remains challenging due to the visual domain gap and the absence of explicit action labels. In this work, we introduce TDV, a trajectory-based framework that leverages data generation to enable generalizable manipulation. TDV focuses on task-relevant objects by leveraging plug-and-play 6D pose estimation techniques to extract object trajectories from human videos, thereby mitigating the impact of irrelevant backgrounds and varying viewpoints. As a modality-agnostic intermediate representation, trajectories are inherently independent of the visual domains and different embodiments, enabling efficient data generation. Our method outperforms previous approaches on RLBench simulation tasks, achieving near 100\% success rates across seven tasks. In real-world experiments, TDV enables cross-embodiment generalization manipulation from one-shot human demonstrations by leveraging efficient trajectory generation techniques.
  </details>

- **[Comfortably Stuck: Evaluation Pitfalls in Bench2Drive's Closed-Loop Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21741)**  `arXiv:2610.21741`  `cs.RO`  
  _Thomas Schwarzfischer, Emad Aghajanzadeh, Moritz Thoma, Nael Fasfous, Manoj Rohit Vemparala, Pierpaolo Mori, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-loop evaluation is widely regarded as a more faithful way to assess end-to-end (E2E) driving models than open-loop evaluation, and Bench2Drive, built on the CARLA simulator, has become the dominant benchmark for it. We show that its evaluation protocol itself contains pitfalls that can misrepresent the models scored on it. Bench2Drive's comfort metric, adapted from nuPlan, has several implementation errors and a segmentation scheme that rewards standing still, so it mostly measures how long a vehicle stays stationary, correlating with stationary time at r = 0.97 across eight models. We propose a corrected, distance-based metric that cuts that correlation to r = 0.06. Under it, the strong driving-score/comfort tradeoff the original metric reports is no longer detectable once models are compared at matched driving speed, which suggests that comfort trades against how fast a model drives rather than against how well it drives. We also uncover two pitfalls in how the harness supplies navigation signals: the default controller can replace a model's own steering with an angle toward the route target point, and the target point supplied at evaluation time can differ substantially from what a model saw in training. We demonstrate both on UniAD and VAD, where their effects differ sharply, and argue that future closed-loop evaluations should check for them. We will release all fixes as standalone code, so corrected comfort scores can be obtained from logged trajectories without re-running closed-loop simulation.
  </details>

- **[SPEAR: Selective Preference Elicitation with Adaptive Rewards for VLM-Guided Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21621)**  `arXiv:2610.21621`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zewen Wu, Ruizhe Liu, Pei Zhou, Feng Chen, Yanchao Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) enable automated preference labeling for robotic manipulation, yet their deployment remains impractical since each query incurs substantial inference cost, while VLMs frequently abstain on visually challenging comparisons. We propose SPEAR, a framework that transforms VLMs into efficient preference oracles via two synergistic mechanisms. First, we design distinguishability-aware query selection to reduce abstention rates by targeting only informative and distinguishable comparisons. Second, we deploy an adaptive reward transition that bootstraps exploration with VLM-synthesized guidance before preference-based refinement. Experiments on the manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that SPEAR achieves 88.9% average success, outperforming baselines by 23.3 percentage points, while significantly reducing abstention and wall-clock training time on contact-rich tasks. SPEAR learns three Mobile ALOHA manipulation tasks fully online on real hardware, demonstrating end-to-end real-world applicability. By addressing query efficiency and exploration challenges, SPEAR establishes VLMs as practical preference oracles for scalable robot learning without manual reward engineering.
  </details>

- **[CQDC-Warp: GPU-Accelerated Differentiable Quasi-Dynamic Simulation for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21349)**  `arXiv:2610.21349`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yanchen Ding, Jiayi Chen, Jianfeng Xu, Wei Zhao, Li Yi, He Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient synthesis of contact-rich manipulation trajectories is essential for downstream robot learning. Gradient-based planning offers a promising approach, but jointly optimizing motion and contact decisions remains challenging. Meanwhile, many existing planners run on CPUs with limited throughput. We present CQDC-Warp, a GPU-accelerated differentiable quasi-dynamic simulator for batched model predictive control (MPC). We use it to study contact-guided trajectory optimization, in which intended robot--object contacts are specified before planning and encouraged through soft costs, while the simulated dynamics determine contact geometry and frictional response without a prescribed contact-mode schedule. Across $17{,}408$ trials on the $17$-object Allegro in-hand rotation benchmark, our framework achieves $79.8\%$ success, compared with $73.7\%$ for CFree and $65.2\%$ for IMPACT, and reduces mean trajectory-generation time by factors of $27.0$ and $50.2$, respectively. On a single NVIDIA RTX 4090 with $2{,}048$ parallel worlds, forward and backward throughput reach $2.06\times10^5$ and $2.25\times10^5$ world-steps per second, respectively. Further experiments cover manipulation with LEAP and Shadow hands, environmental contact exploitation, bimanual manipulation, whole-body control, and real-world trajectory replay on a Sharpa hand.
  </details>

- **[Evo-DexBench: Benchmarking Generalization across Related Tasks for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21180)**  `arXiv:2610.21180`  `cs.RO`  
  _Bing Cheng, Yiran Mao, Zewei Ye, Yifan Jin, Xinyi Chen, Ruifeng Tu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose dexterous manipulation requires policies to transfer knowledge from related tasks to new tasks with only a few target demonstrations. Existing benchmarks often provide too many target demonstrations. They also leave the differences between source and target tasks uncontrolled. As a result, they cannot clearly distinguish knowledge transfer from task memorization. Our key insight is the Core-Gen principle. Core tasks are related tasks that share a functional interaction and provide source knowledge, while a Gen task is a novel target within the same functional family that has only a few demonstrations. This setup tests whether a policy can reuse functional interaction knowledge. Based on this principle, we propose Evo-DexBench, which controls both source-task relatedness and target-task exposure. It comprises 30 tasks in six functional families and a VR teleoperation pipeline. The resulting dataset covers three everyday scenes per task. It contains 1,248 multimodal demonstrations collected with simulated Shadow Hands equipped with 123 taxels. Experiments show that even the best-performing method achieves an average success rate of only 38.5%, highlighting the challenges existing methods face in transferring functional interaction knowledge. Further analysis shows that, across the two tasks, joint Core+Gen training improves performance by 12% and 38%, respectively, over training on Core followed by fine-tuning on Gen. This indicates that joint training makes better use of Core knowledge and that functionally matched Core data yields greater gains in success rate than mismatched data. We release the Evo-DexBench dataset and benchmark to support the evaluation of dexterous manipulation policies' generalization across related tasks.
  </details>

- **[LEAP: Gradient Flow Augmented Lagrangian Trajectory Optimization for Contact Constrained Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20936)**  `arXiv:2610.20936`  `cs.RO`  
  _César E. Ramos Chuquiure, Ryan Tsai, Yutong Zhou, Zihang Huang, Vansh Thakur, Akshunn Trivedi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating dynamically feasible motions for legged and manipulating robots requires solving a large, nonlinear, non-convex optimal control problem (OCP) in which contact wrenches must satisfy friction-cone constraints throughout the motion, not only at isolated instants. Existing solvers are sensitive to the initial guess and scale poorly with the number of joints. This paper introduces LEAP, which transcribes the OCP with pseudospectral collocation and, instead of a Newton or SQP solve, integrates a primal--dual gradient flow of a Powell--Hestenes--Rockafellar augmented Lagrangian to compute a solution. Because the flow is stiff, this paper designs a linearly implicit integrator with a Gauss--Newton metric, momentum, and a merit-based step controller that keeps the augmented Lagrangian monotone. Across locomotion benchmarks on five robot platforms, from planar hoppers to 32-joint humanoids, LEAP is the only method to solve every tested problem, achieves substantial speedups over Crocoddyl and Aligator on several benchmarks, and reaches trackable references at smaller compute budgets. The resulting trajectories are validated by tracking them in a high-fidelity simulator and on hardware. We will make the code public upon final acceptance of this paper.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating VLA Inference with Perturbation Informed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19656)**  `arXiv:2610.19656`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jehun Lee, Hajun Lee, Jimin Ryu, Youhan Lee, Beomseok Kang, Jonghyun Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action-chunking vision-language-action (VLA) models predict sequences of future robot actions. Under fixed-horizon execution, the robot executes a fixedlength prefix of each predicted chunk before invoking the policy again, leaving the remaining predictions unused. For a given number of executed actions, short execution horizons require more policy calls. Extending the execution horizon amortizes policy inference cost over more actions. However, uniformly applying long horizons across episodes can limit opportunities for precise corrections. To address this limitation, we propose PICES (Perturbation-Informed Chunk and Execution Horizon Selection), a training-free method that jointly selects an action chunk and the number of actions executed before the next policy call. Within a single policy call, the action expert generates a batch of action chunks from multiple initial noise samples and their locally perturbed counterparts, using the same conditioning features across all samples. PICES exploits differences between corresponding actions in each nominal chunk and its counterpart to determine candidate-specific execution horizons and select a nominal chunk for execution. Experiments on simulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that PICES achieves speedup by up to 2.3x in wall-clock policy inference time on Jetson Orin and Thor relative to fixed horizon baselines while improving the success rate.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Single-Axis Testing: Paired Evaluation of Compound Robustness in Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15940)**  `arXiv:2609.15940`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hiroki Sawada, Shunichi Kasahara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action policies are typically evaluated one perturbation at a time, providing a useful diagnosis of their sensitivity to individual distribution shifts. Real-world deployment, however, may involve several shifts simultaneously, and it remains unclear how these individual robustness measurements compose. We ask whether compound robustness can be inferred from single-axis evaluations. We introduce LIBERO-CTRL, a six-axis benchmark that pairs each initial state across single-axis conditions and a matched simultaneous condition. This design reveals two opposing outcome changes that aggregate success rates cannot distinguish: emergent failures, where all single-axis rollouts succeed but the simultaneous rollout fails, and compensated successes, where at least one single-axis rollout fails but the simultaneous rollout succeeds. Because one transition decreases compound success while the other increases it, they can cancel, making aggregate compound performance appear consistent with single-axis measurements even when individual outcomes differ substantially. These opposing transitions can largely cancel in aggregate: even when the difference between the two transition rates is not statistically distinguishable from zero, as many as 29.0% of matched initial states still change outcome. Across six policies and three severity levels, such outcome changes reach 34.5% in the most affected condition. The relative prevalence of the two transitions varies across policies and severities, while the transition rates remain similar under independent re-evaluation of stochastic policies. Compound robustness therefore cannot be characterized from aggregate single-axis success rates alone; matched per-instance evaluation is needed to reveal how joint perturbations alter behavior.
  </details>

- **[When Does Corruption Break the Plan? A Degradation-Tolerance Benchmark for Camera-Only End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29005)**  `arXiv:2608.29005`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haohua Que, Handong Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Camera-only end-to-end (E2E) driving models are nearing deployment, where the camera stream is degraded by blur, noise, low light, weather, frame loss, and memory faults. How much a policy tolerates before its driving breaks is unclear. Corruption-robustness benchmarks target detection or bird's-eye-view perception, not the planning output that drives the car. We present DriveDegrade, a benchmark for image-degradation tolerance in camera-only E2E driving. Sixteen corruption families at five severities are injected on the fly inside the image loader, one operator reaching fifteen policies, and we evaluate open-loop planning on nuScenes and NAVSIM plus a CARLA closed-loop anchor. First, mild degradation barely affects planning, and the families that break it have a clear threshold at mid severity. Second, fragility is corruption-dependent: blur, JPEG, and raindrop damage planning most, while weather and bit error are tolerated far into the range. Third, a flat curve is ambiguous, so we separate corruptions that degrade the image from those that remove it. A planner that reads its camera must lose accuracy when information is deleted, whatever it does under quality loss. On these two axes the planners separate sharply, quantifying the ego-status shortcut without mistaking indifference for robustness. A released vision-language-action planner is flat on both axes, and blinding all six of its cameras costs it only 11.5 percent.
  </details>

- **[Discriminative Barrier Functions for Safe Adversarial Imitation Learning from Observation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13938)**  `arXiv:2607.13938`  `cs.RO`  
  _Anubhav Vishwakarma, Bhaumik Mehta, Caleb Hsu, Byron Boots, Tyler Han, Karen Leung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inverse Reinforcement Learning (IRL) algorithms are powerful tools for learning from and generalizing expert demonstrations, but they often rely on unconstrained exploration, rendering them unsafe for real-world deployment. Meanwhile, Control Barrier Functions (CBFs) can guarantee the safety of control systems, but the analytical design of CBFs can be time-consuming and esoteric. In this work, we address these limitations jointly by constraining reward function candidacy during IRL to the space of CBFs, yielding a formulation that exhibits safe online control with continuous experiential improvement. Crucially, this framework enables the data-driven recovery of barrier functions directly from unlabeled expert observations. We demonstrate that the recovered barrier function is robust to unsafe states entirely absent from the expert data. Furthermore, we benchmark our method against standard IRL baselines in a simulated navigation environment, demonstrating improved safety performance. Finally, we investigate the trade-offs of planning-based versus policy-based IRL methods across both simulation and a real world obstacle avoidance task.
  </details>

- **[Robo2Process: Benchmarking and Learning Process-Aware Understanding in Vision-Language Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13040)**  `arXiv:2606.13040`  `cs.RO`  
  _Dayu Xia, Yue Shi, Yao Mu, Huiting Ji, Chaofan Ma, Yingjie Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) used as robotic critics, reward generators, and failure detectors must understand how manipulation unfolds, not only whether it succeeds. Yet fine-grained process supervision is not readily available in a unified form, and existing evaluations do not systematically diagnose these abilities. We introduce Robo2Process, a trace-grounded framework for scalable construction of process-aware supervision from heterogeneous robotic execution data. It aligns visual observations with source-native signals and annotations, extracts local process units (LPUs), and generates structured QA supervision through task-specific sampling, deterministic label construction, and ambiguity filtering. We instantiate this framework as RoboProcessBench, a diagnostic benchmark with 12 question families spanning \emph{static monitoring} and \emph{dynamic reasoning}, and ProcessData, a corpus of 57,892 QA pairs across four data sources and 260 manipulation tasks, split into ProcessData-SFT and ProcessData-Eval. Evaluation of a broad suite of VLMs reveals uneven process understanding. Supervision from ProcessData-SFT improves \textit{Qwen2.5-VL-7B} and \textit{InternVL-3-8B} on local state, motion, progress, and primitive-aware cues. External evaluation on RoboRewardBench shows Robo2Process enables out-of-distribution process-aware reasoning abilities. Overall, Robo2Process provides a source-aware pipeline, diagnostic benchmark, and learnable substrate for process-aware understanding in robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting Embodied Chain-of-Thought for Generalizable Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03784)**  `arXiv:2606.03784`  `cs.RO`  
  _Nan Sun, Yuan Zhang, Yongkun Yang, Wentao Zhao, Peiyan Li, Jun Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied chain-of-thought (CoT) aims to bridge linguistic reasoning with robotic control, yet its effective form and integration remain underexplored. In this paper, we revisit embodied CoT for robotic control at an unprecedented scale. We curate the largest embodied CoT corpus to date, comprising 978,743 trajectories, 226.3M samples, and 2592.5 hours of data. Through extensive experiments, we show that effective CoT must ground high-level semantic understaning in concrete linguistic action guidance -- such as end-effector movement and image-space trajectories -- whereas high-level reasoning alone yields marginal gains. More importantly, we identify that explicit CoT does not scale reliably as an autoregressive action prefix, suffering from compounding errors during inference. To address these challenges, we propose ERVLA, a vision-language-action (VLA) model that effectively leverages linguistic reasoning in generalizable robot manipulation. ERVLA is trained using a CoT-dropout strategy, allowing the model to leverage rich reasoning traces during training while predicting actions directly without CoT during inference to bypass autoregressive instability. This approach enables reliable scaling with increasing pre-training data. ERVLA achieves state-of-the-art results on LIBERO-Plus with an 86.9\% success rate and reaches 53.2\% on VLABench, showcasing superior performance in out-of-distribution settings. Furthermore, ERVLA outperforms competitive state-of-the-art baselines in real-robot experiments, especially in handling semantic ambiguity and long-horizon tasks. Code, data, and model checkpoints are available on the project website:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[MR-LiDAR: A Multi-Resolution Roadside LiDAR Benchmark for Perception Diagnostics and Deployment Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24777)**  `arXiv:2605.24777`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shunlai Cui, Peng Cao, Yuan Zhu, Yongjiang He, Jiacheng Yin, Xiao Huo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LiDAR configuration selection in roadside sensing remains constrained by limited controlled evidence across sensors. To address this gap, we present MR-LiDAR, a controlled cross-configuration benchmark comparing four LiDAR configurations from 16 to 128 beams on dynamic vehicle and vulnerable road user (VRU) targets. Its cross-configuration main set covers matched daytime, nighttime, and vehicle-occlusion conditions, while three deployment and operating condition subsets examine mounting height, target speed, and curved-road geometry. By reducing confounding from scene and trajectory variation, MR-LiDAR links sensor configuration to target point acquisition and algorithmic detection outcomes. We characterize distance-dependent point loss, define effective beam count as the number of vertical beams intersecting the target's angular extent, and introduce a point-based Occlusion Ratio. The results show that 16- and 32-beam configurations degrade earlier with distance, while VRUs exhibit greater point sparsity than motor vehicles. Nominal beam count alone is insufficient to explain roadside scanning capability. In the cross-configuration main set, the 80- and 128-beam configurations achieve comparable results, consistent with their nearly identical downward beam layouts. A uniform 80-beam counterfactual yields only about one third of the effective beam count of the actual non-uniform configuration. Illumination and the tested speed range have little influence on target-point-count patterns, whereas mounting height and near-field road geometry can substantially alter configuration ordering. Finally, an empirical minimal-sufficient configuration matrix combines frame-level geometric sufficiency with matched-box quality and includes threshold sensitivity analysis, providing bounded configuration guidance for the tested conditions.
  </details>

- **[PathPainter: Transferring the Generalization Ability of Image Generation Models to Embodied Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07496)**  `arXiv:2605.07496`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yijin Wang, Yuru Tian, Xijie Huang, Weiqi Gai, Mo Zhu, Xin Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bird's-eye-view (BEV) images have been demonstrated to provide valuable prior information for navigation. Given the global information provided by such views, two key challenges remain: how to fully exploit this information and how to reliably use it during execution. In this paper, we propose a navigation system that uses BEV images as global priors and is designed for ground and near-ground robotic platforms. The system employs an image generation model to interpret human intent from natural language, identify the target destination, and generate traversability masks. During execution, we introduce cross-view localization to align the robot's odometry with the BEV map and mitigate long-term drift in conventional odometry. We conduct extensive benchmark experiments to evaluate the proposed method and further validate its effectiveness on both UAV and quadruped robot platforms. Using only a conventional local motion planner, the quadruped robot successfully completes a 285-meter outdoor long-range navigation task. This work demonstrates how the world-understanding capabilities of foundation models can be transferred to embodied navigation, enabling robots to benefit from the strong generalization ability of existing image generation models.
  </details>

- **[MasDrift: Benchmarking Authorization Preservation Across Multi-Agent Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07556)**  `arXiv:2608.07556`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuoning Xu, Xiucheng Zhang, Hanjun Luo, Yingbin Jin, Yinpeng Dong, Hanan Salam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) decompose long-horizon tasks across supervisors and subagents, but delegated goals do not necessarily carry their original authorization boundaries. Existing safety benchmarks mainly study adversarial compromise, while work on constraint drift lacks controlled architecture-level evaluation. We introduce MasDrift, a benchmark of 600 benign productivity tasks across eight domains. Each task pairs required work with reserved actions. MasDrift compares single-agent, centralized, and decentralized coordination while varying hierarchy depth and peer width, measuring task completion and authorization preservation. Across generic multi-agent conditions, centralized hierarchies achieve 93.9--98.6% task completion versus 85.7--87.0% for peer networks, while unauthorized actions occur in 2.7--19.8% of tasks versus 0.6--0.8%, a gap that widens with hierarchy depth. We further compare two defenses that differ in where authorization evidence resides. One re-anchors every pending call to the original user request. The other carries an attenuated policy along the delegation chain. Re-anchoring reduces unauthorized actions in every model configuration we evaluate, at a cost of 1.6 points of pooled completion. Chain propagation blocks required work instead, forfeiting up to 36.3 points. A heterogeneous case study confirms that the failure follows from coordination rather than model strength. MasDrift exposes a centralization tradeoff and makes authorization preservation a measurable property of MAS design.
  </details>

- **[Stabilizing the Modality Gap: Null-Space Projected Continual Learning for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.19839)**  `arXiv:2507.19839`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Tiantian Peng, Yuyang Liu, Shuo Yang, Qiuhe Hong, YongHong Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The modality gap is intrinsic to multimodal alignment, where embeddings from different modalities remain partially separated in a shared representation space. In continual learning, CLIP-based vision-language models (VLMs) not only suffer catastrophic forgetting but also experience modality gap drift that disrupts the shared embedding geometry. Such drift degrades cross-modal alignment and generalization. We propose FPCD, an efficient framework combining Fast Null-space Projection (FNP) and Contrastive Distillation (CD). FNP accumulates compact Gram matrices of previous tasks' activations and projects gradients onto layer-adaptive approximate null spaces to reduce interference with learned representations. By replacing singular value decomposition with symmetric eigendecomposition, FNP accelerates projection-basis construction by a factor of 5-9. CD uses a small reference set to align the bidirectional image-to-text and text-to-image similarity distributions of the fine-tuned model with CLIP, thereby preserving pretrained cross-modal relations. On the 11-task MTIL benchmark, FPCD achieves 87.6% Last, 76.4% Average, and 65.2% Transfer. It also maintains more stable modality gaps and stronger zero-shot retrieval performance on COCO Captions and Flickr30k than competing methods. These results highlight the importance of controlling modality gap drift during continual adaptation of VLMs.
  </details>

- **[P2TPO: Structure-Aligned Page-to-Token Credit Assignment for Full-Page Document Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21979)**  `arXiv:2610.21979`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yong Zu, Ninghua Yang, Zezhong Tan, Xinhong Ma, Ziqiang Dong, Yang Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end full-page document parsing must preserve the content and structure of heterogeneous elements, yet otherwise accurate outputs can contain local errors and omissions. A single page-level advantage does not explicitly identify which elements differ in quality across sampled outputs or where their errors occur. We introduce Page-to-Token Policy Optimization (P2TPO), a structure-aligned credit-assignment framework that operates on full-page policy trajectories. Reference-element alignment first associates block-level quality contrasts with generated spans. Unified Anchor Pricing (UAP) incorporates matched and missing element outcomes and provides feedback relative to a fixed quality ceiling when sampled outputs receive identical but imperfect scores for a reference element. Within matched blocks, Span Credit Redistribution (SCR) redistributes existing block credit according to aligned correct and erroneous spans, using boundary proxies for within-block omissions while preserving the total block-derived advantage across its tokens. Under a fixed-weight additive page utility, we show that element-indexed contrasts retain local quality differences that page-level aggregation can cancel out. In the main experimental comparison, P2TPO outperforms all evaluated policy optimization baselines in seed-averaged scores on each of the six document parsing benchmarks. Additional experiments demonstrate gains across model scales and initializations.
  </details>

- **[PocketTSLM: Deploying an On-Device Soft-Prompt Time-Series Language Model for Privacy-Preserving Smartphone Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21308)**  `arXiv:2610.21308`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Max Rosenblattl, Nikhil Krishnaswamy, Leon Nissen, Manuel Milling, Nicolas Zumarraga, Ning Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Consumer phones and wearables increasingly collect physiological time-series data, yet most mobile systems treat these streams as summaries or text rather than as temporal evidence. This representation is poorly matched to personal health systems that need to retrieve, route, or summarize sensor evidence while preserving temporal structure. Cloud-hosted time-series language models avoid smartphone limits, but they repeatedly move sensitive physiology off the device and add latency and per-query compute cost. Mobile model alternatives that aggregate time-series data into statistical features or serialize raw samples as text either compress temporal structure or exhaust the context and memory budgets of small on-device models. We present PocketTSLM, a mobile-native deployment of OpenTSLM SoftPrompt, the soft-prompt variant of the OpenTSLM time-series language model. PocketTSLM runs fully on a consumer smartphone by injecting encoded time-series windows as embeddings instead of tokenizing time series as text. A numerical parity protocol verifies identical greedy-decoded token sequences between the Swift port and a Python reference using Apple's MLX machine-learning framework. Answers also match the PyTorch reference, although rationale wording can differ. An on-device context-economics benchmark compares the soft-prompt path with a text-serialized baseline on the same backbone. In our benchmark format, soft-prompting uses approximately 12 times fewer time-series tokens and reduces prompt-prefill cost. PocketTSLM processes 30-second Sleep-EDF electroencephalography clips for sleep staging and a 1,000-sample (10 s) 12-lead electrocardiogram clip from ECG-QA. In comparison, text-serialized baselines exceed the phone's context or memory budget at longer time-series windows. All inference runs entirely on the device, with no health data transmitted during inference.
  </details>

- **[TimeCap: A Scalable Open-Source Pipeline for Captioning Wearable Health Time-Series Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21274)**  `arXiv:2610.21274`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Max Rosenblattl, Milana Gurbanova, Karl Aurel Deck, Narayan Schuetz, Thomas Kaar, Manuel Milling, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series data generated continuously across industrial, environmental, and especially health domains produce multimodal signals whose scale and heterogeneity make their processing and interpretation particularly challenging. Time-Series Language Models (TSLMs) promise scalable interpretation of these signals, yet remain bottlenecked by a fundamental lack of text-annotated time series pairs that are signal-grounded, reproducible, and extensible across modalities and schemas. Existing paired corpora are either closed or limited in scale and modality coverage. Annotation with large language models could scale them up, but its outputs cannot be regenerated exactly, which prevents others from re-deriving, extending, or auditing the resulting corpus. To address this, we release TimeCap, an open deterministic captioning pipeline with three reusable extractor tiers and a cross-channel synthesis layer that operates directly on the signal with no language model in the loop, extensible to new schemas by writing channel definitions alone. This positions TimeCap as a reproducible mechanism for future dataset generation rather than a one-off release. Applying TimeCap to OpenMHC, the MyHeart Counts wearable dataset, yields MHCap, the largest open caption layer for multimodal wearable time-series data. MHCap spans 19 Apple HealthKit channels and 7,005,763 captions over 908,944 person-days. To showcase the impact of the corpus, we train OpenTSLM, a time-series language model, on MHCap and benchmark on health-outcome prediction. Caption-augmented training (OpenTSLM-Cap) outperforms a label-only baseline on 29 of 32 per-user binary evaluations across 8 tasks.
  </details>

- **[Isolating a Language Model's Judgment with a Label-Free Subspace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21256)**  `arXiv:2610.21256`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Chaemin Jang, Donggyu Lee, Jeesoo Kim, Dongman Lee, Jihee Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models are often asked to make judgments: whether a proposed answer is correct, whether one statement follows logically from another, or whether a response is safe. Yet changing the instructions that specify how the judgment should be expressed can make the final answer less reliable, even for the same input. We ask whether this reflects a change in internal judgment or a failure to express it. Comparing hidden-state vectors for the same input under different instructions, we find that their differences concentrate in a low-dimensional expression subspace. We identify this subspace without benchmark labels and remove its contribution from a linear predictor to obtain an isolated judgment. Across correctness, entailment, and safety, the isolated judgment remains reliable under instructions not used to identify the subspace, even when the model's final answer and the original predictor degrade. When the judgment question is negated, isolation raises the predictor's AUROC from 0.504 to 0.825 for entailment and from 0.480 to 0.891 for safety. We also find that judgment is readable in earlier layers, before the expression subspace begins to disrupt the predictor. Together, these results provide a way to separate judgment from expression and recover internal judgments even when the model's final answers are unreliable.
  </details>

- **[INCONVENIENT: A Benchmark to Assess LLM Generalization Capabilities from Convenience Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21038)**  `arXiv:2610.21038`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Sara Rezaeimanesh, Chad Klochko, Kundan Thind, Mohammad Ghassemi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning models for tabular prediction typically assume that training data are representative of the population on which they will be deployed. In practice, however, data are often collected based on convenience, availability, or cost, resulting in systematic sampling biases that can distort feature--target relationships and undermine real-world generalization. Existing tabular distribution-shift benchmarks primarily construct out-of-distribution (OOD) settings by partitioning observations across naturally occurring domains. While such partitions may induce changes in P(Y | X), the resulting shifts are neither explicitly controlled nor isolated, and the evaluation distributions themselves are often not representative of the broader target population. We introduce INCONVENIENT, a benchmark designed to study robustness to convenience-sample bias. Rather than relying on naturally occurring domain partitions, INCONVENIENT directly perturbs feature--target relationships in the training distribution while preserving the original population distribution as the deployment environment. This setting more closely reflects real-world scenarios in which models are developed from unrepresentative samples and subsequently deployed on a broader population. The distinction is particularly relevant for large language models (LLMs), whose pretraining may encode population-level knowledge that extends beyond the relationships observed in a biased training dataset. Unlike existing distribution-shift benchmarks, INCONVENIENT provides explicit control over both the direction and severity of relationship shifts, enabling a systematic assessment of robustness to convenience sampling. The benchmark comprises 200 tabular classification and regression datasets spanning diverse application domains and generates around 15,500 shifted training distributions which lead to substantial downstream degradation.
  </details>

- **[Gibbs-Gap Policy Optimization for Off-Policy RL with Verifiable Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20814)**  `arXiv:2610.20814`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xuanchang Zhang, Tong Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards often trains language models on responses generated by a different, possibly stale behavior policy. Correcting this mismatch with learner-to-behavior importance weights can introduce high variance for long responses. We introduce Gibbs-Gap Policy Optimization (GGPO), which minimizes a log-mean-exp contrast of KL-adjusted rewards among responses to the same prompt, without learner-to-behavior importance weights. We derive GGPO from KL-regularized policy optimization: on policy, its population loss is exactly the KL-regularized optimality gap, and under suitable coverage the learner's single-response expected reward is at least the behavior policy's best-of-$K$ reward, minus terms for the raw finite-group loss, regularization, and finite training data. We characterize reference refresh through exact Gibbs-target composition and a finite-data final-policy reward bound under local reference control and tokenwise behavior mismatch. This refresh is empirically important: in an ablation, fixed-reference GGPO scores $32.96$ on the five-benchmark problem-weighted average, versus $46.58$ with the default refresh schedule. Experiments on mathematical reasoning further show that GGPO outperforms GRPO and AGRO under training-inference mismatch and on fixed offline data, while maintaining its performance as controlled policy staleness grows.
  </details>

- **[RM-OPD: On-Policy Distillation for Generative Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20174)**  `arXiv:2610.20174`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Qinglin Su, Licheng Pan, Zhen Fang, Jiakang Wang, Qingnan Ren, Yunsheng Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning on Mixed-domain preference data (Mix-RL) has become the mainstream approach for training general-purpose Generative Reward Models (GRMs). However, Mix-RL suffers from two critical bottlenecks: the reward sparsity induced by outcome rewards, and the cross-domain interference arising from jointly optimizing heterogeneous capabilities, which together give rise to imbalanced capability acquisition and pervasive reward hacking. Inspired by the success of On-Policy Distillation (OPD) in multi-domain capability integration, we introduce RM-OPD (Reward Modeling via On-Policy Distillation), a two-stage framework that separates specialist acquisition from capability integration. First, we cultivate domain-specialized teacher models via single-domain GRPO fine-tuning, allowing each expert to reach its performance ceiling in isolation; then we consolidate these teachers into one student model through (i) routed on-policy distillation, which provides dense signed token-level supervision on student-generated critiques; (ii) domain contribution reweighting, which reweights token guidance toward target domain shares, preventing specific domains from dominating optimization; and (iii) optional criteria context distillation, which conditions teachers on domain-specific evaluation criteria to sharpen token-level supervision. Across multiple RM benchmarks and two base models, RM-OPD consistently outperforms vanilla Mix-RL and other strong baselines, achieving overall superior performance and exhibiting an emergent "teacher-surpassing" effect. These results establish RM-OPD as a promising paradigm for building generalist GRMs.
  </details>

- **[Thinking-Free Policy Initialization Makes Distilled Reasoning Models More Effective and Efficient Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26226)**  `arXiv:2509.26226`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xin Xu, Clive Bai, Kai Yang, Tianhao Chen, Yang Wang, Saiyong Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR) effectively solves complex tasks but demands extremely long context lengths during training, leading to substantial computational costs. While multi-stage training can partially mitigate this, starting with overly short contexts often causes irreversible performance degradation, ultimately failing to reduce overall training compute significantly. In this paper, we introduce Thinking-Free Policy Initialization (TFPI), a simple yet effective adaptation to RLVR that bridges long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) distillation and standard RLVR. TFPI employs a simple ThinkingFree operation, explicitly discarding the thinking content via a direct </think> append, to reduce token usage during inference. Training with ThinkingFree-adapted inputs improves performance and lowers token consumption, even in the original slow-thinking mode. Extensive experiments across various benchmarks have shown that TFPI accelerates RL convergence, achieves a higher performance ceiling, and yields more token-efficient reasoning models without specialized rewards or complex training designs. With TFPI only, we train a 4B model to reach 89.0% accuracy on AIME24 and 65.5% on LiveCodeBench using less than 4K H20 hours.
  </details>

- **[SHRED: Self-Play for Heterogeneous Road-Users without Expert Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20866)**  `arXiv:2610.20866`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Wei-Jer Chang, Zhouchonghao Wu, Akshay Rangesh, Weixin Li, Masayoshi Tomizuka, Wei Zhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realistic traffic simulation and autonomous planning both depend on modeling interactions among heterogeneous road users whose dynamics, constraints, and behaviors differ fundamentally. Existing approaches predominantly learn realistic interaction behavior from large-scale human driving demonstrations. In this work, we ask whether such behavior can instead emerge purely through self-play. We present SHRED, a framework that jointly trains vehicles, pedestrians, and cyclists through large-scale heterogeneous self-play from random initialization, without demonstrations or logged-trajectory replay. SHRED combines type-specific dynamics and objectives with procedural scenario generation and per-agent randomization, allowing diverse behaviors to emerge through closed-loop interaction. On the Waymo Open Sim Agents Challenge (WOSAC), SHRED jointly controls all three road-user types and outperforms both the closest demonstration-free self-play baseline and prior self-play methods that use demonstration-trained reference policies. Policies trained only on nuPlan map geometry retain comparable realism under zero-shot evaluation on Waymo. For planning, SHRED outperforms all compared planners on the interaction-heavy InterPlan benchmark. Controlled ablations show that heterogeneity in self-play improves both traffic realism and planning performance, with particularly large planning gains in interaction-heavy scenarios. Together, these results establish heterogeneous procedural self-play as a scalable, demonstration-free paradigm for learning realistic and interactive traffic behavior.
  </details>

- **[Twice Upon a Time: Complementary Representations for Time-Series Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22327)**  `arXiv:2610.22327`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hedi Zisling, Liran Nochumsohn, Ilan Naiman, Mihir Parmar, Omri Azencot_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time Series Language Models (TSLMs) enable language-based understanding and reasoning over temporal data, yet a fundamental question remains: what information should their time-series representations preserve, and what variation should they factor out? Existing TSLMs typically expose the language model to a single representation, imposing a fixed inductive bias across downstream tasks. We instead introduce complementary representations as a design principle for time-series reasoning. Our model combines two visual views: line plots preserve absolute measurements and the observed coordinate frame, while normalized delay embeddings factor out positive amplitude rescaling and offsets while exposing temporal structure. Dedicated visual encoders integrate both views into a shared language model through visual tokens and multi-level feature fusion. Evaluation-time interventions verify that the two views exhibit distinct invariance profiles, while single-view ablations show that their combination is particularly beneficial beyond basic perception, with the large gains in prediction and reasoning. Built on Qwen3-VL-8B, our dual-view model improves overall TSRBench accuracy from 44.5% with line plots alone to 53.2%, substantially exceeding the strongest evaluated dedicated TSLM baseline at 36.7%. It further achieves 89.9% accuracy on TSExam and 86.8% numerical accuracy on multivariate ChatTS alignment. Together, these results demonstrate that complementary representations provide an effective design principle for general-purpose TSLMs, enabling models to exploit temporal structure without sacrificing access to the original measurement frame.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22236)**  `arXiv:2610.22236`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bangji Yang, Jiajun Fan, Zhaoyu Zhu, Hongbo Ma, Ge Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation consolidates specialist models into one student at the price of rollouts, teacher passes and gradient steps for every teacher. We show that the consolidation does not need training: with forward passes only, the capabilities of separately tuned specialists can be aggregated into one model that is, on average, more accurate than either specialist alone. When specialists share a base model, distillation has an exact layer-wise solution: each linear layer of the fused model must reproduce every specialist's output on the inputs that specialist receives while reading its own answers, a least-squares problem whose solution needs only the second moments of those inputs. One forward pass over a few hundred rollouts provides them. We call this training-free on-policy distillation (\method). What the rollouts contain matters more than the solver: answers truncated in repetition loops are few among a mathematics specialist's rollouts but carry a large share of their tokens, and removing them lifts the fused model on AIME25 from task arithmetic's level to the specialist's at matched precision. On Olmo~3 7B, a few GPU-hours of forward computation per specialist yield a generalist that keeps most of each specialist's gain on each of seven mathematics and code benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Judging the Judges: Confidence and Disagreement in Rubrics as Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21179)**  `arXiv:2610.21179`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Michael Tian-Yue Liu, Anne Harrington, Trevor Darrell, Yutong Bai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubrics as Rewards (RaR) extends reinforcement learning to non-verifiable domains by using an LLM judge to score responses against a structured rubric. Prior work has focused on improving the rubric or strengthening the single judge, with comparatively little attention to how different single or multiple judges might be used during RaR training. We first show that judge identity alone produces a substantial spread in scores when training Qwen3-4B-Base on RaR-Medicine and RaR-Science across five frontier and open-weight judges. Building on this, we propose RaRE (Rubrics as Rewards with Ensembles), a novel judge ensemble strategy for RaR with two mechanisms: a soft-Likert reward that uses judge logprobs to weight more confident judges more heavily, and a drop-on-disagreement mask that excludes high-disagreement rollouts from the GRPO update. We find that this design is critical: while a naive average of judge scores fails to outperform the best single judge, RaRE outperforms every single-judge RaR variant on both HealthBench and GPQA Diamond, with per-theme analysis showing that it inherits the complementary strengths of its constituent judges. Our findings establish judge ensembling as a compelling axis for improving RaR.
  </details>

- **[Causal Integrated Gradients: Scalable Feature Attribution Along Causal Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22126)**  `arXiv:2610.22126`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Joran Michiels, Anis Ismail, Maarten De Vos, Alejandro Sifrim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing feature attribution methods that account for feature dependencies do not scale to high dimensions. We propose causal integrated gradients (CIG) and causal expected gradients (CEG), which extend integrated gradients to incorporate prior causal structure among input features while retaining its computational efficiency and, under conditions on the causal graph, its axiomatic guarantees. In many settings, some causal structure between features is already known, for example, that a transcription factor causally regulates a target gene's expression. Yet existing gradient-based methods cannot exploit this structure, instead crediting a feature's downstream effects over its true upstream cause, such as ranking the target gene above the transcription factor that regulates it. Our key idea is to embed this causal structure by routing the integration path along the causal graph. The method thereby decomposes each feature's attribution into a direct effect and effects mediated through its causal descendants. We evaluate CIG in three settings of increasing realism. First, on a synthetic benchmark with a known causal model, CIG's attributions best match the ground-truth causal effect from Pearl's do-calculus, matching the accuracy of causal Shapley values at a fraction of the computational cost.
