Second, on simulated single-cell gene expression data with a known gene regulatory network (Dyngen), CIG most accurately recovers the transcription factors driving the simulation. Third, on real interferon-$\beta$ stimulation data, where only an approximate causal graph is available, CIG better prioritizes the transcription factors driving the measured response than DeepSHAP and integrated gradients. Together, these results show that incorporating causal structure into gradient-based attributions recovers upstream drivers that existing methods miss, without sacrificing scalability.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Pairwise Difference Learning: A Comparative Study of Encoding Strategies and Training Pair Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21644)**  `arXiv:2610.21644`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bernard Georges, Mohamed Karim Belaid, Manuel Rodrigues_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pairwise Difference Learning for Classification (PDC) enhances supervised learning in low-data regimes by training a binary similarity classifier on pairs of instances rather than individual samples, effectively inflating the training set from $n$ to $O(n^2)$ examples. However, the PDC framework leaves critical implementation choices underspecified, particularly regarding input representation and pair selection strategies. This work systematically investigates these design decisions through extensive empirical evaluation. We compare four feature encoders (paired features, triplet with relative difference, absolute difference, and a novel triplet with absolute difference) alongside four pair selection strategies ranging from exhaustive to kernel-thinning-based schemes. Experiments across nearly 200 benchmark classification datasets using Decision Tree, Multilayer Perceptron, Gaussian Naive Bayes, and Support Vector Classifier as base models reveal that the proposed triplet with absolute difference encoder ($\phi_{\mathrm{3dabs}}$) achieves the highest average F1 scores. More notably, restricting training to upper-triangle pairs reduces computational time by approximately 40% with negligible accuracy loss. Kernel thinning-based selection provided no accuracy benefits while substantially increasing computational overhead. Overall, PDC configurations outperformed base models in 94% of tested cases, though no single configuration dominated universally, suggesting dataset-specific optimization remains an open challenge.
  </details>

- **[When Does Muon Actually Win? A Paired Data-, Time-, and Tuning-Budget Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21555)**  `arXiv:2610.21555`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mushir Akhtar, Hazem N. Nounou, Majdi Mansouri, Mohamed N. Nounou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon's gains in data efficiency motivate evaluating its performance under explicit data, time, and tuning budgets. On a single A100 GPU, we compare a reference single-device Muon implementation with AdamW across 17.7M- and 33.6M-parameter causal language models and 2.70M-parameter vision transformers. A common 23-candidate development budget selects configurations evaluated with five matched seeds, full validation trajectories, optimizer-step timing, and a frozen one-shot test. Muon reduces validation-loss area under the curve by 3.25--22.44\% at equal data and lowers test loss in all 20 primary pairs. In this configuration, Muon improves time-normalized loss area} by 19.68--22.31\% on vision, while AdamW is better by 3.38--6.96\% on language. The full Muon optimizer call, including auxiliary AdamW updates, consumes 56.1--69.6\% of training time, compared with 6.1--9.1\% for the AdamW baseline. Muon also has higher expected regret under restricted access to these candidate pools. These results characterize small models with reference kernels; their wall-clock ordering does not establish a ranking for larger models or optimized implementations.
  </details>

- **[V-SHARE: Provable Convergence Guarantees for Advantage-based Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21448)**  `arXiv:2610.21448`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kintan Saha, Hamed Masoudi, Xinyi Sheng, Dominik Baumann, Joni Pajarinen, Arsenii Mustafin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In reinforcement learning, advantage-based value-learning methods, unlike standard Q-learning, share information across actions by decomposing the action-value function into a state-dependent value component and action-specific advantages. While this mechanism has proven effective in both tabular and deep reinforcement learning, its theoretical understanding remains incomplete. We introduce V-SHARE, an advantage-based value-learning algorithm that combines cross-action value sharing with an importance-weighted Bellman-residual correction. To our knowledge, we provide the first complete theoretical characterization of an advantage-based algorithm, including both asymptotic convergence and non-asymptotic finite-sample guarantees. We identify a regime in which V-SHARE mitigates the rare-action bottleneck of asynchronous Q-learning and uncover a contraction--variance tradeoff between stronger deterministic contraction and increased importance-weighting noise. Motivated by this tradeoff, we develop decreasing residual-preconditioning schedules that combine faster early contraction with lower asymptotic noise. We further develop a two-timescale analysis that removes the single-timescale residual-weight threshold while preserving convergence guarantees. Experiments on discrete Markov decision processes (MDPs) and deep reinforcement-learning benchmarks corroborate our results.
  </details>

- **[Memorilla: Latent Semantic Memory for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21375)**  `arXiv:2610.21375`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Adibvafa Fallahpour, Parsa Idehpour, Vignesh Kothapalli, Nikita Mounier, Shirley Wu, Shayan Pardis, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory enables large language models (LLMs) to use information beyond their parameters, such as past user conversations, long documents, or an agent's earlier actions. For a frozen LLM, access to this accumulated knowledge is limited to what a memory system places in its context window. However, gathering and combining relevant information needed for each query while keeping the model's context short remains challenging for existing systems. Here we introduce Memorilla, a memory system that gathers information from an entire document collection into a fixed number of query-dependent 'memory tokens' for a frozen LLM. Memorilla encodes documents (such as user chats, book chapters) once into reusable embeddings, then learns to retrieve and place query-specific information in the LLM's input embedding space. Using only 16 memory tokens, Memorilla achieves the best performance against baselines including Mem0 and MemoRAG on five of eight benchmarks spanning personalization, long-document and knowledge-intensive question answering. Notably, on FactKG, it reaches 84.33% accuracy versus 80.43% with full-context inference, while using 108x fewer tokens to represent the source documents. Furthermore, Memorilla can facilitate continual learning in agentic systems where the document count (the agent's past turns) increases throughout the trajectory. In a TextWorld environment, we show that Memorilla reduces the agent's tendency to wander between rooms it has already explored, and improves its average success rate from 18.6% with full-history in-context learning to 24.2%. Overall, Memorilla aids frozen LLMs in accumulating and using knowledge effectively across long-context interactions and takes a step towards building continually learning AI systems.
  </details>

- **[DST-Net: Decomposed Spatio-Temporal Network for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21123)**  `arXiv:2610.21123`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yaohua Zha, Hai Wu, Shu-Tao Xia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Series decomposition has emerged as a widely acknowledged paradigm in deep time series forecasting models. However, existing approaches predominantly employ a homogeneous modeling strategy for all subsequences, thereby overlooking the distinct inductive biases inherent in different components. We identify that the trend component exhibits dominant spatial coupling across channels but carries significant temporal redundancy, whereas the seasonal component is characterized by local temporal evolution but remains susceptible to attention distraction. To address this, we propose a novel disentangled architecture that tailors specific modeling strategies to align with the intrinsic properties of each component. For the trend branch, we design a Latent Graph Block, employing latent graph modeling to capture global topological dependencies and system-wide co-movements. Conversely, for the seasonal branch, we introduce a Gaussian Multi-scale Attention mechanism to mitigate attention distraction. By injecting learnable Gaussian priors, we impose structural constraints that compel the model to focus on multi-granular local contexts, effectively filtering out global noise and accurately capturing local evolutionary phases. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed model.
  </details>

- **[Amortizing Sequential Experimental Design in Reasoning LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20832)**  `arXiv:2610.20832`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Anita Kriz, Antonin Berthon, Nicolás Astorga, Mihaela van der Schaar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite their extensive pretraining knowledge and strong reasoning capabilities, large language models (LLMs) struggle to adaptively gather information in experimental design tasks. We study how to amortize sequential Bayesian experimental design (BED) into an LLM by explicitly training the reasoning that guides action selection using information-gain rewards. Our approach specifies the experimental generative model independently of the LLM's own beliefs, allowing us to optimize expected information gain under a model of our choosing. We investigate the design choices for amortization, including alternative generative-model constructions, reward estimation, hypothesis-space specification, multi-step objectives, and computational costs relative to training- and inference-time BED baselines. On Twenty Questions, the resulting policies improve information acquisition and task performance, outperform inference- and training-time BED baselines at substantially lower computational cost, and generalize to unseen categories. We further demonstrate positive transfer from Twenty Questions training to Medi-Q, a real-world clinical dialogue benchmark. These results suggest that information gathering can be learned as a generalizable reasoning skill in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Retrieval Over Training: Similarity search-based Model Selection for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20243)**  `arXiv:2610.20243`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Christos Panourgias, Roberto Stanzione, Adrien Petralia, Themis Palpanas, Paul Boniol_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Anomaly detection is a fundamental task for time-series analytics, with important implications for the downstream performance of many applications. Despite the large number of anomaly detection methods proposed in the literature, recent benchmark studies have shown that no single detector performs best across highly heterogeneous time series. Therefore, a practical and scalable solution is to develop a model-selection method that, for a given time series, selects the anomaly detector most likely to perform well. Nevertheless, the model selection approaches proposed in the literature suffer from a significant drop in accuracy when applied in Out-of-Distribution (OOD) settings. In this paper, we tackle the aforementioned limitation and propose RAMSAD, a retrieval-based framework for model selection in time-series anomaly detection. Given a new time series, our method queries a knowledge base of previously observed instances, retrieves the top-k most similar series, and transfers detector recommendations from their performance profiles. The framework can operate with standard similarity measures as well as embedding-based representations. Our results show that RAMSAD outperforms existing baselines in In-Distribution (ID) settings and is the most robust selector in OOD, where, when selecting a small subset of detectors, it matches or exceeds ensembles that run the entire detector pool, at a fraction of their cost. Overall, we demonstrate that similarity-based retrieval constitutes a strong and efficient foundation for model selection in time-series anomaly detection.
  </details>

- **[World Modeling through Spectral Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15429)**  `arXiv:2610.15429`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Holger Molin, William Peng, Marco Bagatella, Randall Balestriero_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How do we tell a world model what information to preserve? We study this question through the lens of spectral representation learning. We introduce SpecWM, a JEPA framework that explicitly specifies desired relationships between observations through a target similarity kernel and trains the encoder to reproduce those relationships in its embedding geometry. Different kernels provide different supervision, ranging from physical state information to a fully self-supervised temporal kernel requiring only the ordering of observations within trajectories. We characterize the global optima of the objective, establish conditions for linear recoverability and invariance to nuisance information, and connect temporal-kernel similarity to the behavioral successor measure under an idealized setting. Empirically, the choice of kernel strongly controls which physical quantities are recoverable from the representation. More importantly, temporal spectral alignment improves CEM planning success over LeWorldModel from 52% to 68% on OGBench Cube, 49% to 77% on OGBench Scene, and 29% to 51% on CALVIN. These results suggest that explicitly specifying representation geometry provides a simple way to steer what latent world models preserve for control.
  </details>

- **[Telling FIBS: Probes Effectively Detect Sabotage and Catch Unverbalized Deception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12445)**  `arXiv:2610.12445`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Oskar J. Hollinsworth, Alex F. Spies, Tigist Diriba, Adam Gleave, Chris Cundy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent incidents have highlighted the challenge of monitoring LLM agents and the danger of models deceiving people. We show that white-box deception detection via probes can be scaled up to frontier monitoring settings by collecting the largest deception dataset to date for training probes and introducing a novel probe architecture which can aggregate information across many layers and tokens. Our probes surpass black-box monitors on agentic coding benchmarks, achieving 98.3% AUROC in SHADE-Arena and 98.0% in BashArena (vs. 97.7%, 97.6% resp. for action-only text monitors), and show improved efficacy as the underlying model is scaled up. We further test probes on several cases where deception cannot be determined from the context alone; in these cases of introspective deception, the ground truth can only be determined through careful elicitation or knowledge of a model's training data. In one such evaluation, we show that probes can distinguish transcripts containing a model's true hidden goal from other goals with an AUROC of up to 99.7%. Our probes also detect deception on prominent open-weight models which lie about politically sensitive topics when put under pressure. We release our training dataset, dubbed FIBS, to help drive frontier deployment of effective probes, and encourage the community to expand upon it with further examples of deception and sabotage.
  </details>

- **[D-DOIT: Training-free Adaptation of Discrete Diffusion via Doob's h-Transform](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04938)**  `arXiv:2610.04938`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jieke Wu, Qijie Zhu, Weimin Wu, Zeqi Ye, Minshuo Chen, Han Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose D-DOIT (Discrete Doob-Oriented Inference-time Transformation), a training-free and efficient adaptation method for discrete diffusion models with generic rewards. D-DOIT formulates adaptation as sampling from a reward-tilted target distribution and realizes this transport through Doob's h-transform of the discrete diffusion reverse kernel, using only reward values rather than reward gradients. Unlike continuous diffusion, masked discrete diffusion samples categorical token-reveal transitions rather than continuous state updates. D-DOIT derives the corresponding discrete Doob's h-transform, which guides sampling by reweighting reverse transition probabilities instead of adding a drift correction. To make this transformation practical, D-DOIT avoids expensive future rollouts. At each guided step, D-DOIT samples candidate next states, uses the model prediction head to complete each candidate into a clean sequence, evaluates each completion with the reward oracle, and resamples the next state with probabilities proportional to the rewards. An optional late-stage best-of-K refinement further improves sample quality by branching trajectories only near the end of denoising, avoiding the $K$-fold cost over the full trajectory. Empirically, across regulatory DNA design and protein inverse folding benchmarks, D-DOIT outperforms training-free guidance baselines. It improves enhancer activity and cell-type specificity while preserving sequence naturalness, and achieves the highest success rate in protein inverse folding.
  </details>

- **[ULTRADISCOVERY: Abductive Exploration in an Interconnected, Epistemically Open Universe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03092)**  `arXiv:2610.03092`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Weihan Li, Tianshi Zheng, Yangqiu Song, Ginny Y. Wong, Simon See_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery often begins when scattered clues call for a new way of describing the world. Such abductive exploration can require constructing the representation in which an explanation is stated, when the world is epistemically open, and composing evidence scattered across contexts, when it is structurally interconnected. Existing benchmarks rarely separate these two demands or control them independently. We introduce ULTRADISCOVERY, an interactive world of five domains in which an agent revises an initially successful theory and predicts the outcome of an unseen cross-domain intervention. A $2 \times 2$ design leaves the representation open or discloses it, and leaves the evidence distributed or aligns it, with the latent dynamics fixed. With the representation open, agents across eleven models often retract the axiom they were taught, and none introduces the unobserved entity or rewrites the variables that a replacement requires. Disclosure triples intervention requests and adds about one of the eighteen findings the world affords, and alignment adds less. Two vendor-harness systems carry discovery into more domains, and one of them rewrites the variables in Open episodes. No system makes the exact prediction within 200 paid actions. At larger budgets one exact prediction appears with both aids, while every Open episode remains inexact. The results locate the difficulty in the step from accumulating evidence to composing it into a representation that transfers.
  </details>

- **[Efficiently Estimating Optimal Hyperparameter Scaling Laws through Power-Law Entropy Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01431)**  `arXiv:2609.01431`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiliang Chen, Sebastian Ament, David Eriksson, Maximilian Balandat, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low, Eytan Bakshy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimal hyperparameter scaling laws describe how the best hyperparameters for large language model (LLM) training change with model and data scale, enabling practitioners to predict optimal configurations at production scales without expensive large-scale tuning. However, estimating these scaling laws conventionally requires exhaustive grid searches over thousands of training runs, consuming enormous computational resources. We introduce Power-Law Entropy Search (PLES), a computational cost-aware acquisition function built on multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization that efficiently estimates optimal hyperparameter scaling laws through adaptive experimentation. A key innovation in PLES is that it searches for candidates that reduce the overall uncertainty of a scaling law estimate, instead of optimizing a single objective function. At each iteration, PLES selects the candidate configuration that maximally reduces the uncertainty of the scaling law estimates per unit computational cost, naturally favoring informative small-scale experiments. We evaluate PLES on synthetic benchmarks, surrogate models fitted to real LLM training data, and actual LLM pre-training runs. Across all settings, PLES converges to accurate optimal hyperparameter scaling laws using less than one-tenth of the computational budget required by conventional grid search and other baselines.
  </details>

- **[Tail-Replay: Escaping the Curse of Linear Attention in Prefix Caching for Hybrid LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30310)**  `arXiv:2608.30310`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yirui Liu, Ruoling Qi, Xuaner Wu, Penghang Liu, Jian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hybrid large language models interleave full-attention layers with linear-attention layers to reduce the cost of long-context inference. This structure complicates prefix caching: full-attention key-value caches are token-addressable, whereas linear-attention layers maintain recurrent states that cannot be rolled back to arbitrary prefix boundaries. Existing hybrid prefix caching methods address this mismatch by storing recurrent-state checkpoints. As a result, token-level matches are directly usable only at positions aligned with stored checkpoints, constraining prefix reuse to a discrete set of boundaries. We present Tail-Replay, a prefix caching mechanism that enables unconstrained token-level prefix reuse in hybrid large language models. The key insight is that linear-attention mechanisms such as Gated DeltaNet can be viewed as a structured, lossy compression of the input prefix: gated recurrent updates progressively attenuate the contributions of earlier inputs. Consequently, the recurrent state of a matched prefix can be well approximated by replaying only a short, recent suffix of that prefix. Tail-Replay exploits this property by caching the exact full-attention key-value cache while omitting recurrent-state checkpoints. On a cache hit, it reconstructs the linear-attention states by replaying a short, recent suffix of the matched prefix. As a result, the reuse boundary is determined by the shared tokens rather than by recurrent-state checkpoints. We evaluate Tail-Replay on three Gated DeltaNet-based hybrid models using the LongBench and RULER benchmarks. With only a 5--10\% replay budget, it retains 92.8--99.9\% of full-prefill quality on LongBench and RULER. For serving efficiency, we evaluate time-to-first-token speedups across multiple matched-prefix lengths---8K, 16K, and 32K. The speedup grows with prefix length, reaching $9.1$--$14.3\times$ over full prefill at 32K.
  </details>

- **[Circuit Claims Depend on What Is Extracted and How It Is Compared](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18921)**  `arXiv:2607.18921`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Sheng, Jie Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Circuit extraction identifies model components whose presence preserves a target behavior under ablation, but preserving behavior does not single out one circuit. We study this in a synthetic Lean tactic-prediction benchmark with fixed proof rules and randomized surface form. Across dense and weight-sparse checkpoints of the same transformer, evaluated on atomic and compositional proofs, we vary which extracted object is reported: a compact prediction-preserving circuit, a broader graph retaining surrounding read, write, and routing structure, or a subgraph extracted with a post-ablation loss budget. First, overlap between groups of component-to-component connections is low, sometimes close to a random baseline. Overlap is much higher for selected attention-head sets under deterministic selection, but falls sharply when selection among equally scored heads is randomized. Second, the normalized graph-size ranking of conditions that differ in which supervised checkpoint initializes reinforcement learning (RL) agrees between repeated extractions of the compact circuit and between joint and separate query/key representations of each of the other two graphs. For the loss-budgeted graph, query/key ordering agreement persists through the budget sweep. Finally, across those same conditions, the largest exact-match accuracy gains from RL on compositional proofs come with the largest proportion of components in the compositional circuit that lie outside the corresponding atomic circuits. Ablating these additional components in the sparse RL model reduces compositional accuracy while largely preserving accuracy on the corresponding atomic tasks. A circuit-level claim therefore requires specifying which circuit is reported and how it is extracted and compared; behavior alone does not determine its interpretation. We distill these requirements into a reporting practice for circuit-extraction studies.
  </details>

- **[HarnessMDP: Learning to Control LLM Agent Harnesses with Offline Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05458)**  `arXiv:2607.05458`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haiwen Yi, Xinyuan Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The execution harness is a critical component of LLM agents, shaping how the agent interacts with its environment and organizes multi-step execution. Prior work has shown that improving tools, workflows, and other harness components can substantially improve agent performance. This suggests that the harness can be treated as a learnable control layer that adapts execution decisions from experience, rather than as a predetermined execution configuration. In this work, we introduce HarnessMDP, a learning-based framework that makes harness control itself learnable. HarnessMDP formulates agent execution as a finite-horizon Markov decision process, where the harness learns to select structural execution actions from the evolving agent state. The harness policy is learned offline from collected trajectories using task performance and deployed online to adaptively control new executions. Extensive experiments on coding tasks, $\tau$-bench, AgentBench, and other agent evaluation settings show that HarnessMDP consistently improves execution behavior and final task performance over baseline harnesses. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of HarnessMDP and establish learnable harness control as a practical mechanism for improving LLM agents through better execution. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Calibration for High-Sparsity LLM Pruning: From Single Sources to Capability Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03328)**  `arXiv:2606.03328`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hu Xu, Zhaolong Xing, Congcong Liu, Jiaxing Wang, Zhida Jiang, Junshi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training LLM pruning relies on a small calibration set, usually chosen heuristically and judged by WikiText perplexity or by mean accuracy on general-domain commonsense benchmarks. This single-axis view hides capability-specific effects: at 60\% sparsity, strategies separated by $2.85$ points in averaged commonsense accuracy diverge by $51.9$ points in code retention, and across 15 sources no single one preserves all capabilities. We treat calibration as a capability-coverage problem. Balanced, a uniform four-way mixture under a fixed budget, tests the principle on real corpora: it outperforms every evaluated single source, and its margin over C4 grows from $+4.6$ points at 30\% sparsity to $+18.8$ at 60\%. The advantage holds across model scale and a second model family, and transfers to held-out benchmarks disjoint from calibration construction. When aligned corpora are unavailable, IGSP approximates that mixture from the base model and the evaluation taxonomy alone, outperforming prior self-generation methods by $7.23$--$12.79$ points on the LLaMA main suite. On Qwen2.5-7B, IGSP exceeds Balanced on both the main suite and held-out benchmarks. Capability coverage is a first-order design variable for high-sparsity pruning.
  </details>

- **[ChaosBench-Logic v2: Evaluating LLM Logical Reasoning over Dynamical Systems at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24305)**  `arXiv:2605.24305`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Noel Thomas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard accuracy on binary reasoning benchmarks hides critical failure modes: prior collapse, inconsistency under paraphrase, and inability to reason about parameter-dependent dynamics. We present ChaosBench-Logic v2, a 40,886-question benchmark over 165 dynamical systems with 27 FOL predicates and 78 axiom edges, together with CARE (Calibration- and Adversarial-Robust Evaluation), a protocol that surfaces these pathologies. Evaluating 14 models, we find that regime-transition reasoning remains near random (MCC = 0.05) even for frontier models, whereas FOL deduction with given premises reaches MCC = 0.52. Per-family decomposition shows that the proprietary-model advantage concentrates on cross-indicator (+0.40) and consistency tasks, while open-source Qwen 2.5-32B dominates indicator diagnostics (0.91 vs. 0.45). Two models exhibit negative MCC on bifurcation questions, confirmed as systematic anti-correlation via confusion-matrix analysis.
  </details>

- **[CRAFT: Forgetting-Aware Intervention-Based Adaptation for Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05732)**  `arXiv:2605.05732`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Md Anwar Hossen, Fatema Siddika, Juan Pablo Munoz, Tanya Roosta, Ali Jannesari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can learn new tasks through fine-tuning, but learning tasks sequentially often leads to catastrophic forgetting of earlier ones. This paper proposes CRAFT, a continual learning framework that keeps model weights frozen and instead learns low-rank interventions on hidden representations. Similar tasks share an intervention to help knowledge transfer, with similarity judged by how each task alters the model's output distribution rather than by a learned gating network. Within a shared intervention, a Kullback-Leibler (KL) penalty keeps each new task close to the intervention's existing behavior, which limits forgetting of earlier tasks, while a small, task-private intervention in the deepest layers preserves dedicated capacity that later tasks cannot overwrite. The learned interventions also make it possible to identify the task at test time, so task identity labels are not needed. Across multiple benchmarks and model scales, CRAFT improves overall performance and reduces forgetting compared with strong LoRA-based methods, while remaining robust to task ordering.
  </details>

- **[PathFM: Fast Generative Forecasting of Multivariate Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22358)**  `arXiv:2610.22358`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jiachen Yao, Xiyuan Zhang, Annan Yu, Boran Han, Abdul Fatir Ansari, Shuai Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models are becoming increasingly popular for general-purpose forecasting. However, most leading models predict marginal quantiles, leaving the dependence structure of future trajectories unspecified. This distinction matters for trajectory-dependent quantities such as cumulative demand, peak load, and event timing, yet point and marginal scores do not reveal it. To model this dependence, we introduce PathFM, a trajectory-based forecasting model that uses flow matching to generate complete future paths. We further distill the model to one or two steps and share history attention cache across draws. Together, these enable PathFM to generate multiple paths for a single series in roughly the time needed for one query to a quantile model. The distilled sampler also makes it practical to optimize a calibration loss directly. PathFM achieves competitive marginal accuracy on the established forecasting benchmarks fev-bench and TIME. To complement these evaluations, we introduce Forecast Path Score Bench (fps-bench), which augments fev-bench tasks with energy and variogram scores and downstream metrics. PathFM achieves a higher aggregate path score on fps-bench than all evaluated external baselines. Experiments on chaotic systems further show that our flow model sustains long-run variability, while strong baselines rapidly collapse. PathFM makes generative time series forecasting both competitive and practical, enabling diverse downstream applications.
  </details>

- **[Lean Graph Search for Formal Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22339)**  `arXiv:2610.22339`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yihan Xu, Jia-Chen Gu, Junyi Zhang, Di Wu, Haikang Deng, Po-Nien Kung, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieving relevant lemmas is crucial for formal theorem proving, enabling provers to build machine-checkable proofs from established mathematical results. Recent retrieval systems match library declarations to a target theorem or its intermediate proof steps, but make limited use of multi-hop dependencies among declarations. These dependencies can reveal supporting lemmas that are not necessarily lexically similar to the target theorem but recur in proofs of related problems. To this end, we propose LeanGraphSearch, a graph-based retrieval method for Lean 4 that constructs a graph from Mathlib's forward dependencies and traverses it to uncover supporting lemmas through multi-hop dependency chains. Specifically, LeanGraphSearch links each declaration to the premises it depends on, uses a graph-based ranking algorithm to propagate relevance from semantically matched declarations to their dependencies, and reranks the expanded candidates against the original query. Across two mathematical retrieval benchmarks, LeanGraphSearch improves recall by up to 12% over the strongest baseline, LeanSearch v2, on MathlibQR and increases coverage of complete annotated proof routes on MathlibMPR, covering up to 3.7% of extra groups. More importantly, we evaluate whether these retrieval gains translate into improved end-to-end theorem proving by integrating LeanGraphSearch into an iterative proving loop guided by Lean feedback. Across three theorem proving benchmarks, LeanGraphSearch improves pass rate at {8, 16, 32} reflection turns by as much as 40% compared to vanilla prover and at most 10% compared to LeanSearch v2, showing higher accuracy-cost efficiency. These findings highlight the value of dependency graph search for theorem proving and motivate further research on structure-aware search for proof discovery.
  </details>

- **[Topology-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Wind-Field Super-Resolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22262)**  `arXiv:2610.22262`  `cs.LG`  
  _Arjun Dadhwal, Brian Summa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific super-resolution (SR) models are typically optimized with pointwise reconstruction losses, yet scientific interpretation often hinges on extrema, connected regions, and multiscale scalar structure. We characterize this tension with pretrained CNN and GAN wind-field SR models. Pointwise metrics, bottleneck persistence-diagram (PD) distance, and merge-tree (MT) distance favor the CNN, whereas order-2 Wasserstein PD distances favor the sharper GAN, so even different distances on the same topological descriptor reward different properties. Motivated by this disagreement, we fine-tune the pretrained CNN with differentiable topology-inspired auxiliary losses on scalar speed, gradient magnitude, soft high-speed level sets, and local maxima, and with fixed-pair supervision, which constrains predicted values and persistence at ground-truth persistence-pair endpoints located offline with the Topology ToolKit. A loss study of 16 fine-tuning configurations, each compared with a matched standard fine-tuning control that uses only the original reconstruction loss, on a fixed 168-field benchmark (with topology evaluated on a fixed 160 by 160 subregion of each field), shows that, among the scalar-field losses, gradient supervision provides the strongest PD improvement but does not improve mean MT distance, whereas fixed-pair supervision gives the largest and most consistent joint improvement in PD and MT distances. The final objective combines these two signals. Relative to the pretrained CNN, it lowers mean bottleneck, $W_{2,\infty}$, and $W_{2,2}$ distances by 31%, 25%, and 27% (and by 32-35% relative to the control, which on its own worsens all three), lowers all three on 150 of 168 fields, and gains 1.30 dB PSNR, about half the gain of standard fine-tuning. Mean MT distance also improves, but on only 91 of those 150 fields, indicating that PD and MT capture complementary structure.
  </details>

- **[Interfaces and Modules: A Scalable Representation for Weight-Space Learning across Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21995)**  `arXiv:2610.21995`  `cs.LG`  
  _Michał Kozyra, Michael Bronstein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space learning across architectures requires representations that respect parameter symmetries and scale to large networks. Existing equivariant tensor encoders require architecture-specific symmetry specifications, while neuron graphs make global attention costly. We introduce a typed graph organized around shared coordinate-permutation actions. Its nodes, called interfaces, represent coordinate spaces whose bound tensor axes must be permuted jointly; operation occurrences, called modules, form typed hyperedges. A compiler derives this graph from supported PyTorch models, with exact recovery of the permutation factors defined by its constraints. The Universal Weight Encoder (UWE) combines local equivariant updates to parameter features with global attention over invariant interface summaries. The small number of interfaces makes global attention practical, and a single encoder processes different supported architectures. UWE is competitive with architecture-specific state-of-the-art methods on established benchmarks. On a new zoo of 1,200 transformers spanning 100 architectures, it outperforms the evaluated baselines at ranking networks within unseen architectures, including architectures larger than those used for training, while using substantially less memory.
  </details>

- **[ChronoSheaf: Joint Spatio-Temporal Spectral Filtering on Cellular Sheaves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21958)**  `arXiv:2610.21958`  `cs.LG`  
  _Abeer Mostafa, Zahra Ahmadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sheaf neural networks generalize graph neural networks by assigning each edge a learned linear restriction map that specifies how the local representations of adjacent nodes relate to one another, thereby mitigating oversmoothing. Existing sheaf architectures, however, define the sheaf Laplacian as an instantaneous spatial operator and therefore cannot explicitly model how signals evolve over time on a graph. We first introduce a spectral sheaf Laplacian, defined jointly over space and time, such that a single operator regulates signal propagation across both the spatial graph and the temporal domain. Directly extending this operator's receptive field in space or time would require increasingly complex sheaf structures. Instead, we propose a spectral filter that expands the effective receptive field along both dimensions without enlarging the underlying sheaf. Building on this operator, we introduce ChronoSheaf, a spatio-temporal sheaf network with two key components. First, Transitional Restriction Maps (TRM) decompose each node's restriction map into a spatial term and two temporal frames that independently control its relation to neighboring nodes at the preceding and following timestep, enabling asymmetric temporal influence. Second, the Dual Spectral Filter (DSF) applies a single bounded-degree polynomial filter with an adaptively estimated spectral radius to the joint spatio-temporal sheaf operator. We validate ChronoSheaf on a diverse set of spatio-temporal forecasting benchmarks, where it outperforms state-of-the-art baselines. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Lagrangian Structure of Cost-Penalized Active Feature Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21919)**  `arXiv:2610.21919`  `cs.LG`  
  _Joseph Bingham_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) sequentially selects features to maximize predictive accuracy under an acquisition budget. A natural heuristic greedily maximizes Expected Information Gain minus a cost penalty, EIG(j|x_S)-lam*c_j (EIG-Cost), but its theoretical guarantees are unclear. We provide an a-posteriori certificate decomposing the gap to the optimal hard-budget policy into a budget-slack term and a greedy-suboptimality term Del_lam. The certificate is computable when the penalized optimum is tractable; on small heterogeneous-cost instances, we verify it exactly, finding Del_lam near zero across 4,000 solved cases. On the large clinical task, Del_lam is not computable, so the decomposition is diagnostic rather than a numerical guarantee. Our analysis identifies EIG-Cost as one-step greedy optimization of the Lagrangian relaxation of expected-cost AFA, with lam as its dual multiplier. For equal costs, we prove Del_lam <= exp(-gam*I(Y;X_[d])), where gam is the submodularity ratio, yielding a structural guarantee in the near-submodular regime. Supporting results establish an adaptive chain-rule identity showing that cumulative stepwise EIG optimizes transcript information, and prove that A -> I(Y;X_A) need not be submodular, even for jointly Gaussian variables. This rules out a direct Nemhauser-Wolsey-Fisher bound; naive Bayes restores submodularity. We also bound the randomization gap between hard-budget and expected-cost optima, analyze sensitivity to dual-multiplier error under local smoothness, and derive a conditional CVAE generator-shift bound given a bound on forward predictive KL. We treat this KL as a diagnostic quantity, not as a transform of training loss. Experiments cover synthetic Gaussian settings, AFA benchmarks, and clinical differential diagnosis on MIMIC-IV. On MIMIC-IV, EIG-Cost achieves macro-F1 0.180 +/- 0.012 at a $30 budget, using 6.6% of full-panel cost.
  </details>

- **[Learning Lean Feedback for Efficient Proof-Candidate Evaluation on GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21881)**  `arXiv:2610.21881`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lazar Milikic, Etienne Bamas, Emmanuel Abbe, Viktor Kunčak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training language models for formal theorem proving requires large numbers of verifier calls. Slow checks and timeouts often leave GPUs waiting and withhold rewards from potentially correct proofs. We train neural evaluators to predict Lean 4 verdicts and compiler feedback on GPUs using 4.84M labelled programs. We compare two validity classifiers with a feedback model that annotates proof attempts with predicted goal states and error messages. A two-stage evaluator that screens candidates with a classifier and scores its positive predictions with the feedback model reaches 87.8% accuracy in predicting whether Lean accepts a candidate proof, evaluated on a 68,932-program benchmark collected from public provers. The combined evaluator processes 3.5 candidates per second on one H100, 4.2x the throughput of our 64-worker Lean baseline with environment reuse. Finally, we combine our evaluator with Lean verdicts to obtain a hybrid reward system that shortens GRPO training steps by approximately 31%. After one GRPO epoch with hybrid rewards, the prover achieves nearly the same miniF2F pass@1 as with Lean-only rewards (62.2% versus 62.4%) and a pass@32 of 72.4% versus 71.3%.
  </details>

- **[Lethe: Link Inference Attacks For Evaluation of Edge Unlearning Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21869)**  `arXiv:2610.21869`  `cs.LG`  
  _Andrea D'Angelo, Francesco Gullo, Davide Mottin, Sayan Ranu, Giovanni Stilo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Unlearning (GU) aims to remove the influence of specific training data from a Graph Neural Network without retraining anew. While most methods address removal of nodes and features, Edge Unlearning (EU), aiming at forgetting edges, has recently attracted dedicated attention as a standalone problem due to the hardness of completely removing the impact of an edge in the model. Despite this, no dedicated benchmark for EU exists besides isolated evaluations on feature-rich datasets such as Cora and Citeseer where GNNs exploit node features rather than graph structure. As such, we show two systematic limitations in existing evaluations: dedicated EU methods can be slower than retraining anew, and accuracy metrics are uninformative due to the negligible impact of edge removal. To address this gap, we introduce Lethe, the first comprehensive benchmark for Edge Unlearning, featuring 15 methods and 8 datasets. Our benchmark emphasizes large graphs, introduces evaluation tasks of varying difficulty, and proposes empirical measures based on link inference attacks rather than proxy accuracy metrics. We provide a comprehensive empirical analysis, demonstrate the shortcomings of current evaluations, and release Lethe as a reproducible, extensible benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Physics-Aware Distillation of a Grid Foundation Model for Real-Time Edge Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21833)**  `arXiv:2610.21833`  `cs.LG`  
  _Vlad Alexandru Sănduleac, Shahariya Rasheed Rayamarakkarveettil Puthanpurayil, Noman Khan, Remus Teodorescu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Grid foundation models (GridFMs) are graph transformers pretrained on a masked power-flow objective, and evaluate power flow orders of magnitude faster than an iterative solver. Their size, however, targets data-centre inference, whereas low-inertia operation increasingly asks for inference at the edge, next to the measurement, at batch size one and within a single synchrophasor (PMU) reporting frame. We distil a 20.1M-parameter, 12-layer GridFM teacher into a 0.62M-parameter, 6-layer student, a $32.5\times$ parameter reduction at half the message-passing depth, using an objective that combines the masked power-flow loss with output, physics-residual, latent-state and attention transfer. The physics-residual term, which matches the teacher's power-residual profile, has not previously been applied to typed power-grid graphs. We evaluate on a renewable variant of the IEEE 118-bus benchmark grid in which 26 of the conventional (synchronous) generators are replaced by inverter-based renewables with battery flexibility and standardised reactive-power limits (IEEE 1547), giving $97\,529$ physically valid (AC-OPF-feasible) operating scenarios, and we ablate all eight combinations of the transfer terms, reporting accuracy against ground truth and fidelity to the teacher separately. Distillation lowers student error on all four predicted quantities, by up to $48\%$ on active-power dispatch, and every distilled student is more accurate than its teacher on reactive-power dispatch. The gain is largely carried by the output matching term. The physics-residual term, in the layer-aggregated form used here, is consistently harmful, which we attribute to the spatial averaging it applies to a bus-level residual field. The resulting student is $32.5\times$ smaller in parameter count and has half the message-passing depth, making it a candidate for subsequent edge-device benchmarking.
  </details>

- **[Q($γ_H$): Entropy-Guided Horizon Mixing in Multi-Discount Q-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21764)**  `arXiv:2610.21764`  `cs.LG`  
  _Arwa Albelaihi, Abdulrahman Altahhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fixed discount factor in Q-learning imposes a single planning horizon throughout training, despite the differing demands of early exploration and long-term credit assignment. We propose Q($\gamma_H$), a multi-horizon Q-learning framework that maintains multiple discount-specific heads and adaptively combines them at control time using an entropy-derived confidence signal. The resulting state-dependent mixing weights shift control from shorter- to longer-horizon heads as policy confidence increases, enabling adaptive horizon mixing without modifying individual Bellman updates. Across classical control and deep reinforcement-learning benchmarks, Q($\gamma_H$) shows its strongest improvements on delayed-credit tasks relative to strong fixed-discount baselines. By decoupling horizon adaptation from the underlying learning updates, Q($\gamma_H$) provides a lightweight framework for adaptive planning-horizon control in reinforcement learning, avoiding the need for manual discount-factor selection while remaining compatible with standard value-learning methods.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Federated Echo State Networks for Time-Series Classification via Smooth-L1 Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21742)**  `arXiv:2610.21742`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gengcheng Lyu, Manuel Röder, Frank-Michael Schleif_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Echo State Networks (ESNs), as lightweight implementations of reservoir computing, provide efficient solutions for temporal data processing in resource-constrained environments. While standard ESNs offer computational efficiency, their dense readout layers inflate the stored model and the per-sample inference cost. This paper introduces a Smooth-L1 regularized ESN (SL1ESN) that promotes sparsity in the readout layer while preserving differentiability for gradient-based optimization. The method employs a twice-differentiable Smooth-L1 approximation compatible with Newton's method, which we exploit both for centralized training and for a second-order (Newton-step) federated aggregation scheme. Under a single fixed shrinkage threshold shared by every dataset and both settings, experiments on seven time-series classification benchmarks show that SL1ESN learns readouts that are $77$--$95\%$ sparse in the centralized setting and $75$--$95\%$ sparse in the federated one, while staying within $0.7$ accuracy points of the strongest dense baseline on five of the seven benchmarks in the centralized setting and within $1.6$ points on the same five in the federated one, and the second-order federated update converges in only a few rounds. The resulting sparsity compresses the stored model, the per-sample inference cost, and the server-to-client broadcast by a comparable factor. These results demonstrate the practical value of sparse readouts for edge computing and IoT deployments.
  </details>

- **[From Perception to Prediction and Intervention: Benchmarking Proactive Video Assistants in Real-Life Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21568)**  `arXiv:2610.21568`  `cs.LG`  
  _Boyu Cui, Junhao Luo, Zhihao Wang, Linghan Chen, Zejun Li, Jiwen Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal large language models have advanced video understanding, but existing benchmarks mainly evaluate whether models can interpret observed visual content. In real-world scenarios, assistants must additionally anticipate what assistance is needed and decide when to provide it. To address this gap, we introduce \textsc{P$^3$IBench}, a benchmark for proactive video understanding in real-life streaming scenarios. It evaluates three complementary capabilities: daily perception, risk prediction and intervention. We benchmark 12 representative open-source and proprietary video-language models, including streaming and adapted non-streaming systems. Our evaluation shows that current models struggle to make well-calibrated response decisions. Finally, we construct additional proactive training data and train \textsc{P$^3$I-Assistant-4B}, achieving improvements on several tasks, though gains remain uneven across capabilities. Together, these findings show that effective proactive assistance requires not only visual understanding, but also precise response timing and appropriate restraint.
  </details>

- **[A Quantum Machine Learning Approach to Content-Based Media Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21562)**  `arXiv:2610.21562`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mina Abbaszadeh, Ouissal Moumou, Anna Pearson, Bob Coecke, Rob Cooper, Alessandro Piscopo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We use quantum machine learning techniques to develop a content-based media recommender system and evaluate its performance on TV programmes using textual descriptions from one week of BBC iPlayer data. Grounded in the Quantum Natural Language Processing framework (QNLP), programme synopses are mapped to variational quantum circuits using their semantic structure. The parameters of these circuits are trained via a supervised pairwise similarity learning task.We benchmark the quantum recommender against a classical BiLSTM baseline trained on the same dataset and objective. While both models achieve comparable performance on the similarity learning task, the quantum model requires significantly fewer parameters (47k vs. 17.8M). When the BiLSTM is constrained to a matching parameter budget, its performance degrades substantially (by 3.63 percentage points in Mean Average Precision (MAP)), indicating that the structured quantum representation provides a highly parameter-efficient alternative in this setting. We demonstrate the feasibility of executing the trained models on quantum hardware by deploying the circuits on Quantinuum's System Model H2 (and accompanying emulator) using a SWAP-test-based protocol. The results confirm that the similarity signal is preserved under realistic, noisy conditions. Taken together, these findings highlight the potential of variational quantum circuits for recommendation tasks and represent a practical step toward their real-world deployment.
  </details>

- **[RDL-Bench: A Benchmark for Task-Aware Question Answering over Relational Databases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21382)**  `arXiv:2610.21382`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yao Cheng, Siqiang Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Relational databases (RDBs) remain the primary infrastructure for storing and managing structured data across enterprise and scientific domains. Natural-language (NL) interfaces to RDBs aim to let users ask questions without writing SQL, but real user needs go beyond SQL-style lookup: some questions require reasoning over the observable database state, while others require predictive inference from historical relational records under temporal constraints. Existing benchmarks address these goals in isolation. To address this gap, we introduce RDL-Bench, a benchmark for task-aware question answering (QA) over real RDBs. RDL-Bench organizes diverse user questions into reasoning-oriented questions, whose answers are determined by the current database state, and prediction-oriented questions, whose answers depend on future outcomes under temporal constraints. Each instance contains an NL question, task metadata, schema information, temporal context, either executable evidence or target-construction logic, and denotation-level ground truth. RDL-Bench supports two evaluation settings: native solvers under their intended capabilities, and task-aware modular systems where a router dispatches each question to an appropriate execution module.
  </details>

- **[Same Target, Different Origins: Aligning Latent Rollout for Consistent PDE Emulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21370)**  `arXiv:2610.21370`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xinying Li, Jing Xu, Wenjun Wang, Limei Han, Fenglei Cao, Ruoxi Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural PDE solvers commonly generate multi-step predictions through autoregressive rollout, recursively applying a learned evolution operator and feeding each prediction back as input for the next step. Approximation errors can therefore propagate through the rollout, causing predictions at a shared target time to depend on their temporal origins and rollout depths. For a well-posed deterministic PDE flow, however, states at different times along the same physical trajectory should, under exact evolution, reach the same target state. To this end, we propose Align Latent Rollout (ALR), a regularization approach that promotes consistency across rollout origins through the neural PDE solver's internal representations. A second-order Taylor analysis of latent discrepancy propagation motivates two objectives: zeroth-order latent state consistency, which aligns representations at a shared target time, and first-order latent dynamics consistency, which aligns local evolution responses to shared perturbations. ALR can be integrated into different neural PDE solvers by regularizing suitable internal representations, without modifying their backbone architectures. Experiments across multiple PDE benchmarks and neural-operator backbones show that ALR improves prediction accuracy while reducing shared-target discrepancies across rollout origins.
  </details>

- **[Spectrum Is Not Enough: A Random-Matrix Account of Data-Free Preconditioning for Differentially Private Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21312)**  `arXiv:2610.21312`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shilin Zhang, Kangning Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data-free preconditioning builds the Kronecker factors of KFAC from synthetic probes instead of private data. DP-KFC validates a probe by matching the eigenvalue decay of the private factors; we turn this criterion into a random-matrix account of what a probe can reconstruct. A single dichotomy organizes it: in the factorization $ A \otimes G $, the error-signal factor $ G $ and the architecture-determined bulk of the activation factor $ A $ are reconstructible without data, while only the data-induced spikes of $ A $ are not. We prove the $ G $ half is data-insensitive up to an explicit error-energy bound, with zero violations over a full training trajectory across four modalities and a natural-image benchmark. We locate the failure boundary at a Baik-Ben Arous-Péché threshold: a bulk-only probe suffers an exact damage $s^2/(1-s^2)$ from a data-induced spike, which becomes invisible once it exceeds $(d/n)^{1/4}$, confirmed on an injected-spike surrogate. Holding the probe spectrum fixed and varying only its eigenbasis, we show that spectral-shape matching is neither sufficient nor the operative object: a probe with a near-exact matched spectrum (log-ratio dispersion $0.02$) still inflates the residual $2 \times $-$90 \times $ when its eigenvectors are random. A closed-form whitened-volume law explains this and predicts the within-spectrum ordering of the critical volume, though not its cross-dataset magnitude. Downstream, per-layer tuning does not transfer to accuracy and the headroom is a small oracle-minus-white gap. Under a privacy mechanism the source ordering survives while learning does, then inverts at a noise cross-over where the flat probe overtakes the matched ones.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Learning from Label Proportions via Moment Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21262)**  `arXiv:2610.21262`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tianhao Ma, Wei Wang, Yivan Zhang, Dong-Dong Wu, Takashi Ishida, Gang Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning from label proportions (LLP) is a weakly supervised setting where training data are grouped into bags with only bag-level label proportions observed, and the goal is to learn a classifier that predicts labels for individual instances. Recently, a large body of methods has emerged; however, most of them rely on the assumption that instance-label pairs are sampled i.i.d. and randomly assembled into bags, an assumption often violated in real-world scenarios. Our experimental results indicate that these methods perform suboptimally under non-random settings. In this work, we adopt a more realistic assumption: bags are sampled i.i.d., and instance labels are conditionally independent given the instances. Under this setting, we show that the label counts follow a Poisson multinomial distribution. Motivated by this observation, we propose LLP via moment matching (LLP-MM), a simple yet effective approach which leverages multi-order factorial moments as training objectives, encouraging the classifier predictions to match these moments and thereby more fully exploit the underlying statistical structure of the data. Extensive experiments on benchmark datasets under various bag construction strategies demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach while maintaining high computational efficiency. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Resisting Harmful Instructions through Training-Free Weight Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21189)**  `arXiv:2610.21189`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ali Ebrahimpour-Boroojeny, Xiaocong Yang, Samuel Schapiro, Zahra Borhani, Francisco R. Ortega, Yujia Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-following language models can comply with requests that conflict with intended behaviors and safety alignment. We introduce a training-free weight-editing approach for reducing compliance with targeted undesirable instructions. Our method needs only forward passes on a small set of prompt pairs per target behavior, and because the edit is stored in the model's weights, changing or deleting instructions in the prompt cannot undo the weight modification. We evaluate the approach on two target harmful behaviors across five instruction-tuned models: instructed falsehood and instructed sexism. On two instructed-lying benchmarks, it improves mean honest response rates by 6.2 and 4.6 percentage points over the strongest alternative, significantly on three of the five models and within noise on the other two. In a controlled study of seven prompt formats, it maintains higher honest-response rates than single-layer activation steering when orders to lie arrive in single-turn formats not used to construct the edit. For instructed sexism, it reduces the mean sexist-response rate from 57.9% to 24.3%; combining the edit with a fairness prompt reduces it further to 11.9%. These results establish our contrast-based weight editing approach as an effective training-free intervention in two distinct behavioral domains, supporting its use both independently and alongside prompt-based safeguards.
  </details>

- **[Predicting Live Birth After Fresh Embryo Transfer in IVF/ICSI: Differential ICM/Trophectoderm Morphology Encoding and a Hybrid Attention--Logistic Regression Ensemble](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21134)**  `arXiv:2610.21134`  `cs.LG`  
  _Abdul Raheem Shahzad, Jinxin Chen, Ming Zhang, Juan Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting live birth before fresh embryo transfer could support shared decision-making in IVF/ICSI, but routine clinical data are small, heterogeneous, and contain pathway-dependent variables prone to outcome leakage. We aimed to construct a curated single-centre IVF/ICSI dataset and develop a prediction model suited to small tabular clinical data. \textit{Materials and Methods:} We assembled 616 fresh embryo-transfer cycles (251 live birth, 365 non-live birth) with clinical, laboratory, and embryological predictors, and embryo morphology encoded with a novel differential weighting of the inner cell mass and trophectoderm. After KNN imputation, robust scaling, and selection of 50 features, we developed a Hybrid Attention Network (HAN), a learnable feature-attention gate followed by a compact multilayer perceptron, and a HAN + logistic-regression (LR) ensemble with a validation-tuned weight. Models were benchmarked against classical machine learning, TabPFN, and text-serialized language models on a held-out test set; AUC-ROC was the primary metric. \textit{Results:} The HAN + LR ensemble achieved the highest test AUC-ROC (0.710; accuracy 0.710, F1 0.630), ahead of the HAN backbone alone (0.700), logistic regression (0.667), the TabPFN + LR hybrid (0.666), and TabPFN (0.650). Text-serialized language models performed near chance (AUC $\le$ 0.61). The differentially weighted trophectoderm feature ranked highest by consensus importance across models. \textit{Discussion:} A compact attention network combined with a linear model outperformed both components and strong foundation-model baselines on a small clinical cohort. \textit{Conclusion:} The curated dataset and HAN + LR ensemble offer a transparent, reproducible basis for IVF/ICSI live-birth prediction; external validation is the next step.
  </details>

- **[CausalEvo: Causal Skill Self-Evolution for Health LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21016)**  `arXiv:2610.21016`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuchen Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents for health can accumulate reusable textual skills from task feedback, such as calculation criteria, measurement time points, and formula units. Deciding which skills to retain is consequential: useful rules improve later answers, while faulty generalizations propagate errors. We propose CausalEvo, a method for causal skill self-evolution that estimates the relative effects of deliberately changed skill sets while holding the model weights, feedback cases, and rule count fixed. At each evolution step, a frozen LLM proposes candidate rules, which are pooled with the incumbent skills so that the current skill set remains a feasible choice. Paired skill replacements against shared background rules are scored on the same feedback cases; least squares converts the score differences into relative skill values, and the top-ranked rules are retained, favoring incumbents on ties. A decision-loss bound separates candidate support, approximation, and estimation errors. On six MedCalc-Bench-Verified tasks with 120 held-out cases, CausalEvo achieves 60.00% accuracy, compared with 55.83% for SkillOpt and 51.67% without skills. Across the recorded training configurations, which use different optimization budgets, it consumes 55.6% fewer training tokens than SkillOpt. Gains concentrate in particular calculations alongside regressions on others, and paired accuracy intervals include zero. CausalEvo turns task feedback into controlled decisions about skill retention and replacement. Project repository:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Imperfect Is Perfect: Learned Column Prefilling for Branch-Price-and-Cut](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21001)**  `arXiv:2610.21001`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haoran Liu, Yu Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Column generation folklore warns that initial columns hurt: a warm start may bias the dual and slow the root of branch-price-and-cut. We show the opposite once the columns are imperfect in the right way. Preloading the support of the optimal root LP, the best columns one could know in advance, fails to finish on half of our hardest instances; a pool of ordinary routes that need not belong to any optimal solution, held outside the master and priced at the current smoothed dual, speeds the root up. Because the columns need not be optimal, producing them becomes a learning task: a neural edge scorer guides a constructive generator that builds the pool in seconds. A second network predicts the initial center of Wentges smoothing from the instance file, and with it the direction of the smoothed pricing queries; the solver's own updates run unchanged. With prefill and center in place, exact pricing dominates root pricing time, so we rebuild bucket-graph labeling around its dominance test for the GPU and certify on the CPU. Inside RouteOpt, the state-of-the-art exact solver for capacitated vehicle routing, the pipeline reaches the same certified root bound in 13.2% of vanilla RouteOpt's root pricing time over 95 instances with 300 to 1000 customers, prefill alone in 26.1%, and 17.5 times faster in the best case. In an exact branch-price-and-cut solver for unrelated parallel machine scheduling, prefill with smoothing and the learned center certifies all 25 benchmark instances, 12 times faster than the solver's own column generation, which certifies 20 of them.
  </details>

- **[Sliced Wasserstein Sketches with Error Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20788)**  `arXiv:2610.20788`  `cs.LG`  
  _Elaheh Akbari, James Liu, Keaton Hamm, Soheil Kolouri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compact representations that support geometrically meaningful comparisons are valuable for learning from probability distributions. We study optimal, free-weight $M$-atom quantization of one-dimensional probability measures in quadratic Wasserstein distance and extend it to higher dimensions via slicing. Using $L$ shared projections yields a sketch with $O(LM)$ comparison time. Our main result is an exact decomposition of squared distance error into residual and partition-mismatch terms, yielding computable certificates for arbitrary centroid sketches. For optimal sketches, a finite $(2+\delta)$ moment for some $\delta>0$ guarantees $O(M^{-1})$ error in the finite-direction sliced distance, while equal-mass sketches can converge more slowly. Each slice preserves its mean, while storing residual energies enables covariance recovery from sufficiently many generic directions. Experiments on document, point-cloud, and flow-cytometry benchmarks show that optimal sketches substantially reduce storage while preserving the classification performance of uncompressed sliced distances. They also improve generalization from few labelled subjects and support end-to-end learning as compact pooling layers.
  </details>

- **[Quantile-Guided Diffusion for Probabilistic Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20739)**  `arXiv:2610.20739`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shadi Omari, Bashar Khoury, Assaf Schuster_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantile forecasts summarize uncertainty at each future time step, while diffusion forecasters represent it through sampled future trajectories. We study how predicted quantiles can serve as distributional controls for diffusion forecasting. Specifically, we investigate how the mechanism of quantile integration affects its effectiveness in guiding a pretrained diffusion forecaster. We find that residual control throughout the denoising network yields the most consistent improvements. We instantiate this approach as Control-TS, which learns a quantile-control pathway while keeping the pretrained backbone frozen. Across eight benchmarks, Control-TS consistently improves the backbone's CRPS, achieves competitive performance against state-of-the-art forecasting models, and retains its gains in capacity-matched comparisons. Further analyses examine how these gains depend on quantile granularity, predictor choice, and forecast quality. Finally, we introduce the Conditional Linear Predictive Score (CLPS), which complements distributional metrics by evaluating the downstream forecasting utility of generated futures. Together, these findings support predicted quantiles as an effective interface between explicit uncertainty estimates and diffusion-based trajectory generation.
  </details>

- **[In-Context Batch Black-box Optimization via Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20138)**  `arXiv:2610.20138`  `cs.LG`  
  _Quoc-Anh Hoang Nguyen, Xinyu Zhang, Daolang Huang, Julien Martinelli, Samuel Kaski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Batch black-box optimization is important for expensive systems where many candidates can be evaluated in parallel, yet only a few rounds of evaluation are affordable. In this regime, effective batch construction must identify multiple promising regions while avoiding redundant evaluations, and conventional Bayesian optimization can require increasingly difficult batch-selection procedures as the batch size grows. We introduce Flow Matching for Thompson-Sampling Refinement (FMTR), an amortized approach that learns offline a continuous, history-conditioned proposal distribution from which variable-size batches can be sampled. FMTR is trained offline by conditional flow matching on Thompson-sampling argmaxes refined toward high-value regions via a horizon-dependent Langevin pushforward map, adapting the exploration-exploitation tradeoff over the optimization horizon. At test time, the model parameters remain fixed and batch generation requires neither task-specific surrogate refitting nor numerical acquisition optimization. FMTR achieves strong performance against batch BO and in-context baselines on synthetic and real-world benchmarks, while substantially reducing the computational cost of batch selection.
  </details>

- **[Exhaustive Canonical Skeletons Generation: A Formal Dataset for Decomposable Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20108)**  `arXiv:2610.20108`  `cs.LG`  
  _Giorgio Morales, Anikó Ekárt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural symbolic regression (SR) models require diverse datasets of mathematical expressions for robust training. However, existing datasets rely predominantly on stochastic sampling methods, which suffer from high structural redundancy and incomplete functional coverage. To address this, we introduce an exhaustive dataset of univariate canonical skeletons generated via a deterministic constrained context-free grammar. A canonical skeleton acts as a generic structural representation that subsumes more specific functional forms, eliminating redundant expressions and avoiding repeated computational efforts. Although our dataset consists of univariate skeletons, it is designed to support the decomposable SR paradigm to solve multivariate SR problems without assuming linear separability. Furthermore, we pair this dataset with a sampling strategy that generates clean numerical data without triggering numerical instabilities or relying on artificial protected operators. Canonicalizing two existing neural SR datasets reveals that our dataset is substantially more compact, eliminates structural repetitions, and achieves a uniform operator distribution free of simplicity bias. Finally, we establish a benchmark of 150 SR problems across three tiers, on which state-of-the-art SR methods are shown to struggle significantly.
  </details>

- **[TimeRSI: Towards Self-Improved Agentic Deployment for Time-Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19725)**  `arXiv:2610.19725`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haoxin Liu, Deqing Fu, Lucheng Fu, Yiqiao Jin, Rushi Qiang, Harshavardhan Kamarthi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series foundation models provide strong zero-shot forecast, but deployment for real-world applications still requires task-specific designs for model inputs and outputs. Direct inference can leave domain knowledge and recurring forecast errors unaddressed, while tailored pipelines require substantial human expertise and effort. We introduce TimeRSI, a self-improving agentic deployment system around frozen forecasting models. A coding agent translates task descriptions into executable pipeline changes. It tests each revision on historical forecasting windows and uses observed errors to guide subsequent revisions. During deployment, an adaptive selector chooses among the resulting pipelines using completed forecasts under similar conditions. Across 198 tasks on TIME and fev-bench, TimeRSI outperforms all submissions on six point and probabilistic forecasting metrics. Controlled comparisons support the value of feedback-guided revision over non-iterative pipeline search. Further experiments verify the generalization with different coding agents and forecasting models. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[THEIA: A Multimodal Dataset and Benchmark for Vision-Language Analysis of Layout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35035)**  `arXiv:2609.35035`  `cs.LG`  
  _Giuseppe Chiari, Michele Piccoli, Federico Viola, Davide Zoni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The integration of artificial intelligence into computer-aided design frameworks has sparked a shift in the design of analog integrated circuits (ICs), transitioning the field from using manual and algorithmic-based solutions to adopting automated and intelligent paradigms. In this scenario, the GDSII file represents the industry-standard database containing the ultimate and most accurate source of information of the analog circuit, encapsulating the complex physical geometries and parasitic realities that define tape out performance. This paper proposes THEIA, a novel dataset containing thousands of layout images paired with question-answer conversations, along with a benchmark that employs a fine-tuned vision-language model (VLM) to analyze GDSII files of analog circuits, enabling designers to interact with and query physical layouts as intuitive, meaningful entities. Experimental results using thousands of analog designs across five realistic tasks demonstrate that the proposed fine-tuned VLM outperforms state-of-the-art general-purpose VLMs by a significant margin (up to 73%), highlighting a fundamental gap between general-purpose multimodal reasoning and domain-specific layout understanding.
  </details>

- **[The Safety Relay in Roleplay Jailbreaks: A Component-Resolved Causal Analysis of Harm Recognition and Refusal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30585)**  `arXiv:2608.30585`  `cs.LG`  
  _Md Mokarram Chowdhury, Ernie Chang, Yang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are trained to follow instructions while refusing harmful requests. Jailbreaks exploit this balance to elicit content a model would ordinarily reject. Roleplay jailbreaks are especially concerning: ordinary instructions defining a persona, scenario, and task can induce compliance while the harmful request remains plainly visible. We investigate which components drive this reversal and how they affect the computations leading to the response. Across two benchmarks, three model families, and four authored wrappers, we compare matched harmful and benign requests under controlled combinations of these components. We trace internal harmful--benign contrasts at the request and where generation begins, then evaluate selected internal interventions on held-out requests. A focused study examines influential attention heads and feed-forward computations, testing their functions and downstream connections.Our analysis yields three findings. (1) Successful attacks retain the measured harmful--benign distinction at the request, while its refusal-associated expression weakens where the answer begins, a pattern we call safety-relay attenuation. (2) The contributions of roleplay components depend on the other components present. (3) Reversing selected roleplay-associated internal changes restores refusal, demonstrating their causal influence. Together, these findings connect ordinary roleplay instructions to internal changes in refusal and motivate safeguards that preserve the influence of harm-related information on the response.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Modal, Multi-Temporal Geospatial Foundation Models Improve Global Above-Ground Biomass Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04792)**  `arXiv:2608.04792`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ghjulia Sialelli, Linus Scheibenreif, Jan Dirk Wegner, Konrad Schindler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
