For edge deployment, visual token compression, W4A8 quantization, and hardware-software co-optimization enable on-device inference of the 4B variant on the M100, reducing prefill latency from 400 ms to 188 ms.Project Page:this https URLCode Repository:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Imagine Before You Predict: Interleaved Latent Visual Reasoning for Video Event Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05769)**  `arXiv:2606.05769`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tianxiang Jiang, Linquan Wu, Sheng Xia, Songze Li, Ziang Yan, Haoyu Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video event prediction (VEP) requires models to infer unobserved future states from partial video evidence. Existing video MLLMs usually verbalize intermediate future reasoning in text space: once visual evidence is verbalized, fine-grained motion, geometry, and interaction cues can be lost, leading to plausible but visually ungrounded hallucinations. We introduce Future-L1, an interleaved latent visual reasoning framework that lets an MLLM alternate between language tokens and continuous latent visual spans during autoregressive decoding. To train this capability, we construct Future-L1-50K by selecting examples where future visual hints help prediction and align latent states to future-frame embeddings, then further optimize sampled latent trajectories with LA-DAPO, a latent-aware RL objective with outcome-contrastive and temporal-diversity rewards. Future-L1 achieves new state-of-the-art results on both benchmarks: on FutureBench, it improves Qwen3-VL-8B from 61.0 to 85.4 and exceeds the previous best Video-CoE by 10.4 points; on TwiFF-Bench, it improves the average score from 2.44 to 3.04. These results suggest that future-oriented video reasoning benefits from preserving intermediate visual semantics in latent space rather than translating every reasoning step into text.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Metrics for Synthetic Data Utility: A Cross-Domain Study of Appearance and Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22467)**  `arXiv:2605.22467`  `cs.CV`  
  _Patryk Bartkowiak, Dominik Michels, Soren Pirk, Wojtek Palubicki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose SADGE, a quantitative similarity metric that predicts the performance of synthetic image datasets for common computer vision tasks without downstream model training. Estimating whether a synthetic dataset will lead to a model that performs well on real-world data remains a bottleneck in model development. Existing evaluation metrics (e.g., PSNR, FID, CLIP) primarily measure semantic alignment between real and synthetic images (Appearance Similarity Score). Less commonly, structural similarity between images is considered to assess the domain gap (Geometric Similarity Score). However, to the best of our knowledge there exists no studies that evaluate which similarity metric is the best downstream predictor for a given synthetic dataset. In this paper, we show over a wide variety of different synthetic datasets and downstream tasks that neither appearance nor geometry alone can reliably predict downstream performance; rather, it is their non-linear interplay that dictates synthetic data utility. Specifically, we measure how commonly used Appearance and Geometric Similarity metrics computed between synthetic and real images correlate with downstream performance in object detection, semantic segmentation, and pose estimation. Across five public synthetic-to-real benchmark families and 15 dataset-level variants (79k image pairs), SADGE achieves the strongest association with downstream transfer performance under both linear and rank-based criteria, reaching Pearson r=0.88 and Spearman rho=0.77. We compute for each combination of geometry-based methods and appearance-based approaches SADGE scores across all benchmark families. The best configuration is obtained by fusing DINOv3 appearance similarity with MASt3R geometric consistency through a constrained bilinear interaction, outperforming both the strongest geometry-only baseline and the strongest appearance-only baseline .
  </details>

- **[EPIC-Bench: A Perception-Centric Benchmark for Fine-Grained Embodied Visual Grounding in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17070)**  `arXiv:2605.17070`  `cs.CV`  
  _Haozhe Shan, Xiancong Ren, Han Dong, Yingji Zhang, Haoyuan Shi, Jiayu Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While large vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly adopted as the perceptual backbone for embodied agents, their fundamental vision-language grounding (VLG) capability in embodied scenarios remains unexplored. We present \textbf{EPIC-Bench}, \textbf{E}mbodied \textbf{P}ercept\textbf{I}on Ben\textbf{C}hmark, a fine-grained grounding benchmark designed to systematically evaluate the VLG capabilities of VLMs in real-world embodied environments. EPIC-Bench comprises 6.6k meticulously human-annotated tuples (Image, Text, Mask) spanning three core stages of the embodied interaction pipeline: Target Localization, Navigation, and Manipulation. With 23 fine-grained tasks, multi-dimensional settings and evaluation over 80 leading VLMs, EPIC-Bench provides a diagnostic analysis.
  </details>

- **[SR-Prominence: A Crowdsourced Protocol and Dataset Suite for Perceptually-Weighted Super-Resolution Artifact Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14847)**  `arXiv:2605.14847`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ivan Molodetskikh, Kirill Malyshev, Mark Mirgaleev, Nikita Zagainov, Evgeney Bogatyrev, Dmitriy Vatolin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern image super-resolution methods generate detailed, visually appealing results, but they often introduce visual artifacts: unnatural patterns and texture distortions that degrade perceived quality. These defects vary widely in perceptual impact--some are barely noticeable, while others are highly disturbing--yet existing detection methods treat them equally. We propose artifact prominence as an evaluative target, defined as the fraction of viewers who judge a highlighted region to contain a noticeable artifact. We design a crowdsourced annotation protocol and construct SR-Prominence, a dataset suite containing 3,935 artifact masks from DeSRA, Open Images, Urban100, and a realistic no-ground-truth Urban100-HR setting, annotated with prominence. Re-annotating DeSRA reveals that 48.2% of its in-lab binary artifacts are not noticed by a majority of viewers. Across the suite, we audit SR artifact detectors, image-quality metrics, and SR methods. We find that classical full-reference metrics, especially SSIM and DISTS, provide surprisingly strong localized prominence signals, whereas no-reference IQA methods and specialized artifact detectors often fail to generalize across datasets and reference settings. SR-Prominence is released with an objective scoring protocol that allows new metrics to be benchmarked on our suite without further crowdsourcing. Together, the data and protocols enable SR artifact evaluation to move from binary defect presence toward perceptual impact. SR-Prominence is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[V-GIFT: Boosting Visual Instruction Tuning with Self-Supervised Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12966)**  `arXiv:2604.12966`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sophia Sirko-Galouchenko, Monika Wysoczanska, Andrei Bursuc, Nicolas Thome, Spyros Gidaris_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) perform well on many vision-language tasks but often struggle with vision-centric problems that require fine-grained visual perception. Recent evidence suggests that this limitation arises not from weak visual representations, but from under-utilization of visual information during instruction tuning, where many tasks can be partially solved using language priors alone. We propose a simple and lightweight approach that augments visual instruction tuning with a small number of visually grounded self-supervised tasks expressed as natural language instructions. By reformulating classical self-supervised pretext tasks, such as rotation prediction, color matching, and cross-view correspondence, as image-instruction-response triplets, we introduce supervision that cannot be solved without relying on visual evidence. Our approach requires no human annotations, no architectural modifications, and no additional training stages. Across multiple models, training regimes, and benchmarks, injecting only a small fraction (3-10%) of such visually grounded instructions consistently improves performance on vision-centric evaluations. Our findings highlight instruction tuning with visually grounded SSL tasks as a powerful lever for improving visual perception in MLLMs through simple adjustments to the training data distribution. Code available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PhyMix: Towards Physically Consistent Single-Image 3D Indoor Scene Generation with Implicit--Explicit Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10125)**  `arXiv:2604.10125`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dongli Wu, Jingyu Hu, Ka-Hei Hui, Xiaobao Wei, Chengwen Luo, Jianqiang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing methods for single-image 3D indoor scene generation often produce visually plausible results that violate real-world physical constraints, limiting their reliability in robotics, embodied AI, and virtual environment design. To bridge this gap, we introduce a Physics Evaluator that assesses four aspects (geometric priors, contact, stability, and deployability) through nine sub-constraints, establishing a benchmark for evaluating physical consistency. Our analysis reveals that even state-of-the-art methods produce scenes with substantial physical inconsistencies despite their visual plausibility. To address these limitations, we propose a framework that incorporates feedback from the Physics Evaluator into both training and inference to improve the physical plausibility of generated scenes. Specifically, PhyMix comprises two components: (i) implicit alignment via Scene-GRPO, a critic-free group-relative preference optimization strategy that uses physical evaluations to guide the generator toward physically plausible layouts, and (ii) explicit refinement via verifier-guided agentic repair, which translates evaluator diagnostics into executable scene-editing skills to correct residual violations at inference time. PhyMix unifies physical evaluation, preference-based alignment, and agentic inference-time correction to improve physical plausibility while retaining the observed scene content. Experiments show improvements in reconstruction quality and physical plausibility, while qualitative examples on stylized and real-world images illustrate the applicability of the framework beyond synthetic inputs.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Efficient & Effective Camera Pose Estimation Strategies for Novel View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.20428)**  `arXiv:2603.20428`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jhacson Meza, Martin R. Oswald, Torsten Sattler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Novel view synthesis (NVS) approaches such as NeRFs or 3DGS can produce photo-realistic 3D scene representation from a set of images with known extrinsic and intrinsic parameters. The necessary camera poses and calibrations are typically obtained from the images via Structure-from-Motion (SfM). Classical SfM approaches rely on local feature matches between the images to estimate both the poses and a sparse 3D model of the scene, using bundle adjustment to refine initial pose, intrinsics, and geometry estimates. In order to increase run-time efficiency, recent SfM systems forgo optimization via bundle adjustment. Instead, they train feed-forward (transformer-based) neural networks to directly regress camera parameters and the 3D structure. While orders of magnitude more efficient, such recent works produce significantly less accurate estimates. To stimulate research on developing SfM approaches that are both efficient and effective, this paper develops a benchmark focused on SfM for novel view synthesis. Using existing datasets and two simple strategies for making the reconstruction process more efficient, we show that: (1) simply using fewer features already significantly accelerates classical SfM methods while maintaining high pose accuracy. (2) using feed-forward networks to obtain initial estimates and refining them using classical SfM techniques leads to the best efficiency-effectiveness trade-off. Our benchmark and code are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Out-of-Distribution Object Detection in Street Scenes via Synthetic Outlier Exposure and Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16122)**  `arXiv:2603.16122`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sadia Ilyas, Annika Mütze, Klaus Friedrichs, Thomas Kurbiel, Matthias Rottmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-distribution (OOD) objects are often silently overlooked by object detectors, which produce no detection for them, leaving downstream modules with nothing to act on and no indication that anything was missed. A reliable object detector must detect OOD objects by localizing and correctly classifying them as OOD. Existing approaches primarily focus on OOD detection for image classification or rely on auxiliary branches, and typically do not treat in-distribution (ID) and OOD objects in a unified manner. We present SynOE-OD, a Synthetic Outlier-Exposure-based Object Detection method, which uses text-guided inpainting and an open-vocabulary object detector to generate and verify synthetic outliers offline. These are then used to fine-tune an object detector of choice with an added OOD class, which requires no auxiliary branch at inference time. On the OoDIS benchmark, SynOE-OD outperforms the strongest open-vocabulary baseline, reaching AP_50:95 of 57.6%, 29.4% and 39.2% on RoadObstacle, RoadAnomaly and Fishyscapes, against 48.5%, 22.7% and 22.7%. This state-of-the-art performance holds for both fine-tuned closed-set and open-vocabulary detectors while maintaining competitive ID performance in street scenes.
  </details>

- **[Thinking with Images as Continuous Actions: Numerical Visual Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.23959)**  `arXiv:2602.23959`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kesen Zhao, Beier Zhu, Junbao Zhou, Xingyu Zhu, Zhongqi Yue, Hanwang Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent MLLMs increasingly rely on visual chain-of-thought to perform region-grounded reasoning over images. However, existing approaches ground regions via either textified coordinates (causing modality mismatch and semantic fragmentation) or fixed-granularity patches that both limit precise region selection and often require non-trivial architectural changes. In this paper, we propose Numerical Visual Chain-of-Thought (NV-CoT), a framework that enables MLLMs to reason over images using continuous numerical coordinates. NV-CoT expands the MLLM action space from discrete vocabulary tokens to a continuous Euclidean space, allowing models to directly generate bounding-box coordinates as actions with only minimal architectural modification. The framework supports both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. In particular, we replace categorical token policies with a Gaussian (or Laplace) policy over coordinates and introduce stochasticity via reparameterized sampling, making NV-CoT fully compatible with GRPO-style policy optimization. Extensive experiments on three benchmarks against 11 representative visual reasoning baselines demonstrate that NV-CoT significantly improves localization precision and final answer accuracy, while also accelerating training convergence, validating the effectiveness of continuous-action visual reasoning in MLLMs. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AI Knows Where You Are: Exposure, Bias, and Inference in Multimodal Geolocation with KoreaGEO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03371)**  `arXiv:2506.03371`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaonan Wang, Bo Shao, Hansaem Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in vision-language models (VLMs) have enabled accurate image-based geolocation, raising serious concerns about location privacy risks in everyday social media posts. Yet, a systematic evaluation of such risks is still lacking: existing benchmarks show coarse granularity, linguistic bias, and a neglect of multimodal privacy risks. To address these gaps, we introduce KoreaGEO, the first fine-grained, multimodal, and privacy-aware benchmark for geolocation, built on Korean street views. The benchmark covers four socio-spatial clusters and nine place types with rich contextual annotations and two captioning styles that simulate real-world privacy exposure. To evaluate mainstream VLMs, we design a three-path protocol spanning image-only, functional-caption, and high-risk-caption inputs, enabling systematic analysis of localization accuracy, spatial bias, and reasoning behavior. Results show that input modality exerts a stronger influence on localization precision and privacy exposure than model scale or architecture, with high-risk captions substantially boosting accuracy. Moreover, they highlight structural prediction biases toward core cities.
  </details>

- **[What Should We Remove? Evaluating the Reliability and Costs of RLVR Data Decontamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21229)**  `arXiv:2610.21229`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Esther Xin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) math corpora are routinely screened for benchmark overlap, but the reliability and data-loss costs of cleaning policies remain underexamined. We audit sixteen open RLVR math corpora comprising 1,673,514 corpus-specific training items against six benchmarks containing 1,949 questions. We evaluate eight policies based on exact, lexical, and semantic matching using 594 independently and blindly dual-reviewed training-benchmark pairs. Calibration and held-out sets have disjoint benchmark questions and training-example families. A separate review of all 83 candidate matches for 80 sampled training items measures item-level false removal. At cosine threshold 0.80, 52 of 59 sampled removals (88%) were confirmed false removals; this is not a demonstrated population rate. No semantic threshold in our preregistered grid (0.80-0.98) meets the 90% conservative-precision target on calibration. The existing judge-adjudicated policy achieves 98.1% resolved precision and 98.2% candidate-frame recall on held-out corpora. It has zero confirmed false removals among six sampled removals, with one unresolved case; this does not establish zero risk. Within the evaluated candidate frame, this policy offers a more favorable precision-recall balance than the semantic-threshold alternatives tested. Recall does not measure contamination outside the candidate frame. We release reviewed labels, per-policy decisions, and coverage accounting to support evaluation of cleaning reliability and cost rather than overlap counts alone.
  </details>

- **[One Bias Score, Two Factors: How Mitigations Affect Option Bias in Multiple-Choice Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20993)**  `arXiv:2610.20993`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hyunjong Ok, Minjae Park, Jaeho Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reordering multiple-choice options can change a model's answer, an effect studied as selection bias (a preference for certain option letters) and as order sensitivity (changes in answers across option orderings). We connect these perspectives through the pooled selection-rate standard deviation (pooled SD), which measures how unevenly option letters are selected across questions and orderings. When every option of each question appears equally often at each position, the pooled SD is exactly the product of two label-free factors: the flip rate and the positional skew. The flip rate is the fraction of questions whose chosen option changes across orderings; the positional skew is the standard deviation of option-letter selection rates among those questions. Across 41 checkpoints and 12 benchmarks, the flip rate tracks accuracy and output uncertainty, while the positional skew tracks the estimated option-symbol prior. Prior correction for selection bias mainly reduces the positional skew. Aggregating each ordering with its reversal, a mitigation for order sensitivity, mainly reduces the flip rate. Both reduce the range of accuracy across correct-answer letters by similar amounts, but aggregation leaves fewer model-rank reversals. Applying prior correction before aggregation reduces both factors and further narrows this accuracy range. However, aggregation lowers the flip rate partly by settling questions on the same wrong option. We therefore recommend reporting both factors to distinguish mitigation effects, alongside accuracy to distinguish stability from correctness.
  </details>

- **[A Large-Scale Dataset for Multi-Class Financial News Classification in Brazilian Portuguese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20630)**  `arXiv:2610.20630`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Leomax Bandeira, Gabriel Aguiar, Dalton Cézane Gomes Valadares, Luiz Pereira, Helder Fernando de Araújo Oliveira, Gustavo Xavier, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The development of Financial Natural Language Processing (NLP) systems for Brazilian Portuguese is hindered by the scarcity of domain-specific datasets. To address this, we present a large-scale corpus of approximately 160,000 Brazilian Portuguese financial news articles collected from web sources. To establish initial benchmarks for a weakly supervised multi-class classification approach based on semantic similarity, we annotated subsets of this corpus both manually and using a Large Language Model (LLM). We evaluated three similarity-based methods -- cosine similarity, K-Nearest Neighbors (KNN), and FAISS-based nearest-neighbor search -- using multilingual semantic embeddings. Our experiments reveal that the task is highly sensitive to label quality and class distribution. On the manually labeled subset, KNN achieved the best performance (47.3% accuracy, 31.9% F1-score), closely followed by FAISS. Conversely, on the LLM-annotated subset, while accuracy reached approximately 50%, F1-scores dropped to near 20%, highlighting the negative impact of label imbalance and noisy weak annotations. These findings establish foundational baselines for a challenging task, underscoring the critical need for high-quality manual annotations. Ultimately, this dataset and our preliminary results provide a reference point for future research in Brazilian Portuguese Financial NLP.
  </details>

- **[DeepUnfolding_Agent: A Classical_Quantum Adaptive Control API](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20615)**  `arXiv:2610.20615`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shanika Iroshi Nanayakkara, Shiva Raj Pokhrel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adaptive optimization workflows in machine learning, federated learning, quantum machine learning, and hybrid classical quantum systems often embed controller logic directly inside task specific training scripts. This coupling limits reuse, reproducibility, safety validation, and fair comparison across execution environments. This paper presents DeepUnfolding Agent, a schema-driven FastAPI platform that exposes deep unfolded adaptive control as an external software service. External executors register a strategy schema, submit round-level diagnostic variables, receive schema-bounded control actions, execute one optimization round, and return feedback for controller update. The framework separates controller intelligence from domain specific execution through a strategy registry, diagnostic to state adapter, controller factory, safety guard, executor abstraction, feedback update path, SQLite persistence layer, Docker deployment, and reproducible benchmark scripts. The implementation supports classical, quantum-aware, hybrid, Qiskit Aer, and optional IBM quantum processing execution pathways. We validate the software through unit, schema, API, safety, orchestration, Quantum execution, checkpointing, and Docker tests, and demonstrate external user fixed versus adaptive workflows across finance, healthcare, genomics, transport, noisy quantum simulation, and hardware backed IBM Quantum execution. The results show that DeepUnfolding-Agent provides a reusable and traceable adaptive control layer for heterogeneous optimization workflows. Our quantum experiments are interpreted as API level quantum control and hardware portability evidence.
  </details>

- **[A Jury, Not an Electorate: Label-Free Reliability Estimation for Ensembles of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21085)**  `arXiv:2610.21085`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Alessandro Rozza, Davide Mambelli, Steven Motta, Juri Taresch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An ensemble of language models is a jury, not an electorate. The answers are testimony from witnesses of unknown reliability, and the question is not what the majority said but who is credible. Most ensemble methods assign each expert a fixed reputation, often learned from labels. We ask whether it can instead be inferred from the pattern of agreement and disagreement among the experts. We model the experts as a jury: each question has a latent true answer, each expert reports it with unknown reliability, and those reliabilities are estimated by Expectation Maximisation (EM) from unlabelled answer configurations alone. We show that its decision rule reduces to a log-odds weighted linear score over the experts' answer distributions. What changes is not how the jury votes, but how it learns whom to trust. On a 1560-question benchmark, the jury, with nine experts of up to 3B parameters, performs comparably to the same fusion rule with label-fitted weights, showing no statistically significant advantage from supervision. Instead, it outperforms the best single model with matched self-consistency and the strongest label-free alternative on the full benchmark.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Downstream QA: Rethinking How We Evaluate Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22209)**  `arXiv:2610.22209`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sebastian-Antonio Toma, Jean de Kernier, Mike Ply, Oliviu Matei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent memory benchmarks evaluate whether an agent's responses reflect correct knowledge, measuring downstream question-answering accuracy as a proxy for memory quality. We show this evaluation paradigm is incomplete: it conflates memory correctness with retrieval and generation quality, producing both false positives where correct responses mask broken memory and false negatives where incorrect responses blame correct memory for retrieval failures. We introduce FEB-30, a benchmark of 30 fact-evolution trajectories spanning 6 diagnostic categories and 3 difficulty levels, constructed via paradigm inversion: documents are generated from known fact trajectories, yielding deterministic ground truth without human annotation. Each trajectory defines how a fact should evolve across multi-author artifacts (documents, emails, conversations), enabling direct inspection of the memory store after each ingestion step. Applying FEB-30 to a representative memory system, we demonstrate that intrinsic and extrinsic evaluation diverge by an average of 16 percentage points, with 44% of trajectories exceeding meaningful divergence thresholds. Diagnostic decomposition reveals that the system correctly classifies memory operations (create, update, reinforce, supersede) only 20% of the time, a failure entirely invisible to extrinsic evaluation, and that failure modes differ systematically by category and difficulty. We release the benchmark, document generation pipeline, and scoring framework to support reproducible evaluation of agent memory systems.
  </details>

- **[Priming Base Models for Agentic Post-Training: An Open Midtraining Recipe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22019)**  `arXiv:2610.22019`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Soumye Singhal, Joseph Jennings, Ka Shun Shum, Alexander Bukharin, Khushi Bhardwaj, Zirui Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier models, general-purpose and domain-specific alike, now include a midtraining stage between pretraining and post-training, but little has been published about how this stage is built. We present an open midtraining recipe that primes base models for agentic post-training, and we show its effectiveness on two Nemotron models of different sizes. Pretraining typically runs at short context and sees almost no agent interactions, while SFT data is mostly synthetic, and very long SFT stages can hurt the model's later RL trainability. Our midtraining stage sits between the two. It trains at 256K context for hundreds of billions of tokens on a diverse mixture of natural software-engineering data like packed repositories, pull requests, commit histories, and build logs together with a small share of agentic traces. On Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, 500B midtraining tokens improve SWE-bench Verified from 60.7 to 64.5, SWE-bench Multilingual from 55.8 to 62.8, and SWE-bench Pro from 32.0 to 35.5 after identical full SFT. On the larger Nemotron 3.5 Super, 280B midtraining tokens improve DeepSWE, a harder benchmark of long-horizon tasks, from 11.7 to 15.7 and SWE-bench Verified from 72.9 to 74.3 after identical full SFT. Midtraining also improves robustness across agent harnesses on both models. We report the ablations behind the recipe and an evaluation protocol built on short fixed SFT runs, since base-model proxy metrics did not correlate reliably with post-training results.
  </details>

- **[Reliable Knowledge Acquisition for Continual Learning Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21739)**  `arXiv:2610.21739`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yixu Huang, Jiaqi W. Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As agents powered by foundation models move into open-ended environments, continual learning from experience becomes essential. For agents built on models with fixed parameters, this learning requires reusing prior task experience in context to improve performance and efficiency on future tasks. Existing memory and experience-learning systems typically focus on either retained facts or procedures, and lack a mechanism for establishing the reliability of claims extracted from the ongoing task stream. Indeed, we find that approximately 12--33\% of entries produced by representative systems harm future task performance when reused. To address these limitations, we propose \textbf{RIKA} (Reliable Knowledge Acquisition), which combines hierarchical extraction of factual, conceptual, procedural, and metacognitive knowledge with Evidence-based Knowledge Admission, which uses source evidence and subsequent experience to govern which claims enter the task agent's context, without additional validation tasks. Across six task streams from Continual Learning Bench and four model backbones, RIKA achieves the highest aggregate score on every backbone.
  </details>

- **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561)**  `arXiv:2610.21561`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sergio Burdisso, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under the MIT license atthis https URL, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.
  </details>

- **[Trajectory-Level Speculation with Semantic Verification for Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21463)**  `arXiv:2610.21463`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zilin Wang, Yinhong Liu, Chao Chen, Qun Liu, Zhijiang Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents solve tasks through long sequences of tool calls, and the prevailing design has a single strong model generate every step, although many steps are routine enough for a much smaller model. Existing ways of sharing this work keep the strong model as the generator: routers and collaborative agents decide which model handles each query or step, and speculative methods check a small model's drafts one step at a time, usually against the strong model's own action. We introduce SpecAgent, a collaboration protocol that turns the strong model from the author of each step into a reviewer of steps drafted by a weak model. The weak model executes read-only tool calls as it drafts, so that every step is grounded in real observations, and holds the first action that would modify the environment. The strong model then verifies the drafted segment in a single prefill pass, commits the longest prefix it judges semantically valid, and repairs the first rejected step. Because verification replaces autoregressive decoding with parallel prefill, the strong model decodes only short verdicts and occasional repairs rather than every step. On $\tau^2$-Bench, SpecAgent comes within one point of the strong model's success rate while roughly halving memory traffic and wall time, and on AutomationBench and VitaBench it recovers 44-75% of the quality gap while lowering both. In an edge-cloud deployment on MCP-Atlas with three cloud models, it closes 53-75% of the weak-strong quality gap while the strong model generates at most half as many tokens. These results suggest that a strong model need not author every step of an agent: reviewing grounded drafts retains most of its quality while cutting both latency and memory traffic.
  </details>

- **[System1-Arena: Which Decisions Can Be Left to System 1?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21244)**  `arXiv:2610.21244`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Tianyu Chen, Xinyang Liu, Ruyi An, Zhendong Wang, Mingyuan Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often make discrete decisions, such as flagging phishing or escalating to a human. System-1 models, such as Jev and the OpenAI Decisions API, return probabilities over options in a single forward pass, while System-2 approaches generate reasoning steps. Which decisions can System-1 models handle, and which require generative reasoning? We introduce System1-Arena, a benchmark that gives all models the same decision requests and scores their probabilities. It contains 92k questions across 105 tasks from 268 public sources, in four groups: text classification and knowledge, applied decisions, evaluation and control, and agent and action. It also includes 138 closed-loop environments, from web shopping and SWE-bench coding control to robotics, games, and driving; in real-time mode, the world evolves while a model decides. We evaluate 30 models: 7 closed LLMs, 13 open LLMs read at the answer token, and 10 System-1 models. The largest System-1 models match or exceed fast closed LLMs in non-agentic accuracy, with better-calibrated probabilities at one-quarter of the latency. A reasoning LLM is the most accurate, at about 70x the cost of a fast LLM. In closed loops, large open LLMs succeed most often (up to 32.9%); small System-1 models rarely succeed.
  </details>

- **[PremiseCheck: Efficient Fact-Checking of Human and AI Peer Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21046)**  `arXiv:2610.21046`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Dingjie Song, Zhiling Yan, Ziqian Zhang, Yuxuan Zhang, Lichao Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Peer review is under strain from the growth of submissions, and review comments written by humans or AI can misstate what a paper says. We introduce ReviewFact, a benchmark that labels such factual errors in review comments under one definition across NLP, machine learning, and the natural sciences, and that measures cost and latency alongside accuracy. A frontier LLM checks comments well but must read the whole paper for each check, while cheap decision models, which score whether a statement holds in a text, fall far short on whole comments that mix several implicit assertions. PremiseCheck is a framework that combines the two. An LLM rewrites each comment as explicit premises without reading the paper, and a decision model checks each premise against the paper. When each comment is checked on its own, PremiseCheck reaches accuracy comparable to GPT-5.5 across all three fields at about half its cost, and on NLP reviews at a quarter of its cost and less than half its latency.
  </details>

- **[CoTranslator: Reverse to Forward Chain-of-Thought Translator for Reasoning Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20828)**  `arXiv:2610.20828`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zinan Tang, Zhuoshi Pan, Kairui Chen, Ze Yan, Shaomian Zheng, Yukun Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning distillation requires chain-of-thought (CoT) data with correct answers, but obtaining such data efficiently remains challenging. Forward CoT generation (FCG) followed by rejection sampling (RS) produces verified, question-only responses, yet may require many teacher calls for difficult problems. Reverse CoT generation (RCG) is cheaper because it conditions on the known answer, but access to this answer can lead to post-hoc rationalization and a mismatch with the student's answer-free inference setting. We propose CoTranslator, which learns to translate reverse CoT responses toward the distribution of verified forward responses. Trained on a small set of paired reverse and rejection-sampled forward responses, it supports large-scale generation and verification of student training data. Across six student models evaluated on six mathematics benchmarks, CoTranslator improves average accuracy over semantic suppression by up to $5.82$ percentage points. For two 4B students, it retains $95.0\%$ of rejection sampling's average accuracy while using $35.9\%$ of its estimated compute. Without retraining, the same translator transfers to a new mathematics dataset, a different teacher, and STEM problems, improving average STEM accuracy by $4.88$ and $4.31$ percentage points for 4B and 8B students, respectively. These results show that CoTranslator offers an efficient and transferable approach to reasoning distillation.
  </details>

- **[Verdict: Encode Once, Decide Many](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20778)**  `arXiv:2610.20778`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Avdhesh Singh Chouhan, Jack Perry_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automating organizational work means answering many small typed questions about one piece of state (does this email need a reply, how urgent is it, who owns it) with probabilities good enough to act on without a human. The open typed-decision model we compare against, like a standard cross-encoder, answers each question with a separate pass over the input. We present Verdict-MQ, an open encoder that reads a case once and answers all of its questions: given identical retained state tokens after truncation (verified on every reported case), a structured attention mask makes the state encoding independent of the questions, keeps questions from influencing each other, and makes option order irrelevant by construction. On the typed-decisions benchmark, a 395M Verdict-MQ matches the soft gold labels significantly better than the released 421M Laya specialist on all five fresh seeds (KL 0.080 vs 0.117, Brier 0.045 vs 0.061), an advantage that survives per-system temperature scaling; its higher accuracy (0.801 vs 0.767) lies past the benchmark's own saturation point, so we read that margin as closer agreement with the benchmark's labelling teacher. On RACE, BoolQ and MultiRC, against a matched fine-tuned baseline it wins on MultiRC and loses on RACE and on BoolQ, where its training is unstable; we measure zero-shot references ourselves, including a closed general system (Jev) that beats every fine-tuned encoder. A controlled comparison measures what the shared state costs: giving each question its own question-aware copy of the state gains 0.9-3.5 points on RACE and MultiRC at 1.4-2.5x the per-case latency, and nothing measurable on typed decisions, with the invariance guarantees unchanged.
  </details>

- **[Amplitude-Only FFN Intervention for Tool-Structured LLM Inference: Method, Gated Evaluation Protocol, and Cross-Model Empirical Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11183)**  `arXiv:2607.11183`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sheng Xu, Zhen Chen, Junhua Wang, Boyuan Huang, Ke Jia, Jiadun Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study Amplitude Gating (AG), an inference-time intervention that masks feed-forward network (FFN) activation coordinates while keeping pretrained weights fixed. Six SwiGLU sites separate shared and gate-only inputs, expanded gate/value channels, and the FFN output. Magnitude-ranked entropy masks and a Newton-Schulz windowed ranking variant provide alternative forward paths without retraining. The corrected September collection covers three Qwen checkpoints and eight text/tool endpoints. Under frozen local text scorers, the displayed Qwen3.5-9B non-NS configurations improve two function-call endpoints by +2.61 and +2.67 percentage points (pp), and static ToolBench in/out-domain endpoints by +1.64 and +2.82 pp. Comparing the lower historical/September AG mean with the higher baseline mean leaves positive margins on these routes. This supports robustness to the two observed executions, rather than formal statistical significance. JSON gains are small and displayed L-Eval effects are negative. These endpoints measure lexical or answer/action-name agreement rather than executed-tool success. Protocol-specific offline selector and Per-Sample Fix-Harm Evaluation (PFHE) analyses complement the fixed-route results. Current experimental data do not establish a significant random-forest (RF) benefit; further experiments are needed. AG provides a task-dependent intervention surface, with causal mechanisms, equal-budget advantages, repeated-run uncertainty, and serving cost still open.
  </details>

- **[Right Answer, Wrong Tokens: Tail-Aware Credit Calibration for RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07976)**  `arXiv:2607.07976`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiuyi Lou, Zicheng Xu, Yu-Neng Chuang, Hoang Anh Duy Le, Zhaozhuo Xu, Guanchu Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although reinforcement learning (RL) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), GRPO-style methods can suffer from a positive-credit contamination failure mode. Specifically, uniform credit assignment can reinforce locally erroneous tail tokens alongside useful tokens within successful trajectories. To address this problem, we propose Tail-Aware Credit calibratiOn (TACO), which calibrates the positive credit assigned to such tokens without token-level error labels. To this end, TACO lets the policy serve as its own reference, assessing each sampled token's rarity relative to the policy's local expectations to compute a tail-risk score. Using this score as a proxy for potential local errors, TACO softly reduces positive credit for high tail-risk tokens while retaining nonzero weights, limiting their reinforcement without eliminating opportunities to learn useful rare patterns. Experiments across four LLMs and eight reasoning benchmarks show that TACO consistently improves over GRPO-style baselines. Notably, TACO also improves training stability, supporting sustained performance gains in long-horizon RL. The source code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[LEAP: Learned Block-wise Evidence Retrieval for Long Audio-Video Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39938)**  `arXiv:2609.39938`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Juyi Lin, Zhiqiang Lao, Jiali Cui, Lin Zhao, Pu Zhao, Dichang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hour-scale audio-visual question answering is constrained by a context dilemma: dense whole-recording encoding rapidly exhausts context limits, whereas uniform temporal compression severely dilutes fine-grained acoustic and visual evidence. We introduce LEAP, a framework where the model retrieves its own evidence without placing the whole recording in one context. LEAP divides a recording into fixed-duration blocks, applying a lightweight localization pass to each block to score short candidate windows. The highest-ranked windows are pooled and re-encoded in a single bounded answer pass. Consequently, the answer input and peak context remain independent of the recording duration. By decoupling evidence localization from reasoning, our framework can localize candidate temporal windows over pre-computed transcripts without decoding media frames, while preserving fine-grained visual and non-speech evidence by routing the final answering pass over raw audio-visual streams. LEAP trains both stages: a localization LoRA improves the selected windows, and an answer LoRA improves the answers read from the same windows. The block grid natively supports causal queries, enabling LEAP to support streaming inference without streaming-specific training. Across several AVQA benchmarks, LEAP improves over the Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B baseline by 4.5-16.8%, and transfers to a second omni-modal backbone, MiniCPM-o 4.5, surpassing its published results by 3.1-13.0%.
  </details>

- **[AI Consilium: a Benchmark of LLM Clinical Multidisciplinary Second Opinions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22298)**  `arXiv:2610.22298`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alexey A. Bokov, Ivan I. Panin, Nikolay B. Nechaev, Mikhail Yu. Valkov, Valeria Yu. Chernina, Natalia Gombolevskaya, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate differential diagnoses for complex cases, but a useful second opinion must also be factually grounded, clinically coherent, actionable, and safe. We developed a multidimensional benchmark using 300 diagnostically resolved NEJM--MedQA cases: 110 for physician reference ratings and Judge calibration and 190 for held-out evaluation. Eight single-model pipelines generated independent role-specific opinions and interactive-consilium reports under free-form and structured clinical-reasoning conditions. A physician-calibrated LLM Judge evaluated 3,040 final reports and the corresponding structured primary-role outputs. Model identity separated report quality more strongly than response format. Structured final reports had higher overall mean scores in seven of eight model pipelines, with their clearest advantage in management (2.740 vs. 2.436). Compared with the structured pre-consilium primary-role opinion, the complete structured synthesis had higher pooled scores across all five shared criteria, with the largest increases in problem prioritization, factual grounding, and reasoning coherence. GPT-5.6-Sol led every evaluated component and reached consensus in the fewest review rounds. These findings show that the benchmark can distinguish model capability, the contribution of multidisciplinary synthesis, criterion-specific effects of response structure, deliberation dynamics, and inference requirements within one common-case evaluation.
  </details>

- **[EMO: A Closed-Loop Test-Time Computation Framework via Alternating Context Optimization and Logits Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22160)**  `arXiv:2610.22160`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Xia, Yehui Tang, Zhi-Hong Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time context optimization adapts frozen language models through textual feedback, but ordinary conditional generation provides no separate control over how strongly to reinforce the distributional changes induced by a context update. We introduce EMO, a training-free framework inspired by expectation--maximization that alternates question-specific context estimation (E-step) and distribution refinement (M-step). The E-step revises a system context using textual gradients from an external judge without access to gold answers. The M-step contrasts next-token distributions under the revised and preceding contexts at a shared prefix to construct a confidence-constrained logit correction. Feedback on the steered response drives the next context update, coupling adaptation across rounds with intervention within each generation. Theoretically, we connect parameter and context optimization through a shared target-KL objective and additive logit representations, and derive the steering vector from context-induced logit displacements. Under context-improvement and faithful-steering assumptions, we prove a one-round decrease in target KL divergence for sufficiently small steering strength. Under greedy decoding across eight models and six benchmarks, EMO improves the zero-shot baseline in 39 of 40 aggregated settings, averaging $+9.10$ percentage points. With the same judge, it adds $+1.53$ points on average over context-only optimization (TextGrad), with consistent MMLU-Pro gains across models. At matched target-model generation budgets, accuracy advantages over best-of-$N$ are strongest on Llama, while oracle coverage is higher in most comparisons despite fewer candidates. The coverage--selection gap motivates allocating test-time computation jointly to candidate generation and verification.
  </details>

- **[Multi-SFT: Permissively-Licensed Multilingual Instruction-Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22086)**  `arXiv:2610.22086`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Daniela Baiamonte, Kamyar Zeinalipour, Elena Fano, Stefano Simonazzi, Andrea Zugarini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning datasets remain overwhelmingly English-centric and commercially restricted, as their content is frequently generated by proprietary models whose terms of service forbid training competing systems. We introduce Multi-SFT, a commercially permissive collection of 502,818 instruction-response pairs across five European languages, built via translation and regeneration pipelines for tasks where translation is insufficient. Human evaluation yields inter-annotator agreement of at least 0.87 across all datasets and languages. Fine-tuning nine models from three different families (EuroLLM, OLMo 2, Qwen3; 1B-32B) and evaluating them on 13 benchmarks shows that Multi-SFT alone is competitive with larger augmented mixes and official instruction-tuned releases, with decisive advantages on safety tasks.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking the Role of Tool Graph Memory in Multi-Turn Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21571)**  `arXiv:2610.21571`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Uiji Hwang, Richeng Xuan, Taeuk Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents solve complex tasks by calling external tools over multiple turns. Recent work has proposed tool graph memory, which stores past experience as tool transitions to guide subsequent selection. However, it remains unclear whether this component consistently improves agents' tool use or undermines it. We evaluate tool graph memory on nine models and two benchmarks, finding that it seldom improves significantly over baselines, and that its gains, where present, cost over twice the LLM calls. The memory's guidance is often incorrect, and random or no guidance retains most of the gain, implying that the benefit stems from extra computation rather than the memory itself. Moreover, even with the correct next tool suggested, many failures remain, such as giving up without a tool call. Storing the same experience instead as textual insights outperforms tool graph memory with almost no extra LLM calls. This suggests that memory serves better as a soft prior than as a prescription of the next tool, leading to a more effective and generalizable solution.
  </details>

- **[Can LLMs Tell Stories Out of Order? A Corpus and Benchmark for Nonlinear Narrative Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21468)**  `arXiv:2610.21468`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Syed Rifat Raiyan, Md. Tariquzzaman, Md Farhan Ishmam, Tenghao Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every story has two orders: the order in which its events happen and the order in which they are told. A story is nonlinear when the two differ. We introduce a benchmark of 83 published works that measures whether language models can tell nonlinear stories. For each work, we annotate one list of events in both orders, together with a story graph of causal dependencies, character knowledge, and the questions the telling raises and resolves. Given events in chronological order, all 19 models tell them, on average, more chronologically than the published works do; asked for a specific structure, they land within the human range about half the time. We then compare a model's stories of the same events under different conditions, using both an automated judge and 71 human readers. Both prefer the chronological story a model writes by default to the nonlinear one it writes on request, which readers find harder to follow in 81% of decisive judgments. Readers also prefer stories written from the story graph to stories constrained to the published telling order (72% of decisive judgments), a difference the judge does not detect. Our results suggest that following an instruction to reorder a story is not yet the same as telling it well out of order.
  </details>

- **[Same Context, Different Agents: A Behavioral Study of Semantic Layers, Orchestration and Compaction in Data Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20959)**  `arXiv:2610.20959`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhivar Sourati, Marianne Menglin Liu, Zheng Wang, Rongguang Wang, Sai Ashish Somayajula, Tao Sheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answering questions over heterogeneous data means combining evidence from files, databases, documents and the web. Data agents automate this, but their success depends on context. Three context-engineering techniques (CETs) help by shaping it: semantic layers describe the data, orchestration splits work among agents, and compaction condenses interaction history. Existing studies validate each CET alone, leaving open how agent behavior shapes its effect. We present a comprehensive analysis of the three CETs, alone and combined, with seven large language models (LLMs) on 1,514 long-horizon tasks from five benchmarks, covering behavior, accuracy and cost. We find that data agents without CETs form three behavior groups: investigators, premature answerers that answer without inspecting data, and early stoppers that give up. Each CET delivers its known benefit, from more investigation to smaller prompts, but only the behavior group it matches turns that benefit into accuracy, such as premature answerers under orchestration. For investigators, the same CETs can hurt: the semantic layer stops them from looking, orchestration makes them overconfident, costing 5 percentage points, and compaction makes them repeat steps. Combining CETs yields smaller gains and losses than their sum. We derive guidance for choosing CETs by agent behavior, task demands and accuracy-cost trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[CoTS: Competitive Token Selection for Latent Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20642)**  `arXiv:2610.20642`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jie Yu, Yuxi Sun, Zhaoxu Li, Chunxiao Liu, Xudong Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent latent reasoning methods move beyond discrete Chain-of-Thought (CoT) by replacing sampled tokens with continuous soft embeddings or hidden states, enabling richer intermediate representations and preserving alternative reasoning paths. However, the need for latent reasoning varies across decoding steps: confident predictions favor discrete commitment, whereas ambiguous steps benefit from preserving multiple plausible alternatives. Existing approaches typically handle this variation through either global soft activation or hard switching based on uncertainty thresholds, treating latent intervention as an all-or-nothing decision rather than continuously adapting its strength. To address this limitation, we propose Competitive Token Selection (CoTS), a training-free framework that reframes latent intervention from binary mode selection into continuous allocation at the token level. CoTS uses the probability competition between the two most likely candidates to dynamically balance discrete commitment and soft exploration at each reasoning step. The second candidate represents the strongest alternative to the model's preferred continuation, so the separation between the two directly reflects the stability of the current discrete choice. When one candidate clearly dominates, CoTS keeps the representation close to the sampled discrete token to preserve a stable reasoning trajectory; as the competition strengthens, it increases the contribution of the soft representation to retain plausible alternatives. This competition-based allocation requires neither manually tuned switching thresholds nor mixing coefficients, and introduces no additional forward passes. Experiments across six reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that CoTS consistently outperforms standard CoT and state-of-the-art training-free latent reasoning methods. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A primer on evaluation methods for large language models in healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14819)**  `arXiv:2609.14819`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Suzannah E McKinney, Phuc Vu, Samuel A Justice, Christopher Humphries, Alyssa Pradhan, Timothy J Keyes, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have a growing range of applications in medicine, and their evaluation is critical for ensuring they provide benefit and not harm. This evaluation can be more challenging than traditional machine learning for many reasons, including probabilistic and open-ended outputs, and behavior that shifts with prompt design and accumulated context. This review covers four key areas of LLM evaluation: principles of study design, statistical methods, capability evaluation and clinical application evaluation. Capability evaluation considers different benchmarks, including multiple-choice, agentic and multi-turn benchmarks, alongside operational metrics like token usage and latency. Clinical application evaluation addresses establishing accuracy of free text outputs, such as human review and LLM-as-a-judge, and clinical trial approaches. Across sections, we describe underlying concepts and potential pitfalls, while emphasizing the importance of aligning evaluation methods with the research question. Together, this article aims to provide a pragmatic basis for designing and executing rigorous evaluations of healthcare LLMs.
  </details>

- **[RefactorPlatform: An Open-Source Harness for Controlled Evaluation of Repository-Scale Refactoring Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04898)**  `arXiv:2609.04898`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Aziz Ben Amor, Drish Mali, Mann Acharya, Vijayasri Iyer, Sébastien Bratières_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Repository-scale refactoring requires coding agents to propagate a single change across many interdependent files without altering program behavior, yet to our knowledge no existing harness isolates the design choices that determine agent success on this task. We present RefactorPlatform, an open-source evaluation harness that holds the environment fixed and varies each design axis explicitly: model backbone (via OpenRouter and GitHub Copilot CLI), execution regime (baseline, retrieval-augmented, and multi-agent), and prompt specificity. Each run executes in an isolated workspace with live terminal streaming, per-task logging of tokens, diffs, and transcripts, AST-based verification, and exportable telemetry for audit and reproduction. Demonstrating the platform on 100 multi-file RefactorBench tasks across four model families, we illustrate the analyses it supports: AST-aware chunking outperforms naive token-window chunking by 25-30 percentage points (pp) across prompt modes, whereas naive retrieval falls below the retrieval-free baseline; a lean retrieval-augmented single agent (86%) beats the sub-agent configuration we evaluated (66%) on matched tasks with no task passing under delegation that fails under retrieval; and retrieval's accuracy gains absorb its token overhead, leaving cost per successful refactoring unchanged. RefactorPlatform is open-sourced to make refactoring-agent evaluation reproducible and auditable.
  </details>

- **[From Signals to Transfer: A Factorised Study of Probe-Based Uncertainty Estimation in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27679)**  `arXiv:2606.27679`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ponhvoan Srey, Xiaobao Wu, Cong-Duy Nguyen, Quang Minh Nguyen, Duc Anh Vu, Anh Tuan Luu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probe-based uncertainty estimation (UE) has emerged as a prominent approach to detect hallucinations in Large Language Models (LLMs) by learning uncertainty from internal model signals. Yet, recent methods vary simultaneously across feature design, training data construction, and evaluation setting, obscuring what actually drives performance. To address this issue, we propose a factorised study of probe-based UE under matched conditions. Our results show that raw hidden states and attention features are difficult to outperform in-domain. However, under distribution shift, structured and compressed features are more robust, suggesting that in-domain performance alone is insufficient to measure progress. Furthermore, prompting and label construction significantly affect probe behaviour. Building on these empirical design principles, we construct benchmark-pretrained probes that retain useful discriminative signal on open-ended factual generation. Their competitive performance relative to limited-label target setting probes and sampling-based estimators suggests that they can provide a practical starting point for subsequent target-domain adaptation. More broadly, our work encourages deployment-oriented evaluation of probe-based uncertainty estimators.
  </details>

- **[Towards Reliable Long-Context Reasoning: Detecting Unanswerable Questions via FactGuard](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.05607)**  `arXiv:2504.05607`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qian-Wen Zhang, Biao Liu, Fang Li, Jie Wang, Lingfeng Qiao, Yifei Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite the success of Large Language Models (LLMs) in long-context tasks, their reliability in recognizing unanswerable questions remains a critical challenge. In information-seeking scenarios, models often suffer from overconfidence and hallucinations when evidence is missing or misleading. In this paper, we propose FACTGUARD, a systematic multi-task workflow for generating evidence-grounded answerable and unanswerable questions. We introduce FACTGUARD-BENCH, a benchmark comprising 25,220 examples with context lengths up to 128K, specifically targeting Lack of Evidence and Misleading Evidence scenarios. Evaluations of eleven representative open-source and proprietary LLMs reveal a significant reliability gap: models struggle to reject unanswerable questions as context scales, with the leading model achieving only 71.62% accuracy. By training with FACTGUARD-BENCH, we improve overall accuracy to 81.17% and significantly enhance models' reasoned rejection capabilities. Our work provides a scalable path toward building more faithful and trustworthy long-context LLMs. Our source is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Training Reasoning Models to Generate Diverse Scientific Ideas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22324)**  `arXiv:2610.22324`  `cs.CL`  
  _Manya Wadhwa, Pranav Narayanan Venkit, Hiroaki Hayashi, Yu Li, Tuhin Chakrabarty, Chien-Sheng Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  One of the first steps of a research pipeline is generating ideas. Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to automate this step, but most of this work optimizes the quality of individual ideas, while overlooking diversity. In our work, we study document-grounded ideation, focusing on increasing the diversity of LLM generated ideas and ensuring they are high quality. Given prior papers, a model must propose a set of ideas that are each high-quality and clearly distinct from one another, while also being grounded in the input documents. To optimize for this, we propose training with a set-level reinforcement learning objective. In our setup, in a single generation, the model writes K structured idea cards grounded in the source papers, and the set is scored jointly, where each idea's quality is weighted by its distance from the ideas written before it. This teaches the model to steer each new idea away from its predecessors. We show that our trained 8B model achieves higher creative utility (a measure combining quality and diversity) than Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-6 Astra on our contamination-free benchmark. In a pilot human study with four experts, our sets are judged as promising as Claude Opus 4.8's and contain marginally more unique ideas. When a coding agent runs proof-of-concept experiments on the ideas, our model's success@k is comparable to Claude Opus 4.8's, so we see that the added diversity does not cost any executability.
  </details>

- **[MindEval2: Clinically Grounded Patients and Rubrics for Evaluating Language Models in Mental Health Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22314)**  `arXiv:2610.22314`  `cs.CL`  
  _John Mendonça, Daniel Tschernutter, José Pombal, Areti Vassilopoulos, Catarina Botelho, Ignacio Sarasua, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With people increasingly turning to AI chatbots for mental health support, the need to evaluate these tools is becoming more pressing. Recent benchmarks attempt to mimic a realistic setting by simulating multi-turn interactions with synthetic patients and evaluating them with an LLM-as-a-judge. However, the usefulness of these methods is limited because simulated patients can be overly forthcoming and self-reflective, and judgments typically involve opaque Likert scales with unclear practical utility. To bridge these gaps, we introduce MindEval2, an evaluation framework that consists of two components: Mental Health Quality Rubrics (MHQR), for evaluation, and MindSim, for patient simulation. For MHQR, we collaborate with PhD-level psychologists to develop a set of rubrics that break down response quality into explicit operational criteria that are more interpretable and actionable than existing evaluation taxonomies. MindSim, in turn, is an LLM harness that combines carefully-designed patient profiles with tools, enabling more varied, challenging, and realistic interactions than existing baselines. The harness also contains a memory component that is updated between each interaction, enabling MindEval2 to model the longitudinal aspect of mental health. We evaluate 18 proprietary and open-weight models on MindEval2, finding that while frontier models have largely improved on issues related to surface level quality and boundaries, guidance calibration remains the dominant failure mode across all models, accounting for most of the quality gap.
  </details>

- **[Grid-Bench: Can LLMs Predict How Discrete Worlds Change?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22312)**  `arXiv:2610.22312`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zixiang Xu, Zirui Song, Wanrong Zheng, Chiyu Zhang, Yue Huang, Yuyang Peng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial instructions describe how to move objects, transform regions, or modify their surroundings. When several operations are applied in sequence, each acts on a configuration shaped by earlier changes. How accurately can large language models (LLMs) predict the resulting spatial state? We introduce Grid-Bench, an executable benchmark in which eight families of operations transform discrete worlds represented as two-, three-, and four-dimensional grids. Given an initial grid, explicit rules, and an event history expressed as text, a model predicts the complete final grid. Reference execution makes every predicted value checkable, supporting comparisons across spatial operations, history lengths, and workloads. Evaluating six LLMs shows that complete-state accuracy falls sharply as histories grow, with substantial divergence among models that perform similarly on single events. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Even over long histories, some models recover many correct changes beyond what copying the initial state would yield. Grid-Bench makes both complete recovery and these differences in returned states directly measurable across a range of spatial conditions.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Target Few-Shot Prompting with Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22294)**  `arXiv:2610.22294`  `cs.CL`  
  _Anton Xue, Alan Li, Rahul Saha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Few-shot prompting is useful when instructions and rewards are hard to specify. One such application is editing by example, where a demonstrated formatting change or API migration may need to be applied across multiple sites. However, such edits often depend on both left and right context, making generation with causal autoregressive models awkward. We therefore introduce multi-target few-shot prompting with diffusion language models, where examples guide the infilling of multiple masked regions within a shared canvas. To address the known challenge of reliably following demonstrations in few-shot prompting, we further leverage selective masking of the canvas to derive a likelihood-ratio reward that favors example-supported infills. Across few-shot prompting benchmarks and software refactoring case studies, we find that demonstrations improve infilling accuracy, with example guidance yielding further gains in several settings.
  </details>

- **[Hy-Memory-30B-A3B: Attributed Reinforcement Learning for Long-Term Memory Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22196)**  `arXiv:2610.22196`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianxiang Fei, Jiazheng Kang, Mao Zheng, Xingyan He, Xiang Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External-memory systems extend language-model applications beyond a context window, but their quality depends critically on the model executing the write path: deciding what to retain, how precisely to represent it, and how new evidence should update persistent state. Existing frameworks typically place a general-purpose large language model behind framework-specific prompts. This raises serving cost, and a writer specialized for one protocol does not transfer directly to another; a unified model that can operate across established memory frameworks remains largely unexplored. We present Hy-Memory-30B-A3B, a compact long-term-memory writer trained across Mem0, MemOS, and Hy-Memory. Memory On-Policy Distillation teaches framework-specific write protocols on student-generated states; Memory RL then optimizes downstream answerability using dense per-chunk probes, attributed cross-chunk rewards, over-storage controls, and chunk-level GRPO. Carrying policy-written banks across windows keeps training aligned with deployment. On fixed 20% benchmark subsamples, Hy-Memory-30B-A3B ranks first on all four evaluated harnesses, including held-out EverMemOS, with a 55.49% macro average versus 54.62% for Claude Opus 5.
  </details>

- **[PremiseSQL: Evaluating Premise Critique and Abstention in Text-to-SQL Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22158)**  `arXiv:2610.22158`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shuo Lin, Jinzhe Li, Chenlu Guo, Yanhan Li, Yuan Wu, Yi Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have substantially improved Text-to-SQL performance, yet existing NL2SQL systems largely assume that user queries are valid and answerable. As a result, models often generate executable SQL even for queries with flawed premises. We introduce premise critique ability in NL2SQL, namely the ability to recognize flawed queries and abstain from generating misleading SQL. To evaluate this capability, we propose PremiseSQL, a benchmark containing 1,800 flawed NL2SQL queries across four categories of premise failures, along with their 1,800 original queries. We further design an evaluation framework to measure intrinsic and prompt-induced premise-aware behavior across three prompting settings. Experiments on frontier LLMs show that current models are strongly premise-trusting by default and rarely critique flawed premises. Stronger critique-oriented prompting improves detection and abstention on flawed queries, but also increases false invalid judgments and suppresses SQL generation on originals. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Intern-Decision: Efficient Multimodal Decision Making with Autoregressive Masked Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22131)**  `arXiv:2610.22131`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tong Zhu, Zhe Xu, Zefeng He, Yunkai Chai, Xuyang Hu, Dehong Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interactive agents repeatedly choose among a finite set of actions, labels, or ordinal outcomes. Generating a free-form response for each choice adds latency and complicates the interface between a model and its environment. We present Intern-Decision, a family of 0.8B, 2B, and 4B multimodal decision models that return structured choices and candidate probabilities from text and optional images. Our autoregressive masked language modeling objective places one decision placeholder per field in a complete assistant JSON skeleton. Ground-truth symbols appear only in supervision, while all fields are evaluated in a single causal forward pass using the backbone's native language-model head. The 4B model achieves a 90.02% unweighted average accuracy across seven evaluation suites, compared with 88.74% for Jev v1.13.0. For a fixed 289-token request with three fields on one RTX 4090, mean latency is 44.16 ms for the 4B model and approximately 33--34 ms for the smaller models. On a separate 96-case known-distribution pilot, temperature scaling reduces expected multiclass Brier from 0.628 to 0.550 and expected calibration error from 0.213 to 0.089. A confidence-based handoff study on 111 public Jevbench-Hard items improves accuracy from 82/111 (73.87%) to 97/111 (87.39%) by routing 42 items to Atria-Dawn-Preview. We also describe qualitative game, visual interface, and browser demonstrations. These results support compact decision models as a practical component of larger agent systems.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Context Length: Characterising the Difficulty of Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22067)**  `arXiv:2610.22067`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shiwen Qin, Cameron Barker, Henry Gouk, Elliot J. Crowley, Shay B. Cohen, Mirella Lapata, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context language models support ever longer context windows, and evaluations typically treat context length as the main indicator of difficulty. However, context length alone cannot explain why models fail on long-context tasks that require reasoning over complex information. Contexts of equal length can pose very different challenges: a task may be solvable from a single local passage, or it may require integrating evidence distributed across the full context. We quantitatively characterise this difficulty through three properties: evidence scope, how much necessary information must be found; evidence dispersion, how widely that information is scattered within the context; and reasoning depth, how many dependent steps are needed to derive an answer from it. We construct LURCH, a multi-domain question-answering benchmark of 6,550 instances with supporting evidence and reasoning graphs, enabling all three properties to be quantified. Evaluations of proprietary and open-weight models show that: (1) at fixed context length, all our three properties are strongly negatively correlated with model performance; (2) as context length increases, performance degrades sharply on samples with dispersed evidence, whereas it remains relatively stable on samples with localised evidence; and (3) difficulty compounds across properties, as questions with moderately high values on all three are harder than those with an extreme value on just one. These findings establish evidence scope, dispersion and reasoning depth as measures of long-context reasoning difficulty that complement context length.
  </details>

- **[Behavioral Forgetting Is Not Privacy in Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21799)**  `arXiv:2610.21799`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tyler Lizzo, Larry Heck_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning aims to remove targeted information from large language models, but existing evaluations often measure forgetting only on a designated benchmark distribution. We argue that such behavioral forgetting is an insufficient standard for privacy-oriented unlearning, and that evaluation should instead test whether forgetting persists across the access paths through which the same information may be recovered. We demonstrate this with a controlled case study of cross-lingual querying: across multiple multilingual model families, forgetting varies substantially when semantically equivalent information is queried in different languages and scripts. A single access path is therefore enough to expose a real gap between behavioral forgetting and information inaccessibility, motivating broader evaluation of access-path generalization and adversarial recoverability alongside in-distribution forgetting.
  </details>

- **[No Single Tokenizer Feature Reliably Predicts Downstream Language Model Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21679)**  `arXiv:2610.21679`  `cs.CL`  
  _Antonio Lopardo, Luis Frentzen Salim, Shikhar Srivastava, Kevin Jiang, Mridul Sharma, Catherine Arnett_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tokenization is a critical component of the language modeling pipeline, but it is poorly understood. Tokenizers can impact downstream performance, but there is currently mixed evidence about which tokenizer features predict a better language model without requiring a full training run. In this paper, we analyze approximately 200 language models trained on nine different languages. We evaluate both models and their tokenizers and test whether there is a relationship between any proposed intrinsic tokenizer evaluations and model performance, as measured by a wide variety of benchmarks.
