Despite the quantity and variety of proposed intrinsic tokenizer metrics, we find that none consistently predicts downstream performance across model families, tasks, and languages. This motivates the need for novel tokenizer evaluation metrics and approaches. We propose novel linguistically grounded metric introduced in this work shows that new metrics can capture aspects of tokenizer quality that existing metrics miss, though their predictive power is likely to vary across model families, languages, and tasks.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Multimodal RLVR with Pairwise Token Contrast and Adaptive Trajectory Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21671)**  `arXiv:2610.21671`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yiran Zhang, Hanbo Huang, Jielan Li, Zhen Zhao, Shiyu Liang, Hongxia Hao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves multimodal reasoning, but group-relative policy optimization broadcasts a single sequence-level advantage to every token of a response, although errors usually localize to a few decisive steps. Recent self-distilled variants refine credit with a teacher--student likelihood gap. However, this signal cannot tell whether it relies on the image or on a language shortcut, and is applied uniformly even when the teacher is uninformative. To address this, we propose Pairwise token contrast and Adaptive trajectory ConfidencE (PACE). Pairwise token contrast scores each response under four teacher views, crossing a correct or incorrect answer hint with the image present or removed, and compares the two hints for every token, which cancels shifts shared by both hints and reveals whether the token's support relies on the image. Adaptive trajectory confidence measures how well the teacher predicts each response given the correct hint and the image, normalizes this score within the batch, and uses it to set how far the token contrast redistributes credit within that response. PACE achieves the best average accuracy on three backbones and ranks first on all evaluated multimodal reasoning benchmarks with Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct. Experiments show that both components matter: replacing pairwise token contrast with a single-view signal underperforms our method, while adaptive trajectory confidence adds further improvement.
  </details>

- **[OPRD: On-Policy Reminder Distillation for Long-Horizon Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21496)**  `arXiv:2610.21496`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuechen Luo, Zhiyi Lai, Miao Ziqi, Zihang Xu, Jingxuan Xu, Songwei Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable repository-level coding requires agents not only to generate correct code but also to preserve constraints and respond to tool feedback across long interactions. However, our diagnostic analysis shows that agents often fail to act on relevant information already available in context, while final Pass/Fail outcomes provide only sparse process supervision. We introduce On-Policy Reminder Distillation (OPRD), which combines Multi-Scope Reminder supervision with outcome-based routing on reminder-free student rollouts. Successful rollouts provide autoregressive self-imitation targets, whereas failed rollouts receive Reminder-conditioned reverse-KL supervision from a teacher that re-evaluates the same student-generated prefixes. A Global Reminder converts final-test diagnostics into trajectory-level guidance, while Turn-local Reminders apply only to the next decision following the relevant environment observation. The teacher and student share a two-stage initialization from Coding SFT and Instruction-Following (IF) SFT. At inference, the student operates without Reminders. On SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Pro, and SWE-bench Multilingual, OPRD improves the benchmark-averaged task resolution rate by 11.3 points over the open-source Qwen3.5-4B base model. Under the same diagnostic protocol, it reduces the aggregate error-event count from 446 to 222, a 50.2% reduction.
  </details>

- **[SITAR: A Situational Distortion Metric for Automatic Speech Recognition Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21440)**  `arXiv:2610.21440`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingzhe Lu, Jinhao Liu, Yunpeng Li, Hang Su, Lichun Fan, Jian Luan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In automatic speech recognition (ASR), transcription errors with similar lexical or semantic discrepancies can have markedly different consequences for the situation conveyed by speech. Capturing these differences requires not merely contextual assessment, but an understanding of what the spoken discourse describes, establishes, or seeks to bring about. However, relying on LLM judges introduces computational cost and scoring variability. We introduce SITAR, a situational distortion metric for ASR, realized through a comprehensive end-to-end construction framework. Specifically, this framework aggregates situational severity and cumulative extent into a unified score via a bounded recursive formulation. Using targets derived from this formulation with limited offline LLM support, we train SITAR as a lightweight BERT-family regressor that scores reference-hypothesis pairs in a single forward pass, entirely free of any prohibitive runtime costs. Recognizing that introducing a new metric demands rigorous justification, we formalize a principled validation protocol spanning both extrinsic validity, assessed at macro and micro levels, and intrinsic scorer reliability. The former, evaluated across diverse datasets and models, demonstrates that SITAR reliably tracks broad capability scaling while providing orthogonal diagnostic utility, whereas the latter, examined on a purpose-built controlled benchmark, confirms the operational soundness of the scoring instrument itself. Through systematic validation, we demonstrate that SITAR holds strong potential as a practical, lightweight complement to existing ASR metrics. We will release the full recipes (code, model weights, dataset, etc.) after the internal review to support community research.
  </details>

- **[RenoLayout: Unified Floorplan Renovation and Furniture Layout for Constraint-Aware Residential Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21366)**  `arXiv:2610.21366`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jingguo Liu, Yuanqing Liu, Chong Zhang, Yue Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Residential design aims to transform existing homes into functional living spaces while satisfying diverse design preferences and physical constraints. However, existing floorplan generation methods typically assume empty building boundaries as input, whereas renovation requires reasoning over existing floorplans. Furthermore, floorplan renovation and furniture layout are commonly addressed as separate tasks, overlooking their inherent spatial dependency. Consequently, practical residential design must preserve immutable structural components while transforming editable spatial elements. It must also optimize furniture placement under design objectives and physical constraints. To address these challenges, we formulate residential design as a design-point-guided conditional generation task and propose RenoLayout, a framework that translates a pre-design residential state together with a design-point set into an executable Geometry-as-Code program. To enable controllable supervision, we construct a Design Map comprising 123 fine-grained design points covering structural renovation and furniture layout. We further introduce Difference-Grounded Design-Point Annotation to derive design-point sets automatically from before--after design pairs with VLM-based consistency verification. RenoLayout is trained using a three-stage CPT--SFT--RL pipeline, progressively improving spatial reasoning, instruction following, and constraint compliance. We also introduce the RenoLayout Benchmark, a set of 200 real-world before--after renovation cases with complete furniture layouts. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first benchmark for unified residential design. These cases include design-critical structural and building-service constraints.
  </details>

- **[VALCON: Benchmarking and Enhancing Value Consistency in Large Language Models via Dual-Role Variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21304)**  `arXiv:2610.21304`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junru Wu, Jingting Zheng, Yuqi Ren, Deyi Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current value alignment evaluations for large language models (LLMs) heavily rely on output compliance, overlooking whether LLMs maintain value consistency when alternating between generator and evaluator roles. Such consistency is increasingly important because modern LLM agents and alignment pipelines often rely on the same LLM to both generate actions and evaluate their acceptability. To mitigate this limitation, we propose VALCON, a role-driven data construction framework that operationalizes value consistency into three benchmark suites (VAB-Consistency, VAB-Boundary, VAB-Challenge) via dual-role variation. By systematically pairing an LLM's generative behavior with its evaluative judgments under identical principles, VALCON synthesizes target evaluation data that exposes hidden value discrepancies. Experiments on 28 LLMs reveal pervasive cross-role inconsistency: models with highly compliant outputs frequently fail to align their judgments with their own generations. To understand and address this gap, we perform mechanistic probing that uncovers a role-dependent representational decoupling associated with this behavioral asymmetry. Guided by these insights, we propose a representation-directed intervention that effectively mitigates consistency failures. Our work establishes value consistency as a distinct alignment dimension and provides a complete evaluation-analysis-mitigation pipeline. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[KBS: A Korean Benchmark Suite for Foundation Model Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21227)**  `arXiv:2610.21227`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yeonkyoung So, Jongmin Kim, Sungmok Jung, Gyuseong Lee, Sangho Kim, Jongyeon Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present a Korean Benchmark Suite (KBS) comprising Ko-ARC, Ko-GSM8K, Ko-EQ-Bench, Ko-WinoGrande, Ko-LAMBADA, and Ko-IFEval, covering six capabilities across 9,396 items. The suite combines review, localization, direct Korean construction, and redesign for Korean, avoiding a translation-only approach to benchmark adaptation. We evaluate Korean-developed models, multilingual open models, and closed API models under protocols that account for model access, prompt format, and scoring differences. Results show that Korean benchmark scores depend on task construction, scoring rules, prompting, and model post-training. These findings suggest that Korean evaluation should report both final scores and the settings used to obtain them.
  </details>

- **[From Long Context to Selective Memory:A Survey of Memory-Centric Compression for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21165)**  `arXiv:2610.21165`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chaogui Gou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context capability has become essential for large language models (LLMs) to handle complex tasks, yet continuously expanding the context window does not remove the computational, storage, and information-selection pressures incurred at inference time. Existing work on prompt compression, KV-cache management, efficient serving, retrieval augmentation, and agent memory is usually discussed in isolation, leaving the flow of information between storage and computation insufficiently understood. This survey reframes long-context compression as a memory-management problem for allocating inference-time resources. We distinguish compute-active memory, which directly participates in model computation, from compute-inactive memory, which lies outside the computation path but can be stored, retrieved, updated, or forgotten, and we characterize offloaded KV caches, persistent prefix caches, and precomputed KV states as intermediate states on the activation path. On this basis, we formulate writing, offloading, recall, retrieval-and-injection, materialization, eviction, consolidation, and forgetting as state transitions within an information lifecycle, and introduce a budgeted cost model that places these operations in a single cost account. The resulting framework unifies short-term context compression, long-term memory management, and the transitions between them, and shows that the central challenge is not merely reducing information volume but deciding which information should participate in costly inference, when, and in what form. We further summarize evaluation dimensions, benchmark gaps, and open challenges, and argue that future research should move from static, pointwise compression toward dynamic, governable, lifecycle-aware memory management.
  </details>

- **[Stop Training Against Soft Users: Mentalized Adversarial Self-Play for Proactive Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21156)**  `arXiv:2610.21156`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zanwu Liu, Dan Zhang, Xiaodi Wang, Yongbo Mao, Xiaoyang Guo, Yonglin Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive dialogue agents must guide multi-turn interactions toward task goals while adapting to users whose intentions and attitudes evolve. Existing planners improve system side action selection through search, learned policies, or user aware planning, but are often trained and evaluated against fixed or overly compliant simulators; as a result, high simulated success can reflect soft user bias rather than robust dialogue ability. We introduce MASP, a Mentalized Adversarial Self Play framework that treats proactive dialogue as a partially observable game between a system and a rationally resisting user. MASP represents the user's evolving mental state with a BDI based latent state, separates reward side teacher mentalization from policy side student mentalization, and alternates self play updates for the system and user policies. This turns robustness to rational resistance into a training objective rather than only an evaluation condition. Across diverse proactive dialogue benchmarks, compared with the widely used SFT and Planner-only approaches, MASP substantially improves success rate (SR) and rationality constrained success (RCSR), reduces average turns (AT), and performs better against hard users who make the task goal more difficult to achieve.
  </details>

- **[From Observation to Intervention: Distilling Self-Generated In-Context Demonstrations via Executable Causal Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21149)**  `arXiv:2610.21149`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minming Ye, Min Liu, Haoang Chi, Ciheng Wu, Naiyang Guan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-generated In-Context Learning (ICL) enables LLMs to synthesize their own few-shot demonstrations, often outperforming manual prompts. However, existing methods filter demonstrations by observation, assuming a rationale is high-quality if it leads to the correct output. We show such filtering suffers from \textit{observational equivalence}, whereby both faithful causal rationales and spurious post-hoc rationales can yield same outputs and are statistically indistinguishable from observation alone. Inspired by independent causal mechanisms, we argue that faithful rationales imply \textit{transfer invariance}, in which causal rationales should remain effective across logically similar samples. We instantiate this criterion as \textbf{E}xecutable \textbf{C}ausal \textbf{I}ntervention for \textbf{D}emonstrations \textbf{D}istillation (ECI2D), which abstracts language rationales into executable programs and applies the causal interventions ($do$-operator) on logically similar samples, retaining only those demonstrations that imply transfer invariance. Experiments on math, logical and commonsense reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ECI2D consistently outperforms observation-based ICL while retaining far fewer demonstrations, with gains exceeding 7 points as the test distribution shifts.
  </details>

- **[Prune the Summary, Not the History: Source-Judged Compaction for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20927)**  `arXiv:2610.20927`  `cs.CL`  
  _Guanghui Min, Chen Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents rely on context compaction: when the interaction history outgrows the context window, a compressor replaces it with a natural-language summary. We show that these summaries contain more than a record of what happened. Compaction prompts ask for decisions and next steps, so each summary mixes facts established by the environment with reasoning written by the compressor, and the agent cannot tell the two apart afterwards. In an analysis of a widely used structured compressor, 83% of failed runs trace back to an action that carries out a specific summary entry, most of which no environment output supports. We propose Source-Judged Compaction (SJC), a training-free post-processing step that scores each summary entry for whether it has a source in the environment outputs or the previous summary, and removes the entries that do not. It changes neither the agent nor the compressor and adds one inexpensive judge request per compaction. The judge is Jev, a non-generative decision model that answers the source question with a calibrated probability instead of text. On AppWorld and OfficeBench with two agent models, SJC improves cross-run reliability over the underlying compressor by 2.1%-8.9% without increasing the agent's token usage.
  </details>

- **[APRiL: An Open-ended Benchmark for Chinese Social Awareness in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20785)**  `arXiv:2610.20785`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tian Lan, Han Zhang, Shanshan Wang, Yahan Yu, Jiang Li, Yugo Murawaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used in social interactions, where appropriate responses require recognizing implicit cues, inferring mental states, identifying conflicts, and considering potential consequences. However, existing benchmarks often focus on specific social abilities, constrained task formats, or particular interaction settings, offering limited coverage of social awareness across diverse contexts. To address this gap, we introduce APRiL, an open-ended benchmark for Chinese social awareness with instance-specific rubrics. APRiL contains 2,196 Social Awareness Evaluation Instances across six categories and two complementary tasks: Social Scenario Analysis evaluates models' understanding of social cues, mental states, and conflict structures, while Socially Aware Response assesses whether they can translate this understanding into appropriate, natural responses. Experiments on 12 models from six families reveal a consistent understanding-to-response gap. Higher-quality scenario analyses improve responses, while weak analyses may reduce response quality. APRiL provides a systematic resource for advancing socially aware language models in Chinese contexts. Our data and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Does Personalization Break Trust? Open-Set Rejection Calibration Under Few-Shot On-Device Intent Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20652)**  `arXiv:2610.20652`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vaibhav Prajapati, Pushp Garg_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Always-on virtual assistants increasingly personalize on-device to new user-specific commands via few-shot fine-tuning (e.g., LoRA), but this adaptation is evaluated almost exclusively on whether it learns the new commands, not on whether the assistant can still recognize an input that matches none of its intents - the out-of-scope (OOS) rejection problem. We show that naive few-shot LoRA personalization degrades OOS-rejection AUROC (area under the ROC curve) on every one of five public intent-classification benchmarks (mean drop $0.055$--$0.258$, five seeds each), while in-scope accuracy is preserved. A scorer-invariant check shows part of this drop is an artifact of the standard confidence scorer's own miscalibration under adaptation, though a smaller, genuine effect remains under any scorer; we trace it to a measurable shrinkage of the embedding margin around known-intent regions (pooled Spearman rho=0.61$). A deployment-cheap fix is scoring rejection by Mahalanobis distance instead of softmax confidence without extra training/retraining recovers most of the lost quality on 3 of 5 datasets at no accuracy cost, beats two further OOD scoring baselines, and generalizes across shot count, LoRA rank, and a second base encoder. We report honestly where the fix falls short and release all code for reproducibility.
  </details>

- **[PRISM: PRIvileged Sibling-guided Multi-Agent Topology Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20628)**  `arXiv:2610.20628`  `cs.CL`  
  _Phuong Tuan Dat, Qi Li, Xinchao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) based on large language models (LLMs) have become a powerful way to address complex problems, and their effectiveness depends critically on the collaboration topology that decides which agents participate and how they communicate. Recent work learns to generate this topology from the task query, fitting the generator to corpora of execution-verified graphs. The post-training method for such generators remains a limitation: a corpus lists the graphs that solved a query but never ranks them, leaving continued supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with no per-query preference to exploit. Without such a preference, the generator drifts toward the topologies the corpus contains most often rather than those that suit each query, so a gain on one task can come at a cost on another. We propose PRISM, which recovers per-query signal from the same corpus by learning with privileged information: a teacher reads, through a zero-initialized low-rank branch, a sibling graph that also solved the query and is unavailable at deployment, and the student matches its per-decision targets on trajectories the student samples itself. Across six diverse benchmarks PRISM improves over every topology-design baseline evaluated, and matched-teacher controls support the contribution of privileged query information. Code is available in this link.
  </details>

- **[Anuvani-Diarization: Zero Shot Speaker Diarization and Annotation Conventions Across 22 Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20327)**  `arXiv:2610.20327`  `cs.CL`  
  _Stephen Fernandes, Narayan Choudhary_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  India's 22 scheduled languages are spoken by over a billion people in conversations that mix languages, often within one turn, overlap freely, and take place over telephones, in meetings, and in media. Knowing who spoke when, the task of speaker diarization, is essential to speech applications for such conversation: voice agents conversing with several people at once, speaker attributed transcription of meetings and calls, contact center analytics, and media monitoring. We present a systematic study of speaker diarization across all 22 languages on IndicDiarBench and introduce Anuvani-Diarization, which adapts a self-supervised end-to-end segmentation model to Indian conversation with uncertainty aware silver supervision. Evaluated zero shot alongside nine recent diarization systems, it reaches a duration weighted diarization error rate (DER) of 16.78% on all 1,164 recordings, the lowest of all systems we ran and second only to the best published result (16.0%), and leads the three strongest rivals in 14 of 22 languages. It matches its parent on six international benchmarks. We also show that the benchmark's utterance level annotation convention is a hidden variable worth 1.9 to 4.1 DER points, depending on the system, and that a convention learned from other Indian transcriptions does not transfer.
  </details>

- **[JetSpec++: Foresight Parallel Drafting with Self Refinements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19792)**  `arXiv:2610.19792`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhaoxiang Feng, Lanxiang Hu, Haoran Yuan, Yu-Yang Qian, Jihui Yang, Peng Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding's effectiveness is governed by two quantities: the acceptance rate of drafted tokens and the cost of producing them. We show the two objectives can be co-optimized with algorithm-system co-design, and present JetSpec++, which retains a strong learned draft model while hiding its cost almost entirely. JetSpec++ combines three components: intra-block self refinement, which trains the draft model to iteratively correct its own parallel predictions within a block of tokens; inter-block foresight drafting, which begins the next block before the current one is verified; and pipelined drafting and verification, which overlaps the two stages so drafting's marginal wall-clock cost approaches zero. In comparison with state-of-the-art speculative decoding methods, JetSpec++ attains a wall-clock speedup of up to $7.45\times$ over autoregressive (AR) decoding and $1.54\times$ over DSpark, with up to 1,361 tokens per second for a single request on GB200, and an average acceptance length of 8.94 across seven benchmarks, while preserving the target model's output distribution. Our code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Towards Unbiased On-Policy Distillation for Block Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05373)**  `arXiv:2610.05373`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zaiquan Yang, Fei Wei, Yong Wang, Yudong Han, Yiyu Li, Zhuofan Zong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as an effective post-training paradigm for language models, with recent efforts extending it to block diffusion language models (BDLMs). However, existing studies focus almost exclusively on small block sizes, leaving distillation into student models with larger blocks underexplored. In this work, we investigate this regime and reveal two critical optimization biases that induce severe training instability. First, mismatched block boundaries between teacher and student cause \textbf{\textit{context misalignment}}, providing distorted supervisory signals that misguide student decoding. Second, even under aligned contexts, an \textbf{\textit{intrinsic optimization bias}} in OPD, where the student tends to rapidly absorb high-support signals while lagging on low-support updates, drives a premature confidence surge that traps weaker students in catastrophic overconfidence collapse. To resolve these, we propose \mbox{\textbf{Un-OPD}}, an unbiased on-policy distillation framework with two novelties for stabilizing BDLM training. First, Un-OPD introduces a boundary-aware step filtering strategy that eliminates context-misaligned decoding steps. Second, Un-OPD proposes moderating optimization intensity at high-support positions via a support-rebalanced confidence calibration, thereby bypassing overconfidence collapse. Beyond stability, we also introduce a rollout reuse mechanism to reduce rollout generation overhead. Extensive experiments on math reasoning and code generation benchmarks show that Un-OPD consistently stabilizes training and delivers superior performance while reducing wall-clock training time by approximately half.
  </details>

- **[DischargeBench: Pre-Deployment Evaluation of LLM Discharge Educators through Persona-Grounded Patient Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20827)**  `arXiv:2609.20827`  `cs.CL`  
  _Won Seok Jang, Youngbin Jang, Chanyoung Ko, Hyunwook Kim, Zonghai Yao, Hong Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective discharge education is personalized: a clinician adapts the discharge plan to each patient's literacy, recall, and personality. Before large language models (LLMs) take on this role with real patients, they need to be evaluated in conversations with diverse patients, yet existing evaluations target static, artifact-generation, or question-answering tasks. We introduce **DischargeBench**, a pre-deployment benchmark in which a candidate LLM educator teaches a persona-grounded simulated patient in an open-ended, multi-turn conversation. We curate **MIMIC-IV-Ext-DischargeBench**, 320 cases across 16 ICD chapters, each paired with a patient persona that varies personality, education level, health literacy, and recall. Each conversation is scored on Conversation Quality, Topic Checklist Score (TCS), Simulated Comprehension Score (SCS), and a Claim-Level Safety Audit (CLSA) by an LLM judge validated against physician annotations. Across eight closed- and open-source LLMs, the best model scored only 0.70 on TCS and 0.72 on SCS, and distrustful and anxious patients lowered comprehension for every model. DischargeBench is intended as a screening step before human evaluation, not as evidence of readiness to educate patients.
  </details>

- **[$Φ$-Bench: Can Large Language Models Engineer the Infrastructure That Powers Them?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10226)**  `arXiv:2609.10226`  `cs.CL`  
  _Leilei Ding, Shumin Wang, Yuting Huang, Fanqi Wan, Yinmin Zhang, Qi Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) now show strong reasoning and coding abilities, raising the prospect that they could assist in developing and optimizing the very infrastructure that powers them. Existing benchmarks, however, mainly focus on isolated kernels, predefined operators, or pre-specified optimization targets, and cannot evaluate open-ended, long-horizon LLM infrastructure engineering. To address this gap, we present $\Phi$-Bench, a benchmark of 85 tasks grounded in real LLM training and inference codebases. The tasks cover nine categories of LLM infrastructure and come in three formats, ranging from kernel function completion to long-horizon implementation and repository-wide end-to-end optimization. They are built by a construction pipeline that turns real pull requests, issues, and repository code into executable tasks with validated tests, and every reward is gated by correctness and anti-hacking checks. Across eight frontier models, the strongest model, Claude Opus 5, scores only 36.53%, and every model performs worse on long-horizon, repository-level implementation than on kernel-level tasks. Trajectory analysis further suggests that stronger models treat optimization as controlled experimentation, validating ideas with cheap local runs and ruling out confounders before drawing conclusions. We will open-source $\Phi$-Bench to facilitate future research on LLM agents' ability to develop and optimize the LLM infrastructure that powers themselves.
  </details>

- **[NeoHorse-1: Towards Recursive Self-Improvement via Agentic Post-Training with Routing Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08183)**  `arXiv:2609.08183`  `cs.CL`  
  _NeoHorse Team, Guoliang Cao, Guohao Dai, Tianyu Guo, Kai Han, Hailin Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires a concrete mechanism through which an AI system observes its capabilities and converts that evidence into the next round of learning. We present NeoHorse-1, a family of agent-native models developed to explore this path through agentic post-training. Our system combines a heterogeneous model pool with intelligent routing, recording the predicted capability demand, selected service tier, and subsequent interaction for each user turn. These records are converted into training examples that preserve interleaved reasoning, tool calls, and harness context, and are admitted through structural validation, six-dimensional semantic evaluation, and subscene-level labeling. Routing signals organize supervised fine-tuning into a three-stage curriculum and extend to routing-guided on-policy distillation, where a teacher supervises student-generated responses under the same progression. Capability-guided allocation then converts evaluation feedback into the next training mixture, closing an evaluation-selection-update loop in which what the system learns to do shapes what it learns from next. Across eleven benchmarks covering harness-based agents, tool use, coding, and instruction following, post-training raises the macro-average from 58.94 to 64.87 at 4B and from 65.60 to 69.04 at 9B, substantially narrowing the aggregate gap between the post-trained 4B model and the 9B base model. We further adapt NeoHorse-1-4B into NeoHorse-Jev-4B, a prefill-only model that returns structured decisions and probabilities for agent workflows. NeoHorse-1 provides an initial prototype of this feedback-driven process and a path toward harness-mediated RSI across successive iterations.
  </details>

- **[Archer: Adaptive Reuse of Cached Hidden States for Efficient Rollback in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08086)**  `arXiv:2608.08086`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xuning He, Qingyuan Shi, Zinan Sheng, Yongding Tao, Huanyu Liu, Ge Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) can revise earlier predictions as context evolves, but rollback requires costly full-sequence recomputation. Conventional key-value (KV) caching assumes immutable historical states and is difficult to reconcile with such revision. We introduce Adaptive Reuse of Cached Hidden States for Efficient Rollback (Archer), a training-free caching method for rollback-capable DLMs. Archer recomputes the mutable response at every update while reusing prompt K/V within a bounded response-state neighborhood. This asymmetric boundary amortizes prompt computation without restricting rollback. Bounded reuse also delays feedback from tentative tokens while keeping response revision active. Our analysis characterizes the cache boundary, bounds state-dependent approximation error, and gives a decoder-margin condition for preserving full-refresh decisions. On code-generation benchmarks, Archer achieves the best mean performance of 33.63% with a 2.57x mean speedup, reaching 2.95x speedup on a single benchmark. On non-code benchmarks, Archer achieves up to 4.33x speedup over LLaDA-Confidence while improving average accuracy. Controlled analyses support the delayed prompt feedback mechanism. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[M$^3$R-Bench: A Unified Benchmark for Evidence-Grounded Multimodal Metaphor Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05817)**  `arXiv:2608.05817`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hong Jiang, Junnan Zhu, Jingwang Huang, Xiao Sun, Yuming Yang, Jiang Zhong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal metaphor understanding requires identifying cross-domain Target-Source mappings and inferring sentiment from visual and textual evidence. However, existing benchmarks largely evaluate these subtasks separately and lack evidence-grounded explanations, limiting their ability to assess coherent cross-modal reasoning. To enable unified and evidence-grounded evaluation, we introduce M$^3$R-Bench, a benchmark of 1,000 human-annotated image-text instances with joint labels for metaphor occurrence, Target-Source mapping, and sentiment, alongside stage-wise explanations for evidence identification, mapping establishment, and sentiment inference. Evaluations reveal that existing models frequently rely on textual shortcuts and produce Target-Source mappings inconsistent with visual evidence. To address this evidence-mapping mismatch, we propose M$^3$R-Reasoner, which combines curriculum-based reasoning supervision with task-aware reinforcement learning. Built on an 8B-parameter backbone, M$^3$R-Reasoner achieves 37.90% Joint Label Accuracy (JLA) metric, outperforming the strongest proprietary baseline by 19.50 percentage points. It further surpasses GPT-5.5 in image-text evidence and sentiment justification scores by 28.45 and 28.11 points, respectively. The dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Relevance-Centric Retrieval: Rubric-Oriented Document Set Selection and Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19747)**  `arXiv:2607.19747`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kailin Jiang, Lei Liu, Jian Xi, Hui Xu, Junlin Liu, Baochen Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models and AI agents become the primary consumers of search results, document set quality determines the upper bound of downstream generation. Yet existing evaluation systems remain confined to scoring documents independently and aggregating via nDCG, ignoring inter-document interactions (redundancy, conflict, complementarity) and unable to answer what makes one document set better than another. To address these issues, we propose a complete evaluate-diagnose-optimize framework. We design SetwiseEvalKit, a three-level, nine-dimension document set evaluation benchmark covering both short-form and long-form scenarios, comprising approximately 28K high-quality evaluation rubrics. We systematically evaluate 12 rerankers: even the best method achieves no more than 45% coverage, cross-document coordination dimensions are universally weak, and no single method maintains top performance across both settings. Building on this, we propose Rubric4Setwise, a training-free method that converts rubric-based evaluation criteria into document set selection signals, achieving the best downstream generation performance with fewer documents and search rounds. It is the only method that maintains state-of-the-art results across both scenarios, validating the effectiveness of closing the loop from evaluation to optimization.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Error from Known Fact: Step-Level Self-Consistency Group Relative Policy Optimization for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18915)**  `arXiv:2607.18915`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaomeng Hu, Jiaqi Hu, Hao Chen, Qi Zhang, Zhanming Shen, Wentao Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs), modern systems not only possess strong foundational capabilities and extensive knowledge, but can also solve complex problems via long, multi-step reasoning. However, as reasoning traces become longer, LLMs may produce a substantial amount of hallucinated content during the reasoning process, which is often difficult to detect. In this work, we conduct a fine-grained analysis of hallucinations arising in LLM reasoning and find that the reasoning traces are particularly prone to Context-Sensitive Factual Hallucinations: cases where the model actually has the relevant knowledge, yet makes factual errors due to contextual interference during reasoning. To address this issue, we propose Step-level Self-Consistency Group Relative Policy Optimization (SSC-GRPO), which assigns step-level rewards to reasoning traces by computing self-consistency scores of individual steps across multiple rollouts. Compared with prior methods, SSC-GRPO achieves state-of-the-art performance on both mathematical reasoning benchmarks and hallucination leaderboards. Our results offer a new perspective for detecting and mitigating hallucinations in the reasoning process of large language models.
  </details>

- **[Learning at the Right Pace: Adaptive Data Scheduling Improves LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22305)**  `arXiv:2606.22305`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zicheng Xu, Ruixuan Zhang, Yu-Neng Chuang, Xiuyi Lou, Hoang Anh Duy Le, Oren Gal, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve remarkable reasoning capabilities through reinforcement learning (RL) post-training. However, existing RL post-training commonly relies on uniform data sampling, which ignores the semantic structure of the training data and the evolving capability of the training policy. To address these limitations, we propose Adaptive Data Scheduling (ADS), a dual-level data scheduling framework that combines adaptive sampling across semantic clusters with policy-boundary sample selection. At the cluster level, ADS organizes samples according to semantic patterns and adapts the inter-cluster sampling distribution to prioritize clusters within the policy's current reach. At the sample level, ADS tracks prompts near the policy's capability boundary within each cluster to obtain informative relative advantages. Experimental results across three LLMs and seven reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ADS improves average accuracy by 5.5% over Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Notably, ADS consistently improves RL methods with different objective designs, highlighting its potential as a general data scheduling strategy. The source code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[SimCT: Recovering Lost Supervision for Cross-Tokenizer On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07711)**  `arXiv:2605.07711`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jie Sun, Mao Zheng, Mingyang Song, Qiyong Zhong, Yilin Cheng, Bichuan Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) is a standard tool for transferring teacher behavior to a smaller student, but it implicitly assumes that teacher and student predictions are comparable token by token, an assumption that fails whenever the two models tokenize the same text differently. Under heterogeneous tokenizers, exact shared-token matching silently discards a large fraction of the teacher signal at precisely the positions where vocabularies disagree. We propose \textbf{\underline{Sim}ple \underline{C}ross-\underline{T}okenizer OPD (SimCT)}, which restores this signal by enlarging the supervision space: alongside shared tokens, SimCT compares teacher and student over short multi-token continuations that both tokenizers can realize, leaving the OPD loss form itself unchanged. We show that these units are the finest jointly tokenizable supervision interface, and that coarser alternatives remove teacher-student distinctions that are useful for on-policy learning. Across three heterogeneous teacher-student pairs on mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, SimCT shows consistent gains over shared-vocabulary OPD and representative cross-tokenizer baselines, with ablations confirming that the improvements come from recovering supervision discarded by exact shared-token matching. Code is available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[What Makes Good Instruction-Tuning Data? An In-Context Learning Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25132)**  `arXiv:2604.25132`  `cs.CL`  
  _Guangzeng Han, Xiaolei Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction-tuning datasets often contain substantial redundancy and low-quality samples, necessitating effective data selection methods. We propose an instruction data selection framework based on weighted in-context influence (wICI), which measures how effectively each candidate example reduces instruction-following difficulty for semantically related peers. Through systematic experiments, we address three key questions: what constitutes effective instruction tuning data from an in-context perspective, whether sample difficulty correlates with in-context influence, and how in-context influence translates to instruction tuning effectiveness. Experiments across multiple models and benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms existing baselines under constrained data budgets, while empirically showing that sample difficulty negatively correlates with in-context influence.
  </details>

- **[MIXAR: Scaling Autoregressive Pixel-based Language Models to Multiple Languages and Scripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11575)**  `arXiv:2604.11575`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chen Hu, Yintao Tai, Antonio Vergari, Frank Keller, Alessandro Suglia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pixel-based language models are gaining momentum as alternatives to traditional token-based approaches, promising to circumvent tokenization challenges; however, existing models remain English-centric and are hard to train as generators. This paper introduces MIXAR, the first generative multilingual pixel-based language model, pretrained on eight high-resource languages spanning Latin and CJK scripts and scaled up to 0.5B parameters. Although pretrained on high-resource languages only, MIXAR generalizes to unseen low-resource languages better than a tokenizer-based model pretrained on the same corpus, with its largest gains on the scripts most distant from the pretraining mix, taking a step toward more equitable language technology. To make free generation work in pixel space, MIXAR replaces the notoriously unstable adversarial training stage of prior pixel-based models with a GRPO-based reinforcement-learning stage that rewards the model on its own free-running generations: it proves more stable and multiplies free-generation accuracy by up to 2.3x on multilingual benchmarks. Finally, MIXAR performs on par with comparable tokenizer-based models on discriminative benchmarks, improves consistently with scale, and withstands orthographic attacks more gracefully than tokenizer-based models. Code and models are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Explanation Generation for Contradiction Reconciliation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22735)**  `arXiv:2603.22735`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jason Chan, Zhixue Zhao, Robert Gaizauskas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing NLP work commonly treats contradictions as errors to be resolved by choosing which statements to accept or discard. Yet a key aspect of human reasoning in social and professional settings is the ability to hypothesize explanations that reconcile contradictions. For example, "Cassie hates coffee" and "She buys coffee everyday" appear contradictory, but are in fact compatible if Cassie was assigned the daily chore of buying coffee for all her coworkers. Despite advances in the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), their ability to hypothesize such explanations remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, we introduce the task of reconciliatory explanation generation, where models must generate explanations that effectively render contradictory statements compatible. We propose a novel method of reusing natural language inference (NLI) datasets, and introduce quality metrics that enable scalable automatic evaluation. Experiments with 13 LLMs in thinking and non-thinking modes reveal models' limited success in this task, with longer reasoning chains negatively associated with explanation success. As a proof of concept, we further adapt our introduced task and metrics for reinforcement-learning training, finding that trained models exhibit improved accuracy in non-thinking mode on two narrative reasoning benchmarks (True Detective and MuSR). Our findings highlight reconciliatory explanation generation as a challenge in LLM reasoning and motivate further work to investigate its potential for improving model capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Writer-R1: Dynamic Rubric Mining for Reinforcement Learning of Open-Ended Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15061)**  `arXiv:2603.15061`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jihao Zhao, Shuaishuai Zu, Zhiyuan Ji, Chunlai Zhou, Biao Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training models for open-ended writing requires diverse prompts and sufficiently precise specifications of response quality. Constructing such specifications at scale entails recovering task requirements, consolidating overlapping criteria, and defining observable differences between performance levels. We introduce Writer-R1, a writing training pipeline built around Inductive Rubric Generation (IRG), which integrates these operations into an automated rubric construction procedure. The pipeline expands an open writing corpus and uses the resulting prompt-specific rubrics throughout supervised training and reinforcement learning (RL). Each rubric is concatenated with the user request and also defines the reward criteria, making the requirements used for assessment available to the writer before generation. A compact model, RubricGen-4B, learns to supply rubrics for new requests. Writer-R1 improves WritingBench scores by 0.182 and 0.158 over the 4B and 8B backbones, respectively, with gains also on HelloBench. Training ablations favor IRG over direct rubric generation, and further analyses support rubric transfer and the robustness of writer improvements across evaluators. These results support using inductively mined rubrics as both generation guidance and reward specifications for open-ended writing.
  </details>

- **[BASIS: Evaluating Belief-Aware Solutions in Interactive Settings beyond Instructions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13832)**  `arXiv:2602.13832`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minyuan Ruan, Ziyue Wang, Kaiming Liu, Yunghwei Lai, Peng Li, Yang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly capable of carrying out complex, long-horizon tasks, yet effective assistance requires more than faithfully executing user instructions. Users' requests reflect their current understanding of the problem, which may be incomplete, outdated, or incorrect. When this understanding diverges from the actual task state, literal instruction following can fail to accomplish the user's underlying goal. We introduce \textbf{BASIS} (\emph{\textbf{B}elief-\textbf{A}ware \textbf{S}olutions in \textbf{I}nteractive \textbf{S}ettings}), a benchmark that evaluates whether agents can infer the mental states underlying user requests, recognize belief-reality mismatches, and use this understanding to develop appropriate solutions. Rather than assessing mental-state inference solely through explicit questions, \textbf{BASIS} evaluates both the utility of such inferences and the quality of downstream solutions in user-agent interactions. Evaluation results show that current models often fail to account for users' mental states when generating solutions, and that solution quality is consistently higher when such information is explicitly elicited and made available during problem solving. We further investigate the practical value of mental-state consideration through comparisons with generic Chain-of-Thought prompting, controlled interventions on mental-state information, targeted training, and multi-agent collaboration experiments under partial observability. Together, these studies highlight the importance of not merely inferring others' mental states, but also using those understandings to provide grounded, goal-directed assistance.
  </details>

- **[MoCo: A One-Stop Shop for Model Collaboration Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21257)**  `arXiv:2601.21257`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shangbin Feng, Yuyang Bai, Ziyuan Yang, Yike Wang, Zhaoxuan Tan, Jiajie Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advancing beyond single monolithic language models (LMs), recent research increasingly recognizes the importance of model collaboration, where multiple LMs collaborate, compose, and complement each other. Existing research on this topic has mostly been disparate and disconnected, from different research communities, and lacks rigorous comparison. To consolidate existing research and establish model collaboration as a school of thought, we present MoCo: a one-stop Python library of executing, benchmarking, and comparing model collaboration algorithms at scale. MoCo features 26 model collaboration methods, spanning diverse levels of cross-model information exchange such as routing, text, logit, and model parameters. MoCo integrates 25 evaluation datasets spanning reasoning, QA, code, safety, and more, while users could flexibly bring their own data. Extensive experiments with MoCo demonstrate that most collaboration strategies outperform models without collaboration in 61.0% of (model, data) settings on average, with the most effective methods outperforming by up to 25.8%. We further analyze the scaling of model collaboration strategies, the training/inference efficiency of diverse methods, highlight that the collaborative system solves problems where single LMs struggle, and discuss future work in model collaboration, all made possible by MoCo. We envision MoCo as a valuable toolkit to facilitate and turbocharge the quest for an open, modular, decentralized, and collaborative AI future.
  </details>

- **[EnsembleLink: Accurate Record Linkage Without Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21138)**  `arXiv:2601.21138`  `cs.CL`  
  _Noah Dasanaike_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Record linkage, the process of matching records that refer to the same entity across datasets, is essential to empirical social science but remains methodologically underdeveloped. Researchers treat it as a preprocessing step, applying ad hoc rules without quantifying the uncertainty that linkage errors introduce into downstream analyses. Existing methods either achieve low accuracy or require substantial labeled training data. I present EnsembleLink, a method that achieves high accuracy without any training labels. EnsembleLink leverages pre-trained language models that have learned semantic relationships (e.g., that "South Ozone Park" is a neighborhood in "New York City" or that "Lutte ouvriere" refers to the Trotskyist "Workers' Struggle" party) from large text corpora. On benchmarks spanning city names, person names, organizations, multilingual political parties, and bibliographic records, EnsembleLink matches or exceeds methods requiring extensive labeling. The method runs locally on open-source models, requiring no external API calls, and completes typical linkage tasks in minutes.
  </details>

- **[MBBQ: A Dataset for Cross-Lingual Comparison of Stereotypes in Generative LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07243)**  `arXiv:2406.07243`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vera Neplenbroek, Arianna Bisazza, Raquel Fernández_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative large language models (LLMs) have been shown to exhibit harmful biases and stereotypes. While safety fine-tuning typically takes place in English, if at all, these models are being used by speakers of many different languages. There is existing evidence that the performance of these models is inconsistent across languages and that they discriminate based on demographic factors of the user. Motivated by this, we investigate whether the social stereotypes exhibited by LLMs differ as a function of the language used to prompt them, while controlling for cultural differences and task accuracy. To this end, we present MBBQ (Multilingual Bias Benchmark for Question-answering), a carefully curated version of the English BBQ dataset extended to Dutch, Spanish, and Turkish, which measures stereotypes commonly held across these languages. We further complement MBBQ with a parallel control dataset to measure task performance on the question-answering task independently of bias. Our results based on several open-source and proprietary LLMs confirm that some non-English languages suffer from bias more than English, even when controlling for cultural shifts. Moreover, we observe significant cross-lingual differences in bias behaviour for all except the most accurate models. With the release of MBBQ, we hope to encourage further research on bias in multilingual settings. The dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Reward Improvement via Adversarial Meta-Prompt Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21040)**  `arXiv:2610.21040`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Wei Dai, Qianchu Liu, Guanghui Qin, Xing Han, Tristan Naumann, Paul Pu Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) has gained wide attention for its ability to work with limited data. In a proposer-solver loop, a proposer model writes training tasks and a solver model learns to solve them through reinforcement learning. Such loops have succeeded in mathematics and coding, where every answer can be checked automatically. Open-ended expert tasks, like clinical report generations, have no answer key, which makes simple question-answer generation not viable. Here the proposer must also write a rubric that lists the content an answer should include and the errors it should avoid. However, the unique challenge behind model-written rubric is the comprehensiveness of it. For naively generated rubric, solvers tend to exploit it, naming the expected topics without the substance behind them, which corrupts the learning process. To solve this problem, we introduce RRI, Recursive Reward Improvement, which uses a reward hacking adversary to supervise the reward. Before the solver trains on a generated task, an adversary writes an answer that games its rubric while being as useless as possible, the same model writes an honest answer without seeing the rubric, and up to two repair steps revise the rubric to make the honest answer win. The exploits found also rewrite how future tasks and rubrics are generated. On HealthBench Professional, RRI improves the adjusted score over the Generation Only self-improvement baseline by 10.9 points at 9B and 12.4 at 27B, and by 10.3 points without any external model. In particular, the gains are largest on difficult and writing tasks. We show that the 27B model trained on the HealthBench Professional description improves by 6.6 points on PRBench Hard and 8.0 on ProfBench without seeing a task from either. We further analyze the exploitable points of model-generated question-rubric pairs to facilitate future research on open-ended self-play.
  </details>

- **[When Reality Outpaces Cognition: A New Scaling Principle for Reliable Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20764)**  `arXiv:2610.20764`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Ahsan Adeel, Talha Bin Riaz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper challenges the understanding capabilities of existing AI paradigms and emerging world models by asking a fundamental question: How quickly can reality change before an intelligent system can no longer reliably perceive, reason, and act? We formulate this as a theory- and model-agnostic challenge in which systems must infer reliable actions for increasingly distant future states across a growing observation--reality gap. We operationalize this challenge through the effective Speed of Coherent Cognition (\(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\)), defined as the highest tested Speed of Reality (\(S_R\)) through which a system continuously satisfies a predefined behavioural reliability criterion from baseline. As a proof of concept, reinforcement-learning agents are trained at the baseline \(S_R\) and evaluated, without retraining, as \(S_R\) is increased by up to \(50\times\) while computational resources remain fixed. We compare Transformer and gated-MLP baselines with \(Co^4\), an architecture inspired by cellular mechanisms implicated in conscious processing and awake thought. Across the tested scenarios, \(Co^4\) achieves substantially higher \(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\) than the baselines, motivating \(S_C^{\mathrm{eff}}\) as a new benchmark and the scaling of coherent cognition as a principle for reliable AI.
  </details>

- **[OntoAct: Hypothesis-Based Neuro-Symbolic Agents through Ontological Abstraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21983)**  `arXiv:2610.21983`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Donghyeok Lee, Bong Gyun Kang, Junyong Ahn, Sungroh Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning from experience requires recovering reusable knowledge and determining when it applies to a new situation. We introduce OntoAct, a hypothesis-based neuro-symbolic framework built on ontological abstraction. OntoAct turns individual experiences into axioms over entity types, relations, and conditions, making their underlying patterns reusable across situations. A large language model (LLM) uses this world knowledge to propose hypotheses and actions, while a symbolic verifier assesses their supporting conditions against current observations. Verification identifies missing evidence, and new observations and intervention outcomes update hypothesis assessments and guide subsequent actions. We also introduce Tacit-FLE, a benchmark built on the Factorio Learning Environment (FLE) for diagnosing and repairing hidden faults in existing factories using inherited operational records. Its synthetic maintenance corpus distributes evidence across records, and an executed repair may leave the fault unresolved, requiring agents to reassess their diagnoses through interaction. Across Tacit-FLE, Mars, and ALFWorld, OntoAct achieves leading task performance with efficient interaction and inference.
  </details>

- **[CIM-Embed: Learning Instruction-Conditioned Multimodal Embeddings for Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21805)**  `arXiv:2610.21805`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yinghua Xu, Pengyang Xie, Yanxiang Zhou, Chuanzhi Tong, Xiao Xiao, Chaoqun Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal objects can require different partitions under different instructions, as when products in an e-commerce catalog are grouped by color, material, or both. Retrieval-oriented embeddings may not recover these partitions, while generative clustering often relies on intermediate descriptions or iterative decisions. This paper presents CIM-Embed, a framework for learning instruction-conditioned multimodal embeddings for clustering. Supervised contrastive learning uses same-group positives and negatives from other groups, optionally including other tasks; heterogeneous structured batches accommodate tasks with different group counts. At inference, the model maps each object's image, text, and instruction directly to an embedding; HDBSCAN then forms a partition without the ground-truth cluster count. We construct ProductMIC from product metadata in Amazon Reviews 2023, comprising training data and an evaluation benchmark for instruction-conditioned clustering. The benchmark contains 5,373 products and 449 single-attribute and compositional grouping tasks spanning 50 leaf categories excluded from our training. With full-dimensional embeddings and HDBSCAN, direct within-task SupCon training achieves a mean per-task V-measure of 95.01, compared with 53.06 for WeMM 9B, the strongest general-purpose embedding baseline evaluated. Frozen product-trained encoders also improve out-of-domain conditional grouping, while controlled transfer experiments distinguish partition transfer from instruction response. We will release the code and dataset upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[ClaimScope: Label-Blind Witness Verification for Mathematical Claim Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21581)**  `arXiv:2610.21581`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Mushir Akhtar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mathematical agents are commonly evaluated by answer accuracy or proof acceptance. We introduce ClaimScope, a 72-task benchmark for auditing proposed claims through a verdict, scope, counterexample, and repair. Tasks cover optimizer-state transport, finite-horizon affine recurrences, and exact $2\times2$ linear algebra. We evaluate Qwen3-8B with direct generation and four matched two-pass conditions, each starting from the same draft. A witness checker evaluates only the model's submitted state, without consulting reference verdicts or supplying a replacement witness. Its feedback still conveys information about validity: a verified refutation establishes that the claim is false, and rejection reasons can suggest structural fixes. In this evaluation, verifier feedback yields $23/39$ accepted counterexamples on invalid claims and $25/72$ safe completions, compared with $15/39$ and $20/72$ for checker-free generic feedback. Strict completion remains $17/72$; verdict accuracy changes from $47/72$ to $46/72$. The claim-grouped safe-completion difference is $.094$, with a bootstrap 95\% interval of $[.000,.198]$. All seven task-level gains follow horizon/input-length diagnostics. A post-hoc schema-only correction of the direct outputs reaches the same 25 safe tasks without another model call. These results separate claim classification from executable evidence, while locating the observed gains in witness-structure correction rather than demonstrating broader reasoning improvement.
  </details>

- **[Probe-and-Resume: When and How to Evaluate Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21088)**  `arXiv:2610.21088`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiakai Xu, Tiancheng Ge, Tujie Guan, Yusheng Li, Rohan Timmaraju, Eugene Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time search can improve agent performance by exploring alternative actions and using a critic to select promising branches. We study this approach in terminal-based computer-use environments, which expose only a partial view of a large underlying state, and find that the Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) configuration we evaluate does not significantly improve task success. We identify two sources of poor critic performance: the agent's observations omit environment state needed for evaluation, and intermediate states offer limited evidence of whether a trajectory will succeed. At intermediate states, critics rank continuations only modestly better than chance, and environment access improves this little. We study when to invoke a critic and what access to give it to evaluate a node. Our findings motivate a policy that invokes the critic only at nodes the agent believes are final, rather than during partial progress. Further, the policy gives the critic read, write, and execute access to a fork of the computer's current and initial state, allowing it to probe and perform potentially destructive actions to surface any hidden state the trajectory omits. We call the resulting design Probe-and-Resume: when the agent claims to be done, the critic probes a fork of its state, and a rejected agent resumes where it stopped. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, this raises success by 8.4 percentage points over MCTS with conventional critics, and approaches the success rate of the Codex CLI agent with the same underlying model at less than half its cost.
  </details>

- **[AlphaDiana: A Measurement System for Agents in Verifiable Reasoning Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20675)**  `arXiv:2610.20675`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhanke Zhou, Zongze Li, Weikai Huang, Xuan Li, Chentao Cao, Xiao Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents pair reasoning models with harnesses that organize model calls, execute tools, load skill instructions, and retain history in memory. Understanding agent performance requires linking execution conditions to outcomes, behavior, and resource use. We introduce AlphaDiana, a measurement system with modular interfaces for datasets, agents, environments, and scorers. It preserves harness-native control loops while controlling file reuse and memory retention separately. AlphaDiana supports macro analyses of complete agents and micro interventions on agent components such as tools, skills, and memory under matched conditions. We study three open-source models and three open-source harnesses on five answer-based benchmarks, with two models also tested on two interactive benchmarks. We find that harnesses do not consistently improve the accuracy of reasoning: gains on questions unsolved by the model alone can be outweighed by losses on questions it answers correctly without tools. This inconsistency extends to component interventions: neither skill guidance nor retained history reliably improves accuracy. Behavior and output-token use must be assessed with correctness: low token entropy can accompany wrong answers, and less output per execution can mean more output tokens per correct answer when accuracy falls. AlphaDiana links answer correctness and execution status to traces and resource records for comparing agents and diagnosing failures under shared task and resource policies.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Model Capabilities After Benchmark Saturation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20667)**  `arXiv:2610.20667`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Morio Hamada, Jehyeok Yeon, Matteo Migliarini, Justin Olive, Magda Dubois_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rapid advances in AI capabilities are causing benchmarks to saturate. Once several models approach perfect scores, a benchmark loses its power to differentiate between them, and with it its utility for informing AI safety cases and frontier policies. Yet benchmark items produce far more information than the scores we typically extract from them: a full transcript of how each model solved the task. We investigate whether these transcripts can be used to assess model capabilities even after benchmark scores have saturated. We simulate saturation retrospectively using historical benchmark and model releases, and test whether protocols that access only correctly solved transcripts from saturated benchmarks can predict capabilities measured on held-out, unsaturated items. We evaluate representative contemporary approaches for extracting information from model transcripts: pairwise judging, single-transcript absolute scoring, and predictions from deterministic transcript features or embeddings. We find that saturated transcripts retain capability information beyond benchmark accuracy. Amongst our evaluated protocols, pairwise judging performs best, reducing prediction error by 5.3 times compared to a baseline using only saturated benchmark scores. This capability-relevant information is distributed across transcript sections, but concentrated in specific channels of information such as reasoning and tool-calls. However, the predictive power of protocols does not always generalise across distribution shifts -- for example by underestimating the capabilities of the first reasoning models introduced in early 2025. Saturated benchmarks thus retain capability-predicting signal in their task transcripts, but extending the lifetime of existing benchmarks requires methods designed to extract these signals robustly.
  </details>

- **[Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20597)**  `arXiv:2610.20597`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haofei Yu, Yixing Chen, Yining Zhao, Xuyan Ye, Yuxiang Liu, Zhigang Hua, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $\tau^2$-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MSP: Modality Self-Play for Training Multimodal LLMs Without Paired Cross-Modal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20965)**  `arXiv:2610.20965`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Robert Moseley, Md Kaykobad Reza, Ameya Patil, Edward Ayrapetian, Salman Asif_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal models have achieved remarkable progress by scaling model capacity and training on massive paired multimodal data. However, this paradigm introduces a fundamental bottleneck: aligned multimodal datasets are expensive to curate, difficult to scale across diverse modality combinations, and increasingly constrain further progress. We introduce Modality Self-Play (MSP), a training framework for multimodal foundation models based on proxy-mediated modality composition. Instead of relying on paired cross-modal data, MSP uses anchor-supporting proxy textual descriptions of unseen modalities as semantic bridges during training. Given an observed modality (e.g., image or audio), the model generates proxy descriptions for complementary unseen modalities, which are then encoded and aligned with the observed modality through learned bridges, enabling cross-modal interaction without real paired non-text supervision.Our central hypothesis is that proxy text, combined with contrastively trained pretrained encoders, provides sufficient semantic structure for multimodal composition to emerge. We show that MSP enables zero-shot modality composition: models trained only with a real anchor modality and accompanying proxy descriptions can integrate and reason over multiple modalities at inference time despite never observing real paired non-text data during training. Empirically, MSP achieves state-of-the-art or competitive performance on multiple multimodal benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate that learned bridges improve alignment, while anchor-supporting proxy descriptions enable effective cross-modal composition. MSP removes the need for real paired non-text (e.g., audio-vision) supervision, while continuing to rely on modality-text pairs, pretrained contrastive encoders, and modality-distribution bridge calibration.
  </details>

- **[Temporal Specification Guidance for Text-to-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20822)**  `arXiv:2610.20822`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Avishree Khare, Hideki Okamoto, Bardh Hoxha, Georgios Fainekos, Rajeev Alur_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-video models often generate the visual concepts requested in natural language prompts but fail to satisfy their intended ordering, timing, and persistence requirements. We propose representing these requirements as specifications in a formal Temporal Logic, and introduce Temporal Specification Guidance (TSG), a training-free inference-time framework that turns temporal specifications into executable objectives for guiding video generation during sampling. TSG scores traces of visual concept evidence using differentiable quantitative semantics and uses the score's gradients to construct a specification-guided denoising direction. We instantiate this framework with video-text attention traces, efficient differentiable scores for Metric Temporal Logic, and gradient-derived attention biases. This instantiation requires neither an external concept classifier nor backpropagation through the video transformer. Its attention-steered forward pass yields a denoising direction that augments classifier-free guidance (CFG). On a benchmark of 220 prompts spanning 11 categories of temporal constraints, including ordered object entry and state transitions, this instantiation improves specification satisfaction accuracy over CFG by 9.5-20 percentage points across five models, with minimal memory overhead. These results demonstrate the potential of temporal specifications and differentiable satisfaction objectives as a flexible interface for controlling video generation.
  </details>

- **[Bias Control and Injection for Multi-Agent Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21290)**  `arXiv:2610.21290`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Sungjae Lee, Soheil Abbasloo, Jabin Koo, Jungseul Ok_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent reasoning with large language models (LLMs) raises a central question for collective decision-making: when agents disagree, whose decision should be trusted? Resolving such disagreements is difficult because agents' decisions are susceptible to non-semantic biases such as majority pressure and response-style preferences, which can obscure the quality of the underlying reasoning. Our analysis shows how bias can be both mitigated and exploited: bias control improves initial answer selection, while bias injection elicits decision instability that serves as a reliability signal. Building on these findings, we introduce a test-time aggregation framework that requires neither validation data nor prior knowledge of relative model strength. The framework implements bias control by presenting one style-normalized reasoning trace per distinct answer, and bias injection by placing each aggregating agent's initially selected answer in the minority. Overall, our framework achieves higher average accuracy and better token efficiency than competing non-oracle aggregation methods across three mathematical reasoning benchmarks and four heterogeneous LLM combinations. It also improves final-answer accuracy when applied to multi-agent debate and Mixture-of-Agents, without modifying their interaction or synthesis processes.
  </details>

- **[DeciCAD: Harness-Grounded Learning of Action Policies for CAD Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21755)**  `arXiv:2610.21755`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Yufeng Peng, Yixin Zhang, Ziyi Liu, Zhikun Xu, Jiarong Yang, Sijie Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model agents operate through closed-loop action policies whose decision space is defined by an agent harness. We investigate how this operating space shapes agent behavior and policy learning in computer-aided design (CAD), a professional domain characterized by persistent state, geometric constraints, and long-horizon procedures. We instantiate this perspective in DeciCAD, a domain-native harness that exposes semantic CAD actions, decision-oriented observations, environment feedback, and persistent state transitions. Building on this harness, we develop a learning pipeline that extracts causal decision supervision from interaction trajectories and improves the policy through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and geometry-grounded online reinforcement learning (RL). Across four CAD benchmarks, DeciCAD achieves the best F1 and intersection over union (IoU) while reducing model-output tokens by 45--88% relative to a general-purpose coding harness. Under matched supervision, its SFT policy improves F1 from 0.6883 to 0.7437 and outperforms the coding policy; subsequent online RL further raises geometry availability from 66.5% to 73.5% and F1 to 0.7880. These results demonstrate that the decision space induced by a harness materially affects both agent execution and policy learning in CAD.
  </details>

- **[MLExpert: Towards a Generalist Agentic Model for Autonomous Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22344)**  `arXiv:2610.22344`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shangheng Du, Jinxin Shi, Shuaiyu Zhang, Yiming Wu, Junpeng Zhao, Shiyang Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are expanding from assisting scientific research and engineering to autonomously developing and improving AI systems, advancing AI for AI (AI4AI) as a pathway toward recursive self-improvement (RSI).
