Workflow-driven machine learning engineering (MLE) systems provide a practical path toward this pursuit through complex orchestration that manages long-horizon experimentation, while recent MLE systems adopt model-directed interaction, allowing models to autonomously organize experiments and adapt optimization strategies based on execution feedback. However, feedback from each experiment reflects several preceding decisions, and a long MLE run contains many such costly experiments. Training must therefore select useful supervision from long interaction trajectories and learn from intermediate outcomes. We propose a post-training method for autonomous long-horizon MLE optimization and apply it to train MLExpert, a 35B Mixture-of-Experts agentic model. We design the Kaggle-MLE Factory to construct executable tasks and an interaction harness for model-directed experimentation. Hierarchical agentic SFT transfers general tool-use and coding capabilities to MLE through outcome-guided supervision. Windowed Interaction RL (WIRL) treats the multi-turn decisions between successive experiment results as an execution window and assigns it a shared outcome-based reward. Asynchronous collection and policy correction support policy updates before the trajectory ends. On MLE-Bench Lite, MLExpert improves the average medal rate of the base model from 37.9% to 72.7%, achieving performance comparable to larger frontier models such as Kimi-K3 and GLM-5.3. Further experiments show that MLExpert improves performance on NatureBench Lite, agent scaffold optimization, and algorithm optimization, suggesting that the learned optimization capability generalizes to other tasks.
  </details>

- **[LEV: Thinking Fast and Deep with Looped Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20951)**  `arXiv:2610.20951`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Dingjie Song, Zhiling Yan, Yuxuan Zhang, Lichao Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models answer typed questions about an input with one probability per option, computed in a single forward pass without generating text. Like the fast System 1 of dual-process theory, they answer at once, but their depth is fixed at training time, so they spend the same computation on easy and hard questions. Models that reason in generated text can think longer, but they pay for every extra step in decoded tokens. We present LEV, a decision model that thinks deeper by rerunning its layers in a loop instead of generating tokens. LEV builds on a language model pretrained as a loop, reads the options after every loop step and trains all steps jointly, so one checkpoint can answer at any depth. On three looped backbones, accuracy rises with depth on the same weights, mostly on hard questions, and a 2.6B LEV goes from 63.2% at depth one to 76.2% at depth four on JevBench. On-demand depth, set per question by a small stopper with a threshold fitted on unlabelled requests, saves 15% to 24% of the computation at a cost of under one accuracy point, level with or slightly above a fixed mix of depths. On a locked test set, the 1.4B and 3B models are 5.4 to 8.8 points more accurate than dense (non-looped) models and cascades of the same latency below 50 ms.
  </details>

- **[ContinualSkillBench: Can LLM Agents Truly Evolve Their Capabilities?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03874)**  `arXiv:2608.03874`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Tianyi Guan, Yiding Wang, Haotong Yang, Siyuan Cao, Shirui Liu, Yi Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern agent frameworks equip large language models with external skill libraries to solve complex tasks. However, it remains unclear whether these systems can effectively evolve their skills and whether the resulting skills improve task-solving capabilities. To bridge this gap, we introduce ContinualSkillBench, a dynamic evaluation framework for in-context continual skill learning. It covers five domains, each with 100 tasks ordered by difficulty and opportunities for skill transfer. Our experiments show that sequential execution generally improves performance, but the gains vary substantially across models and domains. Moreover, across two models and three domains, retaining context and feedback without skill maintenance yields nearly the same mean performance as maintaining an explicit skill library. Explicit skills nevertheless provide selective benefits for tasks requiring reusable procedures or precise outputs. We further find that the weaker model (GPT-4o) accumulates more skills but reuses them less often, suggesting difficulty consolidating task-specific experience into reusable procedures. These findings show that current in-context skill evolution mechanisms can support continual adaptation, but still struggle to consistently consolidate experience into robust and transferable skills.
  </details>

- **[DocPilot: Interactive Agent Orchestration and Training for Long Visual Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21327)**  `arXiv:2610.21327`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Zhuowen Liang, Xiaotian Lin, Yuyu Luo, Haixun Wang, Nan Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal long-document question answering requires models to seek sparse evidence across pages and modalities, understand heterogeneous document content, and reason over the collected evidence. Even advanced vision-language models (VLMs) can overlook sparse evidence and struggle with cross-modal reasoning in a single pass. Existing document agents further introduce multi-step interaction, but some still rely on predefined workflows or coarse-grained actions, limiting the flexibility and granularity of document reasoning. We introduce DocPilot, an agentic document framework that supports expressive orchestration and makes complex document solving learnable by small language models (SLMs). DocPilot orchestrates evidence seeking through page-level navigation and fine-grained evidence localization, and integrates local visual inspection, code-based numerical reasoning, and cross-modal reasoning for multimodal understanding. To transfer these capabilities to small models, we propose curriculum-based agentic training that progresses from single-ability learning to adaptive orchestration through supervision from data-grounded reasoning traces and executable interaction trajectories. Across MMLongBench-Doc, DocBench, FinRAGBench-V, and MADQA, DocPilot achieves strong gains with frontier VLM backbones, while DocPilot-4B delivers competitive performance against substantially larger models, demonstrating strong effectiveness and generalization across diverse long-document reasoning settings. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HTN-Orchestrate: A Hierarchical Task Network Framework for Multi-Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22195)**  `arXiv:2610.22195`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yanchen Deng, Jianguo Huang, Yao Long Teng, Yixuan Li, Handuo Zhang, Ning Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Orchestrating multiple agents on a complex task requires reconciling local subtask execution with global task planning. Dynamic dispatch approaches provide execution flexibility but do not explicitly model subtask hierarchies or dependencies, while predefined workflows impose this structure but fix the decomposition before execution begins. Therefore, we introduce HTN-Orchestrate, a two-layer framework that separates these responsibilities through a hierarchical task network (HTN). The Planner maintains subtask hierarchies and dependencies, deferring decomposition until prerequisite results become available, while local Orchestrators schedule workers to execute each leaf subtask. Results propagate upward under \textbf{AND} semantics, and accumulated findings inform a rebuilt task network when the current decomposition cannot resolve the task. On five benchmarks spanning web research, scientific reasoning, and research workflows, HTN-Orchestrate outperforms ReAct, Agent Team, and DeepSeek Harness using the same base model, achieving 89.73% accuracy on BrowseComp and 73.44% on SUPERChem, exceeding Agent Team by 2.45 and 4.69 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[AutoSFT: An Agentic System for Automated Data Research in Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22194)**  `arXiv:2610.22194`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bingxiang He, Pengqi Sun, Shihao Peng, Shaoxuan Li, Huiyu Xiao, Hongjian Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language model agents increasingly automate post-training, but when given the full training stack they converge on supervised fine-tuning and spend most effort revising training data. Existing systems give the agent one kind of control, selecting rows from a fixed pool or calling a generator, whereas a human data engineer adjusts proportions, prunes, repairs, replaces and synthesizes the data. We design a data action space for these changes and ask whether an automated system can make the full range rather than one of them, and what structure that requires. We first run this loop with a single agent, and it reaches only one action, adjusting category proportions, while its context fills with execution output and is discarded mid-search. We therefore introduce \textbf{AutoSFT}, a multi-agent system where a meta agent separates execution from analysis, gathers proposals from independent angles, and calls a synthesis subagent for capabilities the pool cannot cover, with the model, the trainer, and the budget fixed within each comparison. Adding each component in turn helps: the executor halves context loss, the analysis subagents lift the best IFEval by $+6.5$, and synthesis adds $+3.4$ IFEval and $+2.9$ IFBench. The same loop transfers to code, where analysis adds $+3.7$ MBPP+ over a single agent that does not improve. Every result comes from runs that retrain from the base model each round, so each component's effect is individually attributable. Our findings suggest that automating SFT data research depends more on covering diverse data actions than on a stronger single action.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Escalation Reliability in Healthcare LLM Agents: A Perturbation-Controlled Benchmark of Workflow-State Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21270)**  `arXiv:2610.21270`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zihao Zheng, Junyi Yao, Baichuan Li, Jiayu Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trustworthy biomedical AI requires reliable action selection when evidence is missing, stale, conflicting, or late, not only accurate answers on static inputs. We instantiate an episode-level evaluation protocol as 400 synthetic healthcare workflow episodes: 40 hand-authored semantic seeds across four task classes, two surface parameterizations, and five controlled variants, scored on ANSWER/ASK CLARIFICATION/ABSTAIN/ESCALATE under a pre-declared 5:1 missed-versus-unnecessary escalation cost. Reference actions are single-author study-policy labels, not clinician-adjudicated ground truth. Across five open-weight configurations (1.7B--9.7B), clean decision accuracy reaches 0.97, yet uncertainty-prompted models miss 32--97% of required escalations. The stronger 9.7B configuration narrows but does not remove the gap, falling from 0.97 clean accuracy to 0.78 under conflict. Verbalized confidence is inconsistent (ECE 0.10--0.54; failure AUROC 0.48--0.77), and a development-tuned confidence-only policy yields no utility gain. A transparent workflow-aware controller improves 5:1 utility for every model and reduces missed escalations to 0.12--0.24, at specificity 0.83--0.93. However, a 30-concept lexical stress test reduces each rule detector's recall from 1.00 on trigger phrases to 0.00 on unseen paraphrases, limiting the controller claim to this benchmark. Seed-clustered statistics, ablations, cross-task transfer, raw outputs, and manifests are packaged for audit. The results motivate explicit workflow-state evaluation while showing that apparently effective routing controls need their own generalization tests.
  </details>

- **[MemComposer: Learning to Assemble Memories for LLM Agents via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21091)**  `arXiv:2610.21091`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yixuan Xie, Xueqiang Xu, Xinyi Fan, Xinxi Lyu, Tao Feng, Xing Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent memory enables large language model (LLM) agents to reuse past experiences without updating their parameters. However, semantic similarity alone does not guarantee memory usefulness: downstream performance depends on how memories are selected and presented to the agent. We propose MemComposer, a memory composer that jointly examines the current query and retrieved candidates to decide which experiences to include, how many to select, and how to order them. We train the composer using Experience Policy Optimization (ExPO), a GRPO-based framework that evaluates assembled memory contexts through complete agent interactions and uses task-success feedback to optimize the assembly policy. Across three $\tau^2$-bench domains with two frozen executors, MemComposer outperforms the strongest baseline for each executor while reducing average memory-token usage by 16.9% and 4.9%, respectively. The learned policy also transfers to unseen domains, executors, and memory banks, and benefits from memories generated by stronger models without further training. These results demonstrate the value of learning query-specific memory assembly using downstream task feedback.
  </details>

- **[SkillNEO: Non-Elitist Evolutionary Optimization of Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20926)**  `arXiv:2610.20926`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Enze Ma, Chunyu Miao, Yankai Chen, Jie Yang, Liancheng Fang, Kening Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent skills turn procedural knowledge into reusable artifacts, allowing LLM agents to accumulate and refine expertise through experience. Building on this, skill evolution iteratively improves skills using execution feedback. However, improvement may require several interdependent edits, so low-reward versions can still be worth developing. Requiring immediate reward gains can discard useful partial repairs before their benefits emerge. Non-elitist evolution allows new versions to receive further edits despite scoring below earlier ones. For agent skills, this calls for evidence of what to preserve and repair next. We introduce SkillNEO (Non-Elitist Evolutionary Optimization for Agent Skills), which separately selects a version to use and versions to keep improving. Comparing how related versions perform the same task reveals working steps to preserve and remaining failures to fix. It can also revisit and revise earlier versions using later evidence. Across 86 SkillsBench tasks in eight domains, including software engineering and office work, SkillNEO raises GPT-5.4 Mini's average reward from 49.1% to 66.4%, within GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6 Luna's original-skill range. Mini executes tasks, proposes revisions, and edits skills; final evaluation uses the same task instances. Gains span all eight domains. The same skills improve stronger models without further editing. On the 41 tasks with modified skills, reuse improves Luna by 10.5 percentage points and places GPT-5.5 10.6 points above its published original-skill reference. Execution records show these skills can benefit stronger models even when Mini still fails some task requirements. Search histories also show lower-scoring versions leading to skills ultimately selected for use. Together, these findings show that skill evolution can turn a weaker model's partial progress into reusable procedural knowledge for improving more capable agents.
  </details>

- **[Evolving Together, Failing Apart: The Skill-Policy Co-evolution Trap in Self-Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20845)**  `arXiv:2610.20845`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yujie Feng, Xiaobin Wang, Jinluan Yang, Xiaonan Shi, Pengjun Xie, Philip S. Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill-policy co-evolution, in which an agent's policy model and its skill bank improve each other during training, is a promising paradigm for self-improving agents. Current evaluations score the co-evolved policy on the final bank it trained with (the delivered bank). After deployment, however, skills may be rewritten, added or removed more frequently than the policy model is retrained, leaving the policy to operate on a drifted skill bank that neither the training objective nor existing evaluation protocols address. In this paper, we first establish, through theoretical and empirical analysis, that the fragility hidden by this blind spot is not incidental. The acceptance rule these methods share (cooperative acceptance) ties a policy's competence to the bank it co-evolved with, leaving it fragile once skills drift, regardless of the implementation; empirically, sensitivity to skill drift grows as co-evolution training proceeds. We therefore introduce the CO-evolution Drift Audit (CODA), a deployment-oriented evaluation protocol that simulates skill drift and, alongside success rate under worst-case drift, introduces two new metrics: a consistency rate under meaning-preserving edits and a resistance rate to corrupted skills. Across four co-evolution methods and five benchmarks (six settings), worst-case success is 5-13 points below the peak measured for each delivered pair, erasing 52-93% of the delivered bank's gain over the no-skill baseline. CODA thus reframes the target of co-evolution, from peak performance on the co-evolved bank to the ability to harness skills however they evolve, and provides a shared standard against which future methods can be audited.
  </details>

- **[ARIA: Runtime Inspection and Amendment for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20780)**  `arXiv:2610.20780`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Bolin Shen, Yushun Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS), a local mistake can propagate as other agents build on it, turning a correctable error into a failed task. Diagnosing completed runs or repeatedly re-executing them to search for corrections does not provide a learned policy for intervening while execution is still in progress. We present Agent Runtime Inspection and Amendment (ARIA), a framework for learning when and how to repair ongoing multi-agent executions. Our key intuition is to turn execution-verified repairs into reusable supervision, moving repair search into data construction and training so that a learned model can intervene at runtime without repeatedly testing alternatives. First, we collect over 24{,}000 successful and failed trajectories across four multi-agent frameworks, six benchmarks, and eight host models. From these trajectories, we construct the ARIA dataset, to our knowledge the first large-scale dataset pairing failed multi-agent trajectories with execution-verified repairs. It contains 3{,}953 verified repair pairs, each specifying what went wrong, where to intervene, and how execution should continue. Then, we develop the ARIA framework for MAS-agnostic runtime inspection and amendment, designed to integrate with any MAS that exposes execution trajectories and supports continuation replacement. Using verified repairs as training examples, we teach a compact model to recognize when intervention is needed and how to correct an ongoing run. The aim is to learn repair strategies that apply to related failures in new configurations, rather than merely reproduce individual corrections. Finally, the trained ARIA model demonstrates strong repair performance in both post-hoc and runtime settings, with generalization to unseen frameworks and host models. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[KV$^2$: A Self-Refining KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03198)**  `arXiv:2610.03198`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Johannes Wesch, Danni Liu, Jan Niehues_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The memory footprint of the key-value (KV) cache constrains the practical use of long-context models, and it dominates cost when one prefilled context must later serve many different queries. In this reusable setting, query-agnostic compression trades cost against quality: lightweight estimators are cheap but less accurate, whereas full-context reconstruction scoring is more accurate yet reprocesses the entire prompt. We introduce KV$^2$, a query-agnostic KV-cache compression method based on selective reconstruction. KV$^2$ first uses a lightweight proxy scorer to identify informative in-context tokens, then reprocesses only this subset to compute final eviction scores. On RULER, Needle-in-a-Haystack, and LongBench, KV$^2$'s margin over baselines widens as the budget tightens: on RULER 16K at a 2% KV-cache budget it improves the average score over the next-best baseline by more than 40 percentage points, and on LongBench it attains the highest average across 2%-10% budgets at lower compression-stage runtime and peak memory than full-context reconstruction. Reusable KV-cache compression thus does not require reprocessing the full context. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BenchPreS: A Benchmark for Context-Aware Personalized Preference Selectivity of Persistent-Memory LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16557)**  `arXiv:2603.16557`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sangyeon Yoon, Hyesoo Hong, Wonje Jeung, Yongil Kim, Wooseok Seo, Heuiyeen Yeen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly store user preferences in persistent memory to support personalization across interactions. However, in third-party communication settings governed by social and institutional norms, some user preferences may be inappropriate to apply. We introduce BenchPreS, which evaluates whether memory-based user preferences are appropriately applied or suppressed across communication contexts. Using two complementary metrics, Misapplication Rate (MR) and Appropriate Application Rate (AAR), we find even frontier LLMs struggle to apply preferences in a context-sensitive manner. Models with stronger preference adherence exhibit higher rates of over-application, and neither reasoning capability nor prompt-based defenses fully resolve this issue. These results suggest current LLMs treat personalized preferences as globally enforceable rules rather than as context-dependent normative signals.
  </details>

- **[KnowGPO: Knowledge-Grouped Policy Optimization for Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22331)**  `arXiv:2610.22331`  `cs.AI`  
  _Siqi Chen, Zhengxi Lu, Yuchen Yan, Xingyu Wu, Qipeng Chen, Xin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep search agents solve knowledge-intensive tasks through long-horizon, multi-turn interactions, where assigning credit to individual search decisions is fundamentally difficult. Outcome-based reinforcement learning provides only a single terminal reward shared across an entire trajectory. Recent process-supervision methods aim to assign credit at finer granularity, but they typically anchor credit to either surface-level trajectory states or model-internal signals, both of which become unreliable in deep search where observations are heterogeneous and retrieved contexts are long and noisy. We propose KnowGPO, a reinforcement learning framework that aligns trajectories by the task-relevant knowledge they have acquired rather than by their surface observations. For each query, KnowGPO decomposes the information required to derive the answer into atomic knowledge conditions, and represents the state of a trajectory at each step by the subset of conditions it has satisfied. Trajectories sharing the same knowledge state are grouped for within-group comparison, and the incremental knowledge gain between consecutive steps serves as an environment-grounded process reward. Experiments on Xbench, BrowseComp-ZH, and GAIA show KnowGPO consistently outperforms outcome-only (GRPO) and process-supervision baselines (GiGPO, IGPO) by 3.8 to 4.2 points, with our 8B model rivaling or exceeding most 32B open-source systems.
  </details>

- **[Improving Environment-Agnostic Coding Agents: The Role of Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22289)**  `arXiv:2610.22289`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sergey Rodionov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stronger language models may increasingly construct useful procedures for themselves, reducing the advantage of developer-supplied scaffolding. We investigate why this advantage persists and whether an environment-agnostic agent can be improved without environment-specific engineering. We evaluate a Codex-based agent in two nearly identical prompt variants on ARC-AGI-3, AGI Maze, and Terminal-Bench. Each variant is deployed with unchanged code, prompts, and general-purpose tools across environments, receiving environment-specific information as input. Changing only an instruction to encourage reporting difficulties and returning control for automatic continuation improves mean scores on both ARC-AGI-3 and AGI Maze, while Terminal-Bench shows small, mixed changes. Yet the general agent remains below an ARC-AGI-3-specific reference, including with GPT-5.6-sol. The reference prescribes organization and control rather than supplying new game rules or solutions. To investigate this gap, we transfer reset decisions to a scripted controller that follows a convention already given to the agent. This small change recovers part of the specialized agent's advantage. A complementary AGI Maze experiment shows that additional tutorial experience can reduce performance. Together, these findings highlight the distinction between having information and tools available and using them effectively. Supplied procedures still matter for the models tested here. The performance gains also come with higher token-based cost. The experiments do not separate better use of information and procedures from additional or differently allocated inference. We provide the EnvTraverse framework and an evaluated baseline agent for testing whether further environment-independent changes improve performance in some environments without degrading it in others.
  </details>

- **[Terminal-RSI: Recursive Self-Improving Terminal Agents via Recursive Self-Rewrite and Policy-Refine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22192)**  `arXiv:2610.22192`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junyao Yang, Zongxia Li, Yucheng Shi, Zhongzhi Li, Ruhan Wang, Ruiwen Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Converting verifier outcomes into useful policy updates and partial progress into complete task resolution is crucial for terminal agents. We present Terminal-RSI, which alternates Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR) of verified trajectories with critic-inheriting Recursive Policy-Refine (RPR). We formulate Turn-Boundary Skip-Observation GAE (TB-GAE) to retain token-specific baselines while applying decay across interaction turns. Family Dense Reward (FDR) calibrates semantic progress across task families; Success-Prioritized Reward (SPR) separates strict success from bounded partial credit. A Warm-up Critic Model, recursive critic handoff, and stage-specific learning rates coordinate the two refinement phases. The five-stage sequence from Qwen3.8-27B base model through RSR-1, RPR-1, RSR-2, and RPR-2 yields Terminal-RSI-27B: 19.84% on Terminal-Bench 4.0, 86.52% on Terminal-Bench 2.1, 16.95% on Terminal-Bench 3.0, and 79.00% on Terminal-Bench Hard. On LHTB, process reward rises from 0.21 to 0.37 and strict completion from 0/46 to 5/46; SWR100 success doubles from 3.00% to 6.00%. By recursively coupling verified trajectory rewriting with critic-inheriting policy refinement, each retained stage consolidates discovered behaviors and progressively advances the agent from partial local repairs to strict terminal verification.
  </details>

- **[Realtime-Bench: Can Frontier Models Solve Real-time Interactive Problems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22155)**  `arXiv:2610.22155`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingyu Tang, Xu Yang, Zhehao Zhang, Zheng Wang, Qi Cheng, Haozhe Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-time interaction requires agents to perceive evolving environments and act under temporal constraints, yet existing evaluations of real-time streaming models focus on conversational quality and streaming perception, leaving effective action under strict temporal constraints untested. We introduce Realtime-Bench, a suite of carefully curated dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success. Under Wall Clock, where environments advance according to actual inference latency, the best success rate reaches only 44% and the best Pass^3 is only 36%, demonstrating that real-time interaction remains challenging. To separate serving latency from models' real-time streaming capability, we compare Wall Clock with Paused Clock, which removes inference delays as an idealized reference, and introduce Token Clock, which advances simulated time according to generated token counts, testing temporal awareness independently of physical serving speed. We find that temporal costs limit success in evolving environments and that higher reasoning effort has mixed effects under time pressure, reflecting a trade-off between decision quality and timely execution. These findings suggest directions for improving real-time multimodal agents, including adaptive reasoning with temporal awareness and continuous perception and action.
  </details>

- **[Are You Sure Your LLM Is Sure? Repairing and Auditing Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22139)**  `arXiv:2610.22139`  `cs.AI`  
  _Arijit Dey, Bhartendu Kumar, Ramya Hebbalaguppe, Khuushi Maheshwari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Confidence scores determine whether an LLM answer is accepted, reviewed, or regenerated, yet scalar recalibration can only correct errors visible through the score itself. We introduce RADAR (Repair, Adopt, and Diagnose After Recalibration), a reliability framework that separates confidence repair from deciding whether to deploy the repair and auditing the errors that remain. Under squared loss, we decompose prediction risk into outcome noise, information hidden by the score, and mapping error; restricting repair to order-preserving maps additionally introduces a pooling gap. For repair, we propose Probe-Anchored Isotonic Calibration (PAIC), which learns a monotone map from correctness-probe predictions on unlabeled target prompts. PAIC's excess risk depends on the probe's score-conditional bias and fitting error rather than its overall accuracy. For adoption, we derive a paired procedure with finite-sample control of false improvements. For auditing, we develop a prompt-clustered kernel test that detects feature-aligned residual errors while accounting for dependent answers from the same prompt. Across multiple LLM checkpoints, benchmarks, and distribution shifts, richer predictors can improve confidence estimation, but projecting their information back to a scalar score can lose these gains, and lower prediction risk need not yield better review decisions. Across 47,400 replayed raw-score allocations, paired betting adopts candidates with lower held-out test risk in 42.95% of allocations, versus 15.09% for one-sided empirical Bernstein. RADAR thus provides a principled workflow to repair what a scalar score can express, adopt a repair only when supported by evidence, and diagnose systematic errors that recalibration leaves behind. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[DropKV: Decoupling Residual-Output Perturbation for Near-Optimal KV-Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22130)**  `arXiv:2610.22130`  `cs.AI`  
  _Aozhong Zhang, Selcuk Gurses, Yanxia Deng, Naigang Wang, Chi-Chun Liu, Davis Wertheimer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Key-value (KV) cache management has become a critical bottleneck for scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) to long-context scenarios due to linear memory growth. While recent eviction methods have moved beyond simple attention-score heuristics to incorporate value representations, they typically face a fundamental trade-off: the underlying optimization for minimizing output perturbation is an NP-hard combinatorial problem, leading to mathematically suboptimal heuristics or implementations that are difficult to kernelize. In this work, we propose DropKV, a simple principled eviction framework based on Decoupled Residual-Output Perturbation. By decoupling the joint eviction decision into independent per-token scoring, DropKV circumvents combinatorial intractability and admits a provable constant-factor approximation guarantee under a cone condition that we empirically verify on three long-context LLMs. To ensure practical viability, we provide a high-performance implementation using fused Triton kernels that avoid materializing the full attention matrix, delivering a 19.8x scoring-kernel speedup that translates to up to a 9.9% matched-batch end-to-end prefill speedup. Extensive evaluations on the RULER and LongBench benchmarks demonstrate that DropKV consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines at equivalent cache budgets, achieving the lowest inference latency among eviction methods while also remaining faster than the dynamic cache baseline.
  </details>

- **[PROBE: Learning to Audit Policy Compliance in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22124)**  `arXiv:2610.22124`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kshitij Mishra, Abhijith Sharma, Nils Lukas, Salem Lahlou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern agentic systems extend language models with external tools, enabling them to complete complex tasks through multi-turn interactions. As these agents are deployed in consequential settings, they are often constrained by policies governing pricing, privacy, confirmation, safety, and other requirements. However, existing evaluation methods leave a critical gap: capability benchmarks measure what agents can accomplish, while content-safety red-teaming measures harmful generations, but neither tests whether agents comply with predefined policies during otherwise valid tool use. Such policy violations are difficult to audit because their effects may emerge only after several turns, span multiple tool calls, or surface in downstream systems. We propose PROBE (Policy Rule Observation via Behavioral Exploration), an algorithm for training LLM-based auditors that interact with deployed agents as realistic users and probe them for policy violations through multi-turn, tool-mediated dialogue. We formalize agent auditing as a two-player Markov game and propose a composite reward that balances violation discovery, behavioral plausibility, conversation progress, and policy diversity. To study training objectives and reward design, we compare GRPO, DPO, and GFlowNet-based optimization in a controlled setting. Across extensive experiments, auditors trained with PROBE uncover substantially broader and higher-yield policy violations than same-architecture zero-shot adversaries, even when the auditor is significantly smaller than the target agent. Our results show that multi-turn behavioral auditing is a distinct and necessary axis of agent evaluation, complementing capability and safety benchmarks. PROBE offers a scalable path toward automated policy auditing for real-world agentic systems and suggests a co-evolutionary loop in which auditors expose failures and agents improve from auditor feedback.
  </details>

- **[Learning from Unverified Peers via Shared Residual Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22080)**  `arXiv:2610.22080`  `cs.AI`  
  _Feiyang Chen, Runze Ma, Zichen Wang, Jiong Lou, Zihan Wang, Shuangjia Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A language model can generate several solutions to the same problem, some leading to correct answers and others to incorrect ones. Many post-training methods turn these trajectories into learning signals using supervision from reference answers, verifiable feedback, or external teachers. Without such supervision, learning from the model's own generations becomes less straightforward, as their correctness cannot be reliably assessed. An incorrect solution may nevertheless contain useful intermediate reasoning, so its value as supervision need not be determined solely by its final answer. Can a model improve by learning from its unverified peer solutions without first determining which are correct?We propose Shared Residual Distillation (SRD), an on-policy self-distillation method that learns from the model's own unverified peer trajectories. SRD compares how peers change the prediction distribution relative to a control, retaining adjustments that agree in direction and are conservative in magnitude. This yields supervision without treating any complete solution as correct, and the trained model requires no peers at inference time. Starting from the corresponding base models, SRD raises mean AIME24--26 avg@12 from 11.02 to 16.20 on Qwen3-1.7B, from 19.07 to 45.46 on Qwen3-4B, and from 21.39 to 54.44 on Qwen3-8B. Controlled ablations support the value of the shared-residual construction, and we observe no degradation relative to the base models on two general-capability benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[When Reasoning Is Not Enough: Proactive Inquiry for Cross-Modal Safety in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22068)**  `arXiv:2610.22068`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wei Cai, Jian Zhao, Yuchen Yuan, Xuelong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can act unsafely when individually benign images and texts are interpreted jointly. Beyond implicit reasoning risk, where reasoning drifts toward harm, we identify intent-level risk, where cross-modal ambiguity silently commits the model to a hazardous premise that path-level optimization alone cannot correct. We propose SAFE-PRO, which uses a Risk-Conditioned Uncertainty (RCU) signal, combining predictive entropy with a learned cross-modal risk score, to decide when to ask safety-grounded clarifying questions and where to apply path optimization, under a reward that prioritizes safety and penalizes over-refusal. We also build SSUI-Dialog, a multimodal multi-turn dataset with dual-intent supervision, and theoretically analyze gradient variance and unsafe-completion risk under RCU. Across seven benchmarks and two base MLLMs, SAFE-PRO consistently improves safety over SRPO, roughly halves its over-refusal, and surpasses GPT-4o and Claude-3.5-Sonnet in average single-turn safety.
  </details>

- **[Prism-1: Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22066)**  `arXiv:2610.22066`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yao Long Teng, Jianguo Huang, Yanchen Deng, Jiayi Cai, Bo An_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given a state and a set of options, such as intents, tools or interface elements, System One decision models return a distribution over the options in one forward pass, without generated reasoning. Web systems increasingly build them by reading decisions from language models. Two axioms of rational choice are natural requirements for such decisions, because in web systems the listing and the option set are determined by other components and can change or be manipulated: the order in which the options are listed should not matter, and adding an option should not change the relative odds of the others (Luce's choice axiom). We test the standard reads, namely single-choice question (SCQ) prompting, with shuffled-order training, order averaging or sorted options, and invariant set architectures, and find that each violates at least one axiom: one shuffle changes 12-20% of SCQ answers, sorting only freezes an arbitrary convention, and a well-chosen added option steers 4-34% of decisions. By Luce's theorem, a read satisfies both axioms only if it is a softmax over scores that each depend on one option alone, so in a transformer they hold by construction only if no token that scores an option sees another. We implement this as Prism-1 (Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models): every option is read in its own block at shared positions, with the model's usual left-to-right attention, in one forward pass. On five benchmark sets with Qwen3-8B and Gemma 4 12B, SCQ prompting trained on the same data still changes 7% of its answers under one shuffle, and an added irrelevant option steers 12% of its decisions. Prism-1 stays within one point of that baseline's accuracy and is 2-4 points more accurate once only answers that survive a reordering are counted, while no shuffle changes any of its answers and no added irrelevant option moves its choice among the others.
  </details>

- **[CoWorld Reasoner: Agentic Multimodal Reasoning with the Evolving Video World Model-Driven Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21910)**  `arXiv:2610.21910`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dongjie Cheng, Ruifeng Yuan, Ran Wei, Ning Song, Yongqi Li, Wenjie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As Vision-Language Models (VLMs) advance, they are increasingly expected to perform spatiotemporal reasoning over motion, actions, and view changes beyond what the input images show. Video World Models (VWMs) provide a natural unified engine for visual imagination by generating task-relevant visual content as additional evidence. However, existing approaches typically invoke a VWM through fixed pipelines centered on a predefined form of visual imagination, such as novel-view synthesis. To support flexible visual imagination for diverse reasoning tasks, we introduce CoWorld Reasoner, an agentic framework that uses a VWM as a unified visual reasoning engine. Its Video World Model-Driven Harness exposes multiple visual operations through tools and uses skills to guide the VLM in deciding whether to imagine, selecting an operation, constructing a generation request, and interpreting the resulting visual evidence. We further introduce VLM-VWM Harness Co-Evolution, which jointly revises tools, skills, and execution code based on execution feedback while keeping both model weights fixed. Across seven benchmarks spanning spatial intelligence, temporal and causal reasoning, and perceptual reasoning, CoWorld Reasoner outperforms direct reasoning with the same VLM. The strongest variant improves on all seven benchmarks, including relative accuracy gains of 11.8% on MMSI and 13.0% on ERQA, and outperforms all five evolving baselines using the same backbone across five benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[RSIFlow: Adaptive Self-Improvement Workflows with an Evolving Meta-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21791)**  `arXiv:2610.21791`  `cs.AI`  
  _Huan Zhang, Mingju Chen, Dongxu Zhou, Can Lv, Sen Cui, Faguo Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in complex tasks, including code generation, tool use, and multi-hop question answering. Existing self-improvement methods enhance agent performance through model optimization, harness evolution, and executable artifact refinement, while recursive self-improvement (RSI) further extends optimization to the improvement mechanisms themselves. However, adapting self-improvement workflows to evolving performance bottlenecks while improving workflow orchestration through execution feedback remains a key challenge. We propose \textbf{RSIFlow}, a \textbf{R}ecursive \textbf{S}elf-\textbf{I}mprovement framework that integrates adaptive work\textbf{flow} orchestration with meta-agent capability evolution. RSIFlow diagnoses performance bottlenecks from task-agent trajectories and environmental feedback to dynamically select component-specific improvement workflows. The meta-agent then distills workflow outcomes into component-specific skills and cross-component principles, evolving its own harness to improve subsequent workflow orchestration. Experiments across six benchmarks and two backbone models show that RSIFlow achieves the SOTA average performance on all benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of adaptive workflow orchestration and meta-harness evolution. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Research Goals to Scientific Closure: Evaluating Agents Through Evidence-Supported Physics Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21767)**  `arXiv:2610.21767`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijia Mao, Song Dai, Tao Chen, Xiangyu Lu, Linqian Zeng, Chenxi Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can AI agents organize scientific investigations that lead to well-supported conclusions? To evaluate this capability, we introduce PhysClosureBench, a benchmark of 50 expert-verified, goal-specified research tasks spanning strong-field, attosecond, and neighboring physics areas. Each task specifies the research objective and essential scientific constraints, while leaving the agent to organize the investigation and determine how to generate and validate the evidence needed to answer the question. A 20-criterion Atomic Score measures local scientific accomplishment, while Research Closure (RC0--RC4) assesses how fully the investigation resolves the specified research question based on whether its indispensable evidence requirements and applicable scope conditions are satisfied. We evaluate seven frontier LLMs, where Claude-Opus-5 achieves the highest mean Atomic Score (6.29/10) but reaches RC4 in only 32.7% of attempts. Across the benchmark, 36.2% of attempts scoring at least 8/10 still fall short of RC4, while all seven models reach RC4 on substantially fewer tasks in all three runs than in at least one. Case-level analysis further illustrates how misinterpretation of generated results and unresolved physical applicability limits can lead to incomplete closure. Together, these results reveal a persistent gap between AI agents' local scientific accomplishments and their ability to achieve complete research closure.
  </details>

- **[Data World: Scaling Data Environments from Scratch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21656)**  `arXiv:2610.21656`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhanli Li, Zeyuan Xu, Chaoxu Pang, Zeyuan Liu, Anyang Ji, Te Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data agents use code to process heterogeneous files and derive analytical insights. Scaling their training faces costly environment curation, privacy and intellectual-property restrictions, and difficulty verifying open-ended findings without known generative truth. Our core insight is to specify the world behind the files before generating the files themselves. We introduce Data World, a multi-agent engine that synthesizes environments, tasks, and rubrics from explicit mechanisms. Independent audits check target recoverability from observed files; rubrics support evaluation and RL rewards. Human preference-based curation for question realism selects 100 tasks across 50 held-out worlds and 16 domains for DataWorld-Bench. The engine also yields 2,135 training tasks from 1,037 disjoint worlds. Evaluation of ten frontier models shows that the benchmark remains challenging, particularly for insight discovery, with a consistent computation-insight gap. RL-zero training on the synthesized tasks produces DataWorld-35B-A3B from Qwen3.6-35B-A3B without trajectory distillation. Under matched evaluation, it improves over its base by 7.06 points on DataWorld-Bench, 14.28 on DAComp, 20.90 on DABstep-Hard, and 12.13 on JobBench, a broader occupational benchmark.
  </details>

- **[AgentPerturb: Evaluating LLM Agent Robustness under Controlled Interaction Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21624)**  `arXiv:2610.21624`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuanjian Xu, Jinyi Han, Jianing Hao, Yuxuan Sun, Fanyang Lu, Richeng Xuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world tasks are often complex and noisy, posing substantial challenges for LLM agents. Yet existing evaluations lack a systematic framework for jointly characterizing potential perturbation targets and types. We use this two-dimensional view to conduct a unified study that quantifies the robustness of current frontier models, traces the mechanisms behind perturbation-induced failures, and draws implications for robustness-oriented training. Through controlled evaluations on APEX-Agents and Toolathlon, we find that perturbations consistently degrade performance, while the dominant vulnerabilities vary across task environments. Trajectory-level analysis reveals that agents rarely recognize altered inputs and often propagate corrupted information without verification. A focused cost analysis further shows that successful perturbed trajectories can require substantially more computation. These findings motivate training objectives that cover diverse forms of noise and reward verification, uncertainty awareness, and recovery. We provide a rigorous benchmark-construction pipeline. Code and data will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591)**  `arXiv:2610.21591`  `cs.AI`  
  _M. Leibl, F. Azevedo, S. Uhlich, A. Bonetti, A. Venkitaraman, C. Hsieh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. We address both gaps with a large opensource dataset and a suite of standardized benchmarks. The dataset contains more than 100 operational amplifier (opamp) topologies (1-, 2-, and 3-stage) in the SkyWater130 PDK, each shipped with structural and functional annotations, topology-specific sizing constraints (symmetry and sizing rules), an Ngspice testbench, and a multi-objective NSGA-II sizing run producing around 103 Pareto-optimal and 106 intermediate sizing-performance pairs per topology. On top of this data we define six benchmark tasks with precise protocols: inverse sizing, topology selection, sub-circuit classification, structural circuit generation, Pareto-membership prediction, and multi-objective optimization. Thus, the data presented here constitutes the most comprehensive dataset for ML development in over four decades of analog IC design automation. The dataset, ground-truth annotations, and reference implementations of the metric-defining routines are released under an open license, enabling reproducible and directly comparable evaluation across heterogeneous analog design automation methods.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning-driven Diffusion Models: Language, Vision, and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21539)**  `arXiv:2610.21539`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyuan Ma, Jiabao Wei, Tianyu Wang, Hanqing Wang, Jiadong Pan, Haowen Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models (DMs) have steadily demonstrated outstanding performance in high-fidelity, controllable generation across diverse scenarios, following the "pretraining-finetuning" paradigm established in LLMs. Despite these advances, conventional DMs still face critical limitations in sophisticated reasoning, autonomous exploration of generation pathways, and adaptability to complex, human-aligned objectives. Inspired by RLHF's success in LLMs, RL has recently emerged as a pivotal post-training mechanism to iteratively refine DMs through reward-driven trajectory optimization. This paper presents the first comprehensive review of RL-driven DMs and an empirical analysis of how RL reshapes the diffusion process towards experience-centric, goal-directed generation. We propose a novel technical panorama elucidating RL's multifaceted roles in diffusion-based language, multimodal, robotic, and scientific models, with a granular focus on diffusion trajectory optimization and reward engineering. Through extensive experiments on multiple task-specific benchmarks, we systematically evaluate representative methods to intuitively highlight the advantages of different RL post-training strategies. Furthermore, we reveal and dissect three crucial bottlenecks that currently hinder the real-world deployment of RL-driven DMs: reward design fragility, exploration-exploitation trade-off in high-dimensional generation spaces, and training instability under sparse rewards. Based on these perspectives, we provide a principled roadmap for the integration of RL into DMs and outline promising future research directions for robust, interpretable RL-diffusion systems. We also construct a public repository to continuously track the latest developments in this rapidly evolving field:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Functional Subnetwork Localization for Efficient Continual Learning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21537)**  `arXiv:2610.21537`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuoran Jiang, Wentao Zhu, Yang Xiang, Dongfeng Li, Yunan Zhang, Mengchen Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biological intelligence continually adapts to new information while retaining prior skills through localized plasticity. In contrast, continual learning in large language models (LLMs) remains vulnerable to catastrophic forgetting. A promising direction is to identify functionally specialized subnetworks within LLMs that enable targeted and resource-efficient adaptation. Using gradient-attribution profiling, we localize cognition-associated functional subnetworks exhibiting extremely low mutual overlap. Building on this finding, we propose Function-isolated Memory-Efficient Optimization (CiMEO), a continual learning framework that routes each training example to its corresponding functional subnetwork, restricting parameter updates and optimizer states to selected regions. Across 1.4 million sequential instructions, CiMEO achieves the highest average performance among the compared methods on five generative benchmarks spanning programming, examination-style tasks, and mathematical reasoning, while mitigating catastrophic forgetting. Subspace-overlap and principal-angle analyses further indicate that localized updates help preserve pre-trained representations, highlighting functional subnetwork localization as a practical approach to efficient and stable continual learning in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[QREF: Improving DeepSearch Agents by Aligning Search with Reasoning via Query Refinement and Evidence Filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21512)**  `arXiv:2610.21512`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yujia Chen, Yuhui Wang, Junzhe Wang, Shihan Dou, Di Liang, Yujiong Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  DeepSearch agents achieve strong performance on complex information-seeking tasks through iterative search and reasoning. However, they often issue intent-misaligned, under-constrained, or redundant search queries, resulting in poor evidence coverage and accumulating retrieval noise. To mitigate these limitations, we propose QREF, a plug-and-play Query Refiner and Evidence Filter. Specifically, the Query Refiner rewrites search queries according to the current reasoning intent while ensuring complementary coverage across each search batch. The Evidence Filter retains evidence relevant to both the current search batch and the reasoning objective, reducing distracting retrieval results. We initialize the Query Refiner with supervised fine-tuning and optimize it through reinforcement learning based on interactions with a real-world web search environment, reducing ineffective queries. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that our approach improves the performance of DeepSearch agents across four challenging benchmarks, achieving average relative Pass@1 improvements of 33.55% on WebSailor and 11.04% on TongyiDeepResearch without modifying architectures or retraining their reasoning policies. Compared with the original DeepSearch backbone without QREF, the RL-trained refiner reduces the fraction of query batches that lack discriminative retrieval anchors by 95%. Compared with the SFT-trained refiner, it also lowers the filter-based ineffective query ratio.
  </details>

- **[MetaExp: Learning to Self-Improve through Experience via Verifiable Cross-Task Environment Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21492)**  `arXiv:2610.21492`  `cs.AI`  
  _Renhao Li, Xinjie Shen, Yang Su, Jianhong Tu, Derek F. Wong, Dayiheng Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents can accumulate interaction feedback, but reliably turning that feedback into reusable experience remains challenging. Existing methods either optimize external experience scaffolds or train experience-related behavior on specific task distributions, leaving open whether experience learning itself can become a transferable model capability. Training such a capability calls for supervision beyond isolated tasks, which mainly support in-episode problem solving rather than revealing what knowledge remains useful across episodes. In this work, we introduce MetaExp, a scalable framework that trains this capability through verifiable task families with controlled cross-task structure. Each family preserves a reusable factor while varying task-specific realizations, enabling transfer without direct solution reuse. We propose two complementary designs: Shared-Tool families keep black-box tools fixed across tasks to elicit operational experience, while Shared-Workflow families preserve latent procedures across changing realizations to elicit procedural experience. We scale environments under both designs, collect and filter sequential interaction trajectories, and fine-tune Qwen3.5-122B-A10B on 4,956 trajectories from 1,000 task families. With parameters frozen during downstream evolution, MetaExp improves by +8.5 points on SpreadsheetBench, peaks at +2.3 on APEX-Agents, and improves by +14.8 on AutomationBench. The gains persist under domain-excluded training, supporting experience learning as a transferable capability for continued non-parametric improvement after deployment.
  </details>

- **[FlashAttention with Statistics: Low-Overhead Attention Features for Compact Hallucination Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21483)**  `arXiv:2610.21483`  `cs.AI`  
  _Andrei Volodichev, Maksim Savichev, Nikita Sviridov, Artem Yuliy, Dmitry Simakov, Alexey Zaytsev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can produce fluent answers that the supplied context does not support. A compact frozen proxy model can detect such answers by reading the context and another model's response, even from a proprietary generator. Attention-based detectors use summary statistics of the proxy's attention weights, but standard pipelines materialize the attention matrix, whose memory grows quadratically with sequence length. We compute these statistics without materializing attention, by accumulation in the FlashAttention pass, sparse lookups after it, or a second tiled pass over the scores, and classify them by this path into nearly free and costlier ones. On an H100, the statistics of five published detectors add 16% to the latency of a Qwen3-8B forward at 2K tokens, falling to 1.4% at 35K, and store 22.5 KiB per response token. On three benchmarks, probes trained on frozen Qwen3 proxies of 0.6B to 8B parameters prove strong detectors: a 4B proxy outperforms the strongest of nine zero-shot 4-9B LLM judges in F1 on two of the three, the proxies match or outperform specialized grounding checkers of up to 7B parameters, probing each proxy beats prompting it as a judge, and adding attention features significantly improves F1 in half of the 12 benchmark-proxy pairs, never significantly lowering it.
  </details>

- **[Tool-Augmented Large Language Models for Reliable Medical Calculation from Patient Notes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21477)**  `arXiv:2610.21477`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Zhong, Ling Luo, Xuanlyu Zhu, Yanhua Wang, Yuanyuan Sun, Jian Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical calculators translate raw patient observations into clinically actionable values and scores. However, applying these calculators directly to free-form patient notes requires precise variable extraction combined with the exact execution of complex formulas, scoring rules, and output conventions. Relying solely on direct large language model (LLM) prompting is unreliable in this context, as rule recall, parameter extraction, arithmetic reasoning, and formatting become entangled within a single generative step. To address these limitations, we propose a tool-augmented LLM framework for reliable medical calculation from patient notes. The framework retrieves and structures calculator specifications, generates reusable executable tools, and uses a constrained extractor to map each patient note to calculator-ready inputs. The tools then perform deterministic calculation, separating clinical language understanding from rule execution and arithmetic. Evaluated on the MedCalc-Bench dataset, our method achieves 86.73% final-answer accuracy, outperforming the one-shot prompting and supervised fine tuning-only baselines by 24.0 and 17.27 percentage points. Ablation and error analyses show that these performance gains are primarily driven by rule-grounded tool synthesis and schema-constrained extraction, whereas remaining failures are concentrated in clinical-semantic extraction and benchmark-specific output contracts. By grounding calculator logic in executable tools, the proposed approach alleviates arithmetic hallucinations and improves the computational reliability of the final clinical outputs.
  </details>

- **[JevAgentHarness: Benchmarking, Deploying and Training Decision Models That Share Control with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21412)**  `arXiv:2610.21412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kunlun Zhu, Tao Feng, Xu Han, Heng Wang, Ling Yang, Xiangru Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents now fix software, operate computers and serve customers, but their harnesses, the programs that run them, call a large LLM at every step, which makes long tasks slow and costly. Most of these calls end in a multiple-choice decision, such as which tool to call next. Decision models such as Jev answer these questions directly, at a fraction of an LLM call's time and price. We ask how a harness can delegate these decisions to a decision model with little loss of success. To evaluate decision models in agentic tasks, we build AgentDecisionBench, which tests them from isolated questions up to control of a running agent, on public benchmarks and our DecisionGym environment. The decision model most accurate on isolated questions solved only 69.5% of agent tasks, and without a fitted confidence threshold, delegating tool choices lowered success by 19.0 points. Building on these findings, we then design JevAgentHarness, which fits this threshold for each choice on the agent's own trajectories and lets the LLM decide below it. Because existing decision models never see the states an agent reaches after its own mistakes, we train AgentDecision-4B, an open 4B model, on such states with Jev's answers as labels. On tasks unlike its training data, the agent solved 91.5% of them with AgentDecision-4B and 82.0% with Jev. On tasks like their training data, 4B models trained this way made agents 81.1-89.0% cheaper and up to 2.5 times faster, without losing 3 or more points of task success. The models, data, and harness will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Jev-LiteMAS: Separating Decisions from Generation in Multi-Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21411)**  `arXiv:2610.21411`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kaixiang Wang, Zhaijiacheng Zhou, Jiong Lou, Jie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM systems rely on generative models for expert execution, workflow planning, and artifact review. Yet many coordination operations require only bounded decisions, such as selecting the next expert or judging artifact quality. Profiling three representative MAS frameworks, we find that routing and review account for 31.7-43.5% of generative-LLM tokens. However, replacing these operations with low-cost decision models introduces two interface gaps: generative planners produce execution plans and handoff instructions (planning gap), while reviewers provide actionable feedback for repairing flawed artifacts (review gap), which bounded decisions alone cannot provide. We introduce Jev-LiteMAS, a multi-agent framework that bridges both gaps by combining a lightweight decision model (Jev) with generative execution and feedback. To address the planning gap, Jev selects experts dynamically from the task and prior expert outputs, using accumulated context to ground subsequent execution without a pre-generated trajectory. To address the review gap, Jev screens intermediate artifacts against task criteria: passing artifacts proceed directly, whereas flagged ones undergo LLM re-review for acceptance or corrective feedback. Across four benchmarks and two backbones, Jev-LiteMAS reduces generative-LLM tokens by 12.7-54.6% over LAS, maintaining accuracy within 1.34 percentage points and lowering latency in seven of eight settings. Grounding routing in substantive expert outputs prevents up to 18.0 points in accuracy loss.
  </details>

- **[MemCut: Every Secret Leaves a Trail in the Long-Term Memory of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21405)**  `arXiv:2610.21405`  `cs.AI`  
  _Cheng Zhang, Xuanning Cao, Zhicheng Zheng, Hui Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory has become a crucial component of large language model (LLM) agents, with most research focusing on retrieving relevant information accurately and efficiently. However, a dependable memory system must also forget sensitive user data upon request. To evaluate this capability, we introduce MemCut, a systematic benchmark for user-requested forgetting. Its 456 hand-designed secrets in long-horizon conversations have fully annotated evidence trails to test recovery after deletion, including from messages that never state the secrets directly. We propose two recovery metrics. Native recovery measures reader success through the system's retrieval, whereas residual recovery measures the same reader's success when directly given surviving objects traced to annotated disclosures or evidence. We establish three reference deletion baselines to test how far existing retrievers and write models support forgetting across five memory designs and four LLMs. Disclosure removal reduces native recovery far more than residual recovery. LLM-guided deletion of ten additional turns from twenty retrieval candidates leaves 39-76% residual recovery. Subset sweeps (144 facts, three systems, two models) show that widening the pool at a fixed budget reduces residual recovery more than deleting all original candidates. These sweeps identify candidate coverage as a bottleneck in the reference baselines.
  </details>

- **[Watts If? A Benchmark for Counterfactual Reasoning About the AI Data Center Buildout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21397)**  `arXiv:2610.21397`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pankaj Chouhan, Rajarshi Das, Aditya Dubey, Manminder Tomar, Sreekar Reddy, Samta Sharma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An AI data center is committed years before it draws a watt: a few discrete choices (grid or on-site gas, evaporative or dry cooling, which county to file in) fix its capital, water draw, and carbon path, and LLMs are now asked to help screen them with no test of whether they track what each commitment rules out. We introduce DCBench-440, with two arms reported separately: 200 generative questions over STRIPS worlds for five US deal archetypes, with search-verified gold answers and no tested shortcut above 12% on any question type, and 240 multiple-choice questions grounded in quoted site records, emission equations, and stated scenarios. Across open-weight deployments, accuracy spans 12.5-71.5% (generative) and 58.3-92.5% (multiple choice). One skill fails everywhere: asked to list every action available at a decision gate, no model exceeds 4.0% exact match. The strongest list 72-85% of the actions that would change the project's state but omit most permitted actions that change nothing; with those excused, the best exact match is still 24%. Code, data, and other materials are available upon request.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Reason with Selective Internal Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21392)**  `arXiv:2610.21392`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xun Lu, Shumeng Yang, Xinrui Wang, Songjun Tu, Linjing Li, Daniel Dajun Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from internal feedback can improve language models without ground-truth answers or external verifiers. However, confidence and correctness can diverge during training, and continued optimization of average self-certainty can increase confidence while degrading reasoning accuracy. We introduce SIF, a reinforcement learning method that combines comparative reward construction with selective policy updates. SIF compares ranked token-level self-certainty profiles across multiple windows and peers, assigning positive rewards only when both levels of voting provide sufficient support. Sample and group level filtering determines which responses contribute to policy optimization. Experiments on three backbones across 12 mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks show that SIF's final checkpoints achieve higher average accuracy than the best-validation checkpoints of Intuitor and Vigor, with a gain of 4.71 percentage points over Intuitor on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B. Further analyses show better retention of accuracy during continued optimization, while test-time training experiments demonstrate sustained improvements with as few as four rollouts per prompt.
  </details>

- **[ARGUS: An Agenda-Driven Framework for Reliable User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21346)**  `arXiv:2610.21346`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hang Zheng, Nan Jiang, Zichen Zhu, Zixu Yang, Zhiyang Tang, Shuai Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-fidelity user simulators are essential for evaluating and training agents in interactive environments. However, current LLM-based simulators struggle with reliability issues, such as goal omission and policy non-compliance. We propose ARGUS (Agenda-dRiven Goal-oriented User Simulator), a neuro-symbolic framework that maintains the user's goal state using a dynamic agenda stack manipulated via symbolic operations. By rigorously decoupling state tracking from natural language generation, ARGUS facilitates strict goal progression and constraint-aligned interactions. For cost-efficient deployment, we distill ARGUS into compact models using a unified pipeline: evaluator-guided SFT followed by online RL with dense step-level rewards. To rigorously evaluate behavioral fidelity, we introduce a rubric-based metric Composite Behavioral Score (CBS). Evaluations on $\tau^3$-Bench and MultiWOZ demonstrate the significant superiority of the ARGUS architecture and the efficacy of our training pipeline. Code and data will be released soon.
  </details>

- **[Quantum Versus Classical Machine Learning for Neuropsychiatric Disorders: A Feature-Matched Multi-Cohort Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21344)**  `arXiv:2610.21344`  `cs.AI`  
  _Utsav Poudel, Anuj Nepal, Vyas Viswanath, Subramaniyaswamy Vairavasundaram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Resting-state functional connectivity is widely used in machine learning-based classification of neuropsychiatric disorders. Quantum machine learning models have been proposed for functional connectivity data, but few studies have evaluated them under leave-site-out validation or against matched classical models. We compared six quantum model families with classical ones using identical correlation-derived features across four public cohorts (4,308 subjects). Leave-site-out validation was used for the Autism Brain Imaging Data Exchange (ABIDE I), ADHD-200 (attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder), and REST-meta-MDD (major depressive disorder) cohorts, while the UCLA Consortium for Neuropsychiatric Phenomics (UCLA-CNP) was evaluated using stratified 10-fold cross-validation. The quantum kernel was statistically indistinguishable from a matched classical kernel across all four cohorts, with the average accuracy difference within 1.4 percentage points at 95% confidence. On ABIDE I, a hybrid quantum convolutional network achieved similar accuracy to its classical counterpart (0.645 vs. 0.641), while a trainable quantum kernel exceeded its classical counterpart by 0.064 in area under the curve (AUC). Quantum convolutional networks also achieved higher AUC than their matched classical networks on this cohort and showed smaller train-test gaps on all four cohorts, without an accuracy gain. Overall, most quantum models matched their classical counterparts, but none consistently outperformed them. This study, among the first quantum evaluations of ABIDE I under leave-site-out validation, provides a feature-matched benchmark of quantum and classical models comprising 284 matched comparisons. We further analyse fidelity-based graph construction and account for the observed quantum-classical parity through effective-dimension and eigenspectrum analyses.
  </details>

- **[Constrained Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning in Imagination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21319)**  `arXiv:2610.21319`  `cs.AI`  
  _Raz Shmueli, Shahaf S. Shperberg_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting cost in a multi-agent world model moves most constrained policy updates into imagination: the stronger model-free baseline needs $10$ to $20$ times CA-MAMBA's interactions on the median SMAC configuration, and CA-MAMBA earns $2.8$ to $12$ times the baselines' MAMuJoCo reward at equal budget. In controlled tests on one task per benchmark, pricing the model's cost advantages at parity more than halves a cost-blind learner's cost and meets a tight continuous-control limit the learned multiplier misses, suggesting the model's cost estimates are not the bottleneck.
  </details>

- **[Condition-Contrastive Extrapolation for On-Policy Distillation of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21195)**  `arXiv:2610.21195`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wenxuan Luo, Chenxi Li, Haoyue Liu, Yongxin Guo, Xiaoying Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) improves a student language model by training on its own generated trajectories with supervision from a stronger teacher. However, when conditioned on student-generated prefixes, the teacher can favor invalid continuations that violate the question's premises, causing OPD to encourage erroneous reasoning steps. As a remedy, we introduce Condition-Contrastive Extrapolated On-Policy Distillation (CCE-OPD), which makes teacher supervision sensitive to question conditions. CCE-OPD incorporates (1) a fixed-prefix comparison in which the same frozen teacher evaluates the complete question and a counterpart with a selected condition masked, and (2) a bounded log-probability contrast that reweights the teacher distribution into an auxiliary distillation target. On 200 diagnostic reasoning states, condition contrast reduces the rate of preferring invalid continuations from 27.0% to 16.0%. Extensive experiments across six mathematical benchmarks show that CCE-OPD improves avg@8 over OPD by an average of 1.60 and 1.95 percentage points when distilling Qwen3-8B into 0.6B and 1.7B students, respectively. It also achieves the strongest results on four transfer benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[AgentEval: Evidence-Bounded Assessment of Stateful Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21177)**  `arXiv:2610.21177`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruiqi Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task completion alone does not determine whether a stateful agent satisfied its obligations, and a recorded judgment does not guarantee that its supporting evidence remains valid. We present AgentEval, a system that carries a contract-bound assessment record through tool execution, state and event capture, report qualification, and case maintenance. It separates what the agent did, what the evidence establishes, and whether the conclusion is supported for present use. On 32 tool-world administrations, independent checks agree on all 30 complete episodes, distinguishing satisfied, violated, and unresolved obligations; two interrupted administrations are retained. Controlled interventions on 48 evidence views expose 36 binding faults missed by hash-only checks without changing agent behavior. Scenario Studio connects reviewed cases to 12 two-turn reports with 24 independently checked answers. A separate public-benchmark study on 192 held-out executions reveals ten completed return subgoals within broader task failures and isolates the effect of missing evidence. These studies show how an executable assessment record makes scope, evidential support, and report reuse inspectable across an agent evaluation workflow.
  </details>

- **[MemScope: Evolving Agent Memory Architectures through Localized Code Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21170)**  `arXiv:2610.21170`  `cs.AI`  
  _Miao Su, Yucan Guo, Zixuan Li, Long Bai, Zhongni Hou, Xiaolong Jin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent memory architectures define how LLM agents store and reuse experience across tasks, but they are typically fixed after deployment, limiting their adaptability across task types. Recent self-evolving approaches instead adapt these memory architectures using execution feedback, typically represented as evaluation scores or compressed summaries. However, such coarse-grained feedback is insufficient for precise failure localization in current agent systems, where long-horizon task execution involves multiple interacting components. Without a precise failure location, existing approaches lack a clear code-level target for modification, leaving the update scope weakly constrained and often leading to large-scale rewrites of an otherwise capable memory architecture. We propose MemScope, a framework for evidence-grounded, localized evolution of an existing memory architecture. Its tool-using Patch Agent jointly inspects raw execution traces, runtime memory state, and current architecture code to identify repair targets and apply bounded, in-place edits under provider-identity and memory-compatibility constraints. On GAIA, the trace-and-memory inspection variant achieves 86.67% accuracy, compared with 70.00% for trace-only and 63.33% for summary-only feedback. Under xBench tasks, the evolved memory architecture improves over its original version from 49 to 55 correct answers with DeepSeek V3.2 and from 50 to 53 with Kimi K2.7 Code. On disjoint ALFWorld unseen tasks, success rises from 50.75% to 71.64%. Across archived architecture-snapshot transitions, the median code-change ratio is 4.4%. These results support fine-grained execution evidence as a basis for localized, in-place memory-architecture evolution.
  </details>

- **[GRADS: Graph-Regulated Agentic Data Synthesis with Controllable Difficulty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21167)**  `arXiv:2610.21167`  `cs.AI`  
  _Gaocheng Yang, Ruijie Tao, Kai Ye, Senhua Zhang, Zhengqin Zhu, Ziyang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research agents are increasingly deployed to retrieve precise information from large corpora, yet multi-hop, long-horizon tasks remain challenging.
